Namo tassa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassa.

Homage to the Blessed One, the Arahant, the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One.

Visuddhimagga nidānakathā

The Path of Purification: Introductory Account

Visuddhimaggo [M 0.0001] nāmāyaṃ gantho piṭakattayasārabhūto sakalaloke paṭipattidīpakaganthānaṃ aggo hoti seṭṭho pamukho pāmokkho uttamo pavaro cāti viññūhi pasattho. Tattha hi saṅgītittayārūḷhassa tepiṭakabuddhavacanassa atthaṃ saṃkhipitvā sikkhattayasaṅgahitaṃ brahmacariyaṃ paripuṇṇaṃ pakāsitaṃ suvisadañca. Evaṃ pasatthassetassa visuddhimaggassa nidānakathāyapi bhavitabbameva. Tasmādāni tampakāsanatthamidaṃ pañhakammaṃ vuccati –

This treatise called the Visuddhimagga is the essence of the three Pitakas; it is praised by the wise as the foremost, the best, the chief, the principal, the highest, and the most excellent among the works that illuminate the path of practice in the entire world. For here, the meaning of the Buddha’s words established in the threefold recitation of the Tipiṭaka is summarized, and the holy life comprised of the threefold training is fully and very clearly explained. Thus, there must also be an introductory account to this praised Visuddhimagga. Therefore, now for the sake of explaining that, this work of questioning is stated:

“So panesa visuddhimaggo kena kato, kadā kato, kattha kato, kasmā kato, kimatthaṃ kato, kiṃ nissāya kato, kena pakārena kato, kissa sakalaloke patthaṭo”ti.

“Now, this Path of Purification—by whom was it made, when was it made, where was it made, why was it made, for what purpose was it made, relying on what was it made, in what manner was it made, and why is it spread throughout the entire world?”

Tattha kena katoti ācariyabuddhaghosattheravarena tepiṭakasaṅgahaṭṭhakathākārena kato.

There, by whom was it made? It was made by the eminent Elder Ācariya Buddhaghosa, the author of the commentaries that are a compilation of the Tipiṭaka.

Kadā katoti amhākaṃ bhagavato sammāsambuddhassa sakalalokanāthassa parinibbutikālato pacchā dasame vassasatake (973 -buddhavasse) kato.

When was it made? It was made in the tenth century after the Parinibbāna of our Blessed One, the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One, the Lord of the entire world (in the year 973 of the Buddhist Era).

Kattha katoti sīhaḷadīpe anurādhapure mahāvihāre kato.

Where was it made? It was made in the Mahāvihāra at Anurādhapura, on the island of Sri Lanka.

Kasmā katoti visuddhikāmānaṃ sādhujanānaṃ tadadhigamupāyaṃ sammāpaṭipattinayaṃ ñāpetukāmatāsaṅkhātena attano ajjhāsayena sañcoditattā, saṅghapālattherena ca ajjhesitattā kato.

Why was it made? It was made because he was urged by his own intention—characterized by the desire to make known the right way of practice, which is the means to attain purity, to good people who desire purity—and also because he was requested by the Elder Saṅghapāla.

Ettha pana ṭhatvā ācariyabuddhaghosattherassa uppatti kathetabbā, sā ca mahāvaṃse (cūḷavaṃsotipi voharite dutiyabhāge) sattatiṃsamaparicchede pannarasādhikadvisatagāthāto (37, 215) paṭṭhāya bāttiṃsāya gāthāhi pakāsitāyeva. Kathaṃ? –

At this point, however, the life story of the Elder Ācariya Buddhaghosa should be told. That story is indeed revealed in the Mahāvaṃsa (also known as the Cūḷavaṃsa, in the second part), in the thirty-seventh chapter, from the 215th verse onwards, in thirty-two verses. How? –

Mahāvaṃsa-buddhaghosakathā

The Mahāvaṃsa: The Account of Buddhaghosa

215.

215.

Bodhimaṇḍasamīpamhi, [M 0.0002] jāto brāhmaṇamāṇavo;

Vijjā-sippa-kalā-vedī, tīsu vedesu pāragū.

Near the Bodhi-seat (Bodh Gaya), a brahmin youth was born; he was skilled in knowledge, crafts, and arts, and was a master of the three Vedas.

216.

216.

Sammā viññātasamayo, sabbavādavisārado;

Vādatthī jambudīpamhi, āhiṇḍanto pavādiko.

Well-versed in various doctrines and expert in all teachings, being eager for debate, he wandered throughout Jambudīpa as a disputant.

217.

217.

Vihārameka’māgamma, rattiṃ pātañjalīmataṃ;

Parivatteti sampuṇṇa-padaṃ suparimaṇḍalaṃ.

Having come to a certain monastery, at night he recited the doctrine of Patañjali, complete in its words and perfectly articulated.

218.

218.

Tattheko revato nāma, mahāthero vijāniya;

“Mahāpañño ayaṃ satto, dametuṃ vaṭṭatī”ti, so.

There, a great elder named Revata, perceiving him, thought: “This being has great wisdom; it is fitting to tame him.”

219.

219.

“Ko nu gadrabharāvena, viravanto”ti abravi;

“Gadrabhānaṃ rave atthaṃ, kiṃ jānāsī”ti āha taṃ.

“Who is it that is braying like a donkey?” the elder said. “Do you know the meaning of the donkey’s braying?” the youth replied to him.

220.

220.

“Ahaṃ jāne”ti vutto so, otāresi sakaṃ mataṃ;

Puṭṭhaṃ puṭṭhaṃ viyākāsi, viraddhampi ca dassayi.

When he was told “I know” [by the elder], the youth set forth his own doctrine. [The elder] explained every question asked and also pointed out the errors.

221.

221.

“Tena hi tvaṃ sakaṃ vāda-motārehī”ti codito;

Pāḷi’māhā’bhidhammassa, attha’massa na so’dhigā.

Urged with, “Then you set forth your own doctrine,” the elder recited the Pāli of the Abhidhamma, but the youth did not grasp its meaning.

222.

222.

Āha“kasse’sa manto”ti,“buddhamanto”ti so’bravi;

“Dehi metaṃ”ti vutte hi, “gaṇha pabbajja taṃ”iti.

The youth asked, “Whose mantra is this?” and the elder replied, “It is the Buddha’s mantra.” When he said, “Give it to me,” the elder said, “Take ordination and you shall receive it.”

223.

223.

Mantatthī pabbajitvā so, uggaṇhi piṭakattayaṃ;

Ekāyano ayaṃ maggo, iti pacchā ta’maggahi.

Desiring the mantra, he went forth as a monk and mastered the three Piṭakas. “This is the only path,” he later understood.

224.

224.

Buddhassa viya gambhīra-ghosattā naṃ viyākaruṃ;

Buddhaghosoti ghoso hi, buddho viya mahītale.

Because his voice was profound like the Buddha’s, they named him Buddhaghosa, for indeed his fame was like the Buddha’s on earth.

225.

225.

Tattha ñāṇodayaṃ ñāṇodayaṃ nāmapakaraṇaṃ idāni kuhiñcipi na dissati; nāma, katvā pakaraṇaṃ tadā;

Dhammasaṅgaṇiyākāsi, kacchaṃ so aṭṭhasāliniṃ idāni dissamānā pana aṭṭhasālinī sīhaḷadīpikāyeva; na jambudīpikā; parato (54-55 piṭṭhesu) esa āvibhavissati.

There, having at that time composed a treatise named Ñāṇodaya, he then composed the Aṭṭhasālinī, a commentary on the Dhammasaṅgaṇī.

226.

226.

Parittaṭṭhakathañceva [M 0.0003] parittaṭṭhakathanti piṭakattayassa saṅkhepato atthavaṇṇanābhūtā khuddakaṭṭhakathāti adhippetā bhavesu, kātuṃ ārabhi buddhimā;

Taṃ disvā revato thero, idaṃ vacanamabravi.

The wise one also began to compose a concise commentary. Seeing this, the elder Revata spoke these words:

227.

227.

“Pāḷimattaṃ idhānītaṃ, natthi aṭṭhakathā idha ettha sagībhittayārūḷhā moggaliputtatissattherassa santikā uggahitā sissānusissaparamparātatā mūlaṭṭhakathā kasmā jambudīpe sabbaso antarahitāti vimaṃsitabbaṃ;

Tathācariyavādā ca, bhinnarūpā na vijjare.

“Only the Pāli text has been brought here; there are no commentaries here. Nor are the various traditions of the teachers to be found.

228.

228.

Sīhaḷaṭṭhakathā suddhā, mahindena matīmatā;

Saṅgītittayamārūḷhaṃ, sammāsambuddhadesitaṃ.

The pure Sinhala commentaries, composed by the wise Mahinda, are based on the teachings of the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One established in the three Councils.

229.

229.

Sāriputtādigītañca, kathāmaggaṃ samekkhiya;

Katā sīhaḷabhāsāya, sīhaḷesu pavattati.

Having examined the path of discourse taught by Sāriputta and others, they were composed in the Sinhala language and circulate among the Sinhalese.

230.

230.

Taṃ tattha gantvā sutvā tvaṃ, māgadhānaṃ niruttiyā;

Parivattehi, sā hoti, sabbalokahitāvahā”.

“Go there, and having heard them, translate them into the language of the Magadhans (Pali). That will be for the welfare of the whole world.”

231.

231.

Evaṃ vutte pasanno so, nikkhamitvā tato imaṃ;

Dīpaṃ āgā imasseva idassevāti imasseva mahānāmarañño kāle 953-975 buddhavasse; ayañca vassaparicchedo sīhaḷarājavaṃsaṃ nissāya dassito; yuropiyavicakkhaṇānaṃ pana matena 941-964 buddhavasse iti veditabbo; evamuparipi;, rañño kāle mahāmati.

When this was said, the wise one, being pleased, left that place and came to this island (Sri Lanka) during the time of this very king (Mahānāma).

232.

232.

Mahāvihāraṃ sampatto, vihāraṃ sabbasādhunaṃ;

Mahāpadhānagharaṃ gantvā, saṅghapālassa santikā.

Having reached the Mahāvihāra, the monastery of all good people, he went to the Great House of Striving, to the presence of the Elder Saṅghapāla.

233.

233.

Sīhaḷaṭṭhakathaṃ sutvā, theravādañca sabbaso;

“Dhammassāmissa esova, adhippāyo”ti nicchiya.

After hearing the entire Sinhala commentaries and the Theravāda tradition, he ascertained: “This indeed is the intention of the Lord of the Dhamma.”

234.

234.

Tattha saṅghaṃ samānetvā, “kātuṃ aṭṭhakathaṃ mama;

Potthake detha sabbe”ti, āha, vīmaṃsituṃ sa taṃ.

Gathering the Saṅgha there, he said, “Give me all the manuscripts that I may compose the commentaries.” To test him, the Saṅgha [replied]:

235.

235.

Saṅgho [M 0.0004] gāthādvayaṃ tassā’dāsi “sāmatthiyaṃ tava;

Ettha dassehi, taṃ disvā, sabbe demāti potthake” 234-5 gāthāsu ayamatthayojanā– “tattha mahāvihāre saṃghaṃ mahānetvā saṃgha sannipātaṃ kāretvā ācariyapubbaddhaghoso evamāha ‘aṭṭhakathaṃ kātuṃ sabbe pāḷi-aṭṭhakathā-potthake mama dethā’ti; so saṃgho taṃ vīmaṃsituṃ saṃyuttanikāyato ‘antojaṭā’tiādikaṃ ca ‘sīle patiṭṭhāyā’tiādikaṃ cāti gāthādvayaṃ tassa adāsi ‘ettha tava sāmatthiyaṃ ñāṇappabhāvaṃ dasseti; taṃ disvā sabbe potthake demā’tivatvā”ti; iminā pana ayamatthā dassito hoti “ācariya buddhaghoso visuddhimaggaṃ karonto tadeva gāthādvayaṃ oloketvā, kiñcipi aññaṃ potthakaṃ anoloketvā akāsī”ti; tassa panatthassa yuttāyuttavicāraṇā parato (39-49-piṭṭhesu) āgamissati.

The Saṅgha gave him two verses, saying: “Show your skill here; having seen that, we will give you all the manuscripts.”

236.

236.

Piṭakattaya’mettheva, saddhiṃ aṭṭhakathāya so;

Visuddhimaggaṃ nāmā’kā, saṅgahetvā samāsato.

Right here, having concisely summarized the three Piṭakas along with the commentaries, he composed the work named Visuddhimagga.

237.

237.

Tato saṅghaṃ samūhetvā, sambuddhamatakovidaṃ;

Mahābodhisamīpamhi, so taṃ vācetu mārabhi.

Then, having assembled the Saṅgha, who were skilled in the Buddha’s teachings, he began to have it read near the Great Bodhi Tree.

238.

238.

Devatā tassa nepuññaṃ, pakāsetuṃ mahājane;

Chādesuṃ potthakaṃ sopi, dvattikkhattumpi taṃ akā 238 gāthāya ayamattho– “devatā tassa buddhaghosassa nepuññaṃ nipuṇaññāppasāvaṃ mahājanassa pakāsetuṃ tena likhitaṃ visuddhimaggapotthakaṃ chādesuṃ paṭicchādetvā apassiyabhāvaṃ pāpetvā ṭhapesuṃ; sopi buddhaghoso dutiyampi taṃ likhi, tampi devatā chadesuṃ; tatiyampi likhī”ti; tena vuttaṃ “dvattikkhattumpi taṃ akā”ti; idameva mahāvaṃsavacanaṃ nissāya vittāretvā kathitāya buddhaghosuppattiyā nāma kathāla ekaratteneva visuddhimaggassa tikkhattumpi likhitvā niṭṭhāpitabhāvo pakāsito; īdisī pana kathā bahūnaṃ vimhayajananīpi parikkhakānaṃ saṃsayajananī hoti; tasmā imissāpi vicāraṇā parato (47-8-piṭṭhesu) dassiyissati.

The deities, in order to reveal his proficiency to the great multitude, hid the book; and he, too, composed it a second and a third time.

239.

239.

Vācetuṃ tatiye vāre, potthake samudāhaṭe;

Potthakadvaya’maññampi, saṇṭhapesuṃ tahiṃ marū.

When the book was brought forth to be read for the third time, the deities placed the other two books there as well.

240.

240.

Vācayiṃsu tadā bhikkhū, potthakattaya’mekato;

Ganthato atthato vāpi, pubbāparavasena vā.

Then the monks recited the three books together, whether in terms of the text, the meaning, or the sequence.

241.

241.

Theravādehi pāḷīhi, padehi byañjanehi vā;

Aññathattamahū neva, potthakesupi tīsupi.

There was no difference at all even among the three books, whether with the Theravāda traditions, the Pāḷi texts, the words, or the syllables.

242.

242.

Atha [M 0.0005] ugghosayī saṅgho, tuṭṭhahaṭṭho visesato;

Nissaṃsayaṃ’sa metteyyo, iti vatvā punappunaṃ.

Then the Saṅgha, especially delighted and joyful, proclaimed, saying again and again: “Without a doubt, this is Metteyya.”

243.

243.

Saddhiṃ aṭṭhakathāyā’dā, potthake piṭakattaye;

Ganthākare vasanto so, vihāre dūrasaṅkare.

They gave him the books of the three Piṭakas along with the commentaries; he stayed in the scriptorium of a monastery that was free from overcrowding.

244.

244.

Parivattesi sabbāpi, sīhaḷaṭṭhakathā tadā;

Sabbesaṃ mūlabhāsāya, māgadhāya niruttiyā.

He then translated all the Sīhaḷa commentaries into the Magadhan language, the root language of all.

245.

245.

Sattānaṃ sabbabhāsānaṃ, sā ahosi hitāvahā;

Theriyācariyā sabbe, pāḷiṃ viya ta’maggahuṃ.

That [translation] was beneficial for beings of all languages; all the teachers of the Theravāda tradition accepted it just like the Pāḷi Canon.

246.

246.

Atha kattabbakiccesu, gatesu pariniṭṭhitiṃ;

Vandituṃ so mahābodhiṃ, jambudīpaṃ upāgamī”ti so mahābodhi vandituṃ jambudīpaṃ upāgamīti idaṃ vacanaṃ purimavacanehi asaṃsaṭṭhaṃ viya hoti; pubbe hi “ācariyabuddhaghoso bodhimaṇḍasamīpe jāto”ti ca, “sīhaḷadīpaṃ gantvā sīhaḷaṭṭhakathāyo māgadhabhāsāya parivattehīti tassācariyena revatattherena vutto”ti ca vuttaṃ; tasmā idhāpi ācariyabuddhaghosassa pavatti tadanurūpā “tā bhāsāparivattitaṭṭhakathāyo ādāya sāsanujjotanatthaṃ jambudīpaṃ upāgamī”ti evamādinā sāsanujjotanamūlikā eva bhavituṃ arahati, na pana mahābodhivandanamūlikāti.

Then, when his necessary duties had reached completion, he went to Jambudīpa to venerate the Great Bodhi Tree.”

Ayañca pana mahāvaṃsakathā 1950 - kharistavasse hābadamahāvijjālayamuddaṇayante romakkharena mudditassa visuddhimaggapotthakassa purecārikakathāyaṃ “anekānettha atthi vicāretabbānī”ti vatvā dhammānandakosambīnāmakena vicakkhaṇena vicāritā. Tamettha yuttāyuttavicinanāya dassetvā anuvicāraṇampissa karissāma.

Now, this account from the Mahāvaṃsa was examined by the wise Dhammānanda Kosambī in the foreword to the edition of the Visuddhimagga printed in Roman script by Harvard University Press in the year 1950, stating: “There are many points here that should be investigated.” Having presented it here for the purpose of discerning what is appropriate or inappropriate, we shall also provide a further analysis of it.

Jātidesavicāraṇā

Investigation into the Place of Birth

1. Tattha hi tena dhammānandena “buddhaghoso bodhimaṇḍasamīpe (buddhagayāyaṃ) jātoti na yuttameta”nti vatvā taṃsādhanatthāya cattāri byatirekakāraṇāni dassitāni. Kathaṃ?

1. Therein, the said Dhammānanda, stating, “The claim that Buddhaghosa was born near the Bodhimaṇḍa (Buddhagayā) is not fitting,” presented four contrary reasons to establish that point. In what way?

(Ka) “buddhaghosena pakāsitesu taṃkālikavatthūsu ekampi taṃ natthi, yaṃ magadhesu uppanna”nti paṭhamaṃ kāraṇaṃ dassitaṃ. Tadakāraṇameva. Ācariyabuddhaghosatthero hi saṅgahaṭṭhakathāyo karonto porāṇaṭṭhakathāyoyeva [M 0.0006] saṃkhipitvā, bhāsāparivattanamattena ca visesetvā akāsi, na pana yaṃ vā taṃ vā attano diṭṭhasutaṃ dassetvā. Vuttañhetaṃ ācariyena –

(A) The first reason presented was: “Among the contemporary stories mentioned by Buddhaghosa, there is not even one that occurred in Magadha.” But that is no reason at all. For the Teacher, the Elder Buddhaghosa, while composing the compendium commentaries, did so by merely condensing the ancient commentaries and by specifically changing the language, and not by presenting this or that which he had himself seen or heard. Indeed, this was stated by the Teacher:

“Saṃvaṇṇanaṃ tañca samārabhanto,

Tassā mahāaṭṭhakathaṃ sarīraṃ;

Katvā mahāpaccariyaṃ tatheva,

Kurundināmādisu vissutāsu.

“Beginning that exposition, and making the Mahā-aṭṭhakathā its body, and likewise the Mahāpaccariya and other well-known [commentaries] such as the Kurundī,

Vinicchayo aṭṭhakathāsu vutto,

Yo yuttamatthaṃ apariccajanto;

Athopi antogadhatheravādaṃ,

Saṃvaṇṇanaṃ samma samārabhissa”nti pārā. aṭṭha. 1.ganthārambhakathā ca.

not abandoning whatever appropriate meaning is found in the decisions stated in those commentaries, and also [including] the Theravāda tradition contained therein, I shall properly begin the exposition.”

“Tato ca bhāsantarameva hitvā,

Vitthāramaggañca samāsayitvā;

Vinicchayaṃ sabbamasesayitvā,

Tantikkamaṃ kiñci avokkamitvā.

“Then, discarding only the other language and condensing the extensive method, while leaving no decision incomplete and without deviating from any canonical order,

Suttantikānaṃ vacanānamatthaṃ,

Suttānurūpaṃ paridīpayantī;

Yasmā ayaṃ hessati vaṇṇanāpi,

Sakkacca tasmā anusikkhitabbā”ti pārā. aṭṭha. 1.ganthārambhakathā ca.

clarifying the meaning of the words of the Suttas in a manner that conforms to the Suttas—since this commentary will be such, it should therefore be carefully studied.”

Yatheva ca ācariyabuddhaghosena attano aṭṭhakathāsu taṃkālikāni māgadhikāni vatthūni na pakāsitāni, tatheva sīhaḷikānipi dakkhiṇaindiyaraṭṭhikānipi. Na hi tattha vasabharājakālato (609-653 -buddhavassa) pacchā uppannavatthūni diṭṭhāni ṭhapetvā mahāsenarājavatthuṃ pārā. aṭṭha. 2.236-237, ācariyo ca tato tisatamattavassehi pacchātare mahānāmarañño kāle (953-975-bu-va) sīhaḷadīpamupāgato. Tasmā aṭṭhakathāsu taṃkālikamāgadhikavatthūnaṃ appakāsanamattena na sakkā takkattā na māgadhikoti ñātunti.

Just as the Teacher Buddhaghosa did not present contemporary Magadhan stories in his commentaries, he likewise did not present contemporary Sinhalese or South Indian stories. For therein, no stories are found occurring after the time of King Vasabha (609–653 B.E.), except for the story of King Mahāsena. And the Teacher arrived on the island of Sīhaḷa about three hundred years later, during the time of King Mahānāma (953–975 B.E.). Therefore, merely by the fact that contemporary Magadhan stories are not presented in the commentaries, it is not possible to conclude that the author was not a Magadhan.

[Kha) punapi [M 0.0007] tena “sabbesupi buddhaghosaganthesu uttaraindiyadesāyattaṃ paccakkhato diṭṭhassa viya pakāsanaṃ natthī”ti dutiyaṃ kāraṇaṃ dassitaṃ. Tassapi akāraṇabhāvo purimavacaneneva veditabbo. Apica sāratthappakāsiniyā nāma saṃyuttaṭṭhakathāyaṃ, sumaṅgalavilāsiniyā nāma dīghanikāyaṭṭhakathāyañca vuttasaṃvaṇṇanāyapi veditabbo. Tattha hi –

(B) Furthermore, he presented a second reason: “In all of Buddhaghosa’s books, there is no description that is like an eye-witness account of the Northern Indian region.” The groundlessness of this, too, should be understood from the previous statement. Moreover, it should also be understood from the description given in the Sāratthappakāsinī, the commentary on the Saṃyutta Nikāya, and in the Sumaṅgalavilāsinī, the commentary on the Dīgha Nikāya. For therein it says:

“Yatheva hi kalambanadītīrato rājamātuvihāradvārena thūpārāmaṃ gantabbaṃ hoti, evaṃ hiraññavatikāya nāma nadiyā pārimatīrato sālavanaṃ uyyānaṃ. Yathā anurādhapurassa thūpārāmo, evaṃ taṃ kusinārāya hoti. Thūpārāmato dakkhiṇadvārena nagaraṃ pavisanamaggo pācīnamukho gantvā uttarena nivattati, evaṃ uyyānato sālapanti pācīnamukhā gantvā uttarena nivattā. Tasmā taṃ upavattananti vuccatī”ti saṃ. ni. aṭṭha. 1.1.186; dī. ni. aṭṭha. 2.198 –

“Just as one must go from the bank of the Kalamba River to the Thūpārāma through the gate of the Rājamātuvihāra, so it is from the opposite bank of the river named Hiraññavatī to the Sāla Grove. Just as the Thūpārāma is to Anurādhapura, so is that [Sāla Grove] to Kusinārā. Just as the path entering the city from the Thūpārāma through the southern gate goes eastward and then turns north, so too from the grove, the row of sāla trees goes eastward and then turns north. Therefore, it is called Upavattana.”

Paccakkhato diṭṭhassa viya pakāsanampi dissateva. Tampi pana porāṇaṭṭhakathāhi bhāsāparivattanamattamevāti gahetabbaṃ, tādisāya atthasaṃvaṇṇanāya mahāmahindattherakālatoyeva pabhuti vuttāya eva bhavitabbattāti.

Descriptions as if from an eye-witness account are indeed found. However, even such descriptions should be understood as a mere translation from the ancient commentaries, since such an explanation of the meaning must have been given ever since the time of the Great Elder Mahinda.

[Ga) punapi tena “uṇhassāti aggisantāpassa, tassa vanadāhādīsu sambhavo veditabbo”ti visuddhimagge (1, 30-piṭṭhe) vuttasaṃvaṇṇanaṃ pakāsetvā “tassā panassa avahasanīyabhāvo pākaṭoyevā”ti ca hīḷetvā “indiyaraṭṭhe pana uttaradesesu gimhakāle vatthacchādanarahitā mānusakāyacchavi sūriyasantāpena ekaṃsato dayhati, taṃ na jānanti dakkhiṇaindiyadesikā”ti tatiyaṃ kāraṇaṃ daḷhatarabhāvena dassitaṃ. Tattha pana yadi “sūriyasantāpena ekaṃsato dayhatī”ti etaṃ ujukato sūriyarasmisantāpeneva daḍḍhabhāvaṃ sandhāya vucceyya, evaṃ sati ḍaṃsamakasavātātapasarīsapasamphassānanti pade ātapasaddena samānatthattā na yuttameva. Yadi pana sūriyasantāpasañjātena uṇhautunā daḍḍhabhāvaṃ sandhāya vucceyya, evaṃ sati uttaraindiyadesesu, aññattha ca tādisesu atiuṇhaṭṭhānesu sūriyasantāpasañjātassa uṇhautuno paṭighātāya [M 0.0008] cīvaraṃ senāsanañca paṭisevīyatīti ayamattho na na yutto. Tathā hi vuttaṃ vinayaṭṭhakathāyaṃ (3, 58)

(C) Furthermore, after presenting the commentary stated in the Visuddhimagga (p. 30), "'Uṇhassa' (for heat) means the heat of fire, and its occurrence should be understood in such instances as forest fires," and disparaging it by saying, "But the laughable nature of that [explanation] is quite evident," he (Dhammānanda) presented the third reason as more compelling, stating: "In the northern regions of India, during the summer, a human body without the covering of clothes is certainly scorched by the heat of the sun; this is something those from southern India do not know." Now, in that reasoning, if "scorched certainly by the heat of the sun" were stated with direct reference to being burned by the heat of the sun's rays themselves, then it would be inappropriate, because it would have the same meaning as the word 'ātapa' (sun's heat) in the phrase 'for the purpose of warding off the touch of gadflies, mosquitoes, wind, sun's heat, and creeping things.' But if it were stated with reference to being scorched by the hot climate produced by the sun's heat, then this meaning is not inappropriate; for in the northern Indian regions and in other similarly excessively hot places, robes and lodgings are used to counteract the hot climate produced by the sun's heat. Indeed, it is because it is appropriate that it is stated in the Vinaya Commentary (3, 58):

“Sītaṃ uṇhanti utuvisabhāgavasena vutta”nti.

"'[The lodging wards off] cold and heat' is spoken of by way of a disagreeable climate."

Sā pana visuddhimagge padatthasaṃvaṇṇanā porāṇasuttantaṭṭhakathāhi āgatā bhaveyya. Tathā hi vuttaṃ papañcasūdaniyā nāma majjhimanikāyaṭṭhakathāya sabbāsavasuttavaṇṇanāyaṃ (1, 58) “uṇhanti cettha aggisantāpova veditabbo, sūriyasantāpavasena panetaṃ vatthu vutta”nti. Ettha ca sacāyamattho ācariyena attano mativasena vutto assa, tassa vatthussa porāṇaṭṭhakathāyaṃ vuttabhāvañca tassā atthasaṃvaṇṇanāya attano matibhāvañca yuttabhāvañca pakāseyya. Ācariyo hi yattha yattha porāṇaṭṭhakathāsu avuttatthaṃ visesetvā dasseti, tattha tattha tādisaṃ ñāpakavacanampi pakāsetiyeva, yathā sumaṅgalavilāsiniyaṃ (1, 72) “ettha āṇattiyanissaggiyathāvarāpi payogā yujjanti, aṭṭhakathāsu pana anāgatattā vīmaṃsitvā gahetabbā”ti vacanaṃ, yathā ca papañcasūdaniyaṃ (1, 30) “avicāritametaṃ porāṇehi, ayaṃ pana attano matī”ti vacanaṃ. Na cettha kiñcipi ñāpakavacanaṃ pakāsitaṃ. Tasmā “yadetaṃ ‘uṇhassāti aggisantāpassā’ti ca, ‘uṇhanti cettha aggisantāpova veditabbo’ti ca vacanaṃ, etaṃ porāṇasuttantaṭṭhakathāvacana”nti veditabbanti.

However, that explanation of the term in the Visuddhimagga likely came from the ancient Suttanta commentaries. To show how this is so: it is stated in the Majjhima Nikāya commentary called Papañcasūdanī, in the explanation of the Sabbāsava Sutta (1, 58): 'In this context, "uṇha" (heat) should be understood only as the heat of fire; but this story is stated by way of the sun's heat.' If this explanation had been stated by the Teacher (Buddhaghosa) as his own opinion, he would have declared that the mentioned story about the sun's heat was stated in the ancient commentary, that the explanation of the heat of fire was his own opinion, and why his explanation was logical. For indeed, wherever the Teacher provides a specific meaning not mentioned in the ancient commentaries, he always provides such an indicative statement to make it known. For instance, as in the Sumaṅgalavilāsinī (1, 72): 'In this matter of false speech, the efforts of commanding, throwing away, and permanent [marks] are also suitable; however, since they are not found in the ancient commentaries, they should be accepted after investigation.' And as in the Papañcasūdanī (1, 30): 'This was not considered by the ancients; this, however, is my own opinion.' But here, no such indicative statement is provided. Therefore, the two statements—'uṇhassa means the heat of fire' and 'here, uṇha should be understood only as the heat of fire'—should be understood as the words of the ancient Suttanta commentaries. Thus ends one clarification.

(Gha) punapi tena “papañcasūdaniyā nāma majjhimanikāyaṭṭhakathāyaṃ gopālakasuttaṃ saṃvaṇṇento ma. ni. aṭṭha. 1.350 buddhaghoso ‘magadhavideharaṭṭhānaṃ antare gaṅgāya nadiyā majjhe vālukatthaladīpakā atthī’ti saddahati maññe. Buddhaghosena pana diṭṭhagaṅgā sīhaḷadīpe mahāveligaṅgāyeva, na pana indiyaraṭṭhikānaṃ seṭṭhasammatā mahāgaṅgāti pākaṭoyevāyamattho”ti catutthaṃ kāraṇaṃ dassitaṃ. Taṃ pana idāni mahāgaṅgāya majjhe tasmiṃ ṭhāne tādisaṃ dīpakaṃ adisvā “pubbepi evameva bhaveyyā”ti ekaṃsato gahetvā vuttavacanamattameva. Nadiyo pana sabbadāpi tenevākārena tiṭṭhantīti na sakkā gahetunti pākaṭoyevāyamattho. Tasmā yathā pubbe tassa gopālassa kāle tasmiṃ ṭhāne majjhe gaṅgāya tādisā dīpakā saṃvijjamānā ahesuṃ, tatheva porāṇaṭṭhakathāsu esa attho saṃvaṇṇito, [M 0.0009] tadeva ca vacanaṃ ācariyena bhāsāparivattanaṃ katvā pakāsitanti evameva gahetabbaṃ. Tasmā tampi akāraṇamevāti.

(D) Furthermore, he presented the fourth reason: 'While explaining the Gopālaka Sutta in the Papañcasūdanī, the commentary on the Majjhima Nikāya, Buddhaghosa, it seems, believes that in the middle of the Ganges River between the countries of Magadha and Videha, there are sandbanks and small islands. However, the Ganges seen by Buddhaghosa was only the Mahāveli Ganga in Sri Lanka, not the great Ganges considered supreme by the people of India; this fact is quite evident.' But that is merely a statement made as a definitive accusation based on not seeing such a small island in that place in the middle of the great Ganges at present, assuming 'it must have been so even in the past, just as it is seen now.' It is indeed obvious that one cannot assume rivers always remain in the same state. Therefore, in ancient times during the era of the cowherd mentioned in that sutta, there must have been such small islands in the middle of the Ganges where cows could rest. It should be understood that the ancient commentaries explained the meaning according to how it had been, and the Teacher (Buddhaghosa) simply rendered that explanation from the ancient commentaries into the Pali language. Therefore, that fourth reason is also not a valid reason. Thus ends one clarification.

Brāhmaṇakulavicāraṇā

Investigation into the Brahmin Lineage

2. Atha “brāhmaṇamāṇavo”ti padampi tena evaṃ vicāritaṃ –

2. (2) Next, the term 'brahmin youth' (brāhmaṇamāṇava) was also investigated by that Dhammānanda as follows:

(Ka) “buddhaghoso ‘brāhmaṇakulajāto’ti na sakkā gahetuṃ. Kasmā vedakālato paṭṭhāya yāvajjatanā sabbepi brāhmaṇā

(A) "Buddhaghosa should not and cannot be accepted as being of the brahmin lineage. Why? Because from the Vedic period when the Vedas flourished until the present day, it can be believed that all brahmins..."

Brāhmaṇosya mukhamāsīdi, bāhū rājanya? Kata?;

Ūrū tadasya yada vagya?, Padbhyāṃ gūdro ajāyatā”ti iruveda, 10-maṇḍala, 90; tathā athava 6 veda 19, 6, 6.

The Brahmin was his mouth, his arms were made the Rājanya; his thighs were the Vaiśya, from his feet the Śūdra was born.

Imaṃ purisasuttaṃ nāma mantaṃ jānantīti saddahiyā.

"...know this mantra verse called the Puruṣa Sutta."

Ayaṃ panassā attho – ‘brāhmaṇo assa (brahmuno) mukhaṃ āsi. Bāhū rājañño kato, khattiyā assa bāhūti vuttaṃ hoti. Yo vesso, so assa ūrū. Suddo assa pādehi ajāyī’ti.

The meaning of that verse is: 'The brahmin was the mouth of that (Brahma). The ruling class (Rājañña) was made his arms; it means the Khattiyas were his arms. The one who is the merchant class (Vessa), he was his thighs. The laboring class (Sudda) was born from his feet.'

Buddhaghoso pana ‘paṇḍitabrāhmaṇo’ti ñātopi taṃ gāthaṃ na aññāsi. Tathā hi tena bandhupādāpaccāti padassa atthavaṇṇanāyaṃ ‘tesaṃ kira ayaṃ laddhi – brāhmaṇā brahmuno mukhato nikkhantā, khattiyā urato, vessā nābhito, suddā jāṇuto, samaṇā piṭṭhipādato’ti dī. ni. aṭṭha. 1.263; ma. ni. aṭṭha. 1.508 tissā vedagāthāya asamānattho vaṇṇito”ti.

But Buddhaghosa, although recognized as a 'learned brahmin,' did not know that verse. To show that he did not know it: in his explanation of the word 'bandhupādāpaccā,' he explained a meaning that is different from and inconsistent with that Vedic verse, saying: 'This, it seems, is their view—brahmins emerged from the Brahma's mouth, Khattiyas from his chest, Vessas from his navel, Suddas from his knees, and ascetics (samaṇas) from the tops of his feet.' Thus he (Dhammānanda) investigated, concluding that [Buddhaghosa] could not be of the brahmin lineage.

Ayaṃ panettha anuvicāraṇā – yadi ca taṃkālikānampi brāhmaṇānaṃ laddhi tatheva bhaveyya yathā etissaṃ gāthāyaṃ vuttā, sā catthavaṇṇanā ācariyassa matimattā. Evaṃ sati sā vicāraṇā yuttā bhaveyya. Etissaṃ pana gāthāyaṃ “brāhmaṇosya mukhamāsīdi”ti paṭhamapādena “brāhmaṇā brahmuno mukhato jātā”ti attho ujukato na labbhati. Buddhakāle pana brāhmaṇānaṃ laddhi “brāhmaṇā brahmuno mukhato jātā”ti evameva ahosīti pākaṭoyevāyamattho. Tathā hi dīghanikāye pāthikavagge aggaññasutte (3, 67) –

Herein, this is our further investigation—if the view of the brahmins of that time were indeed as stated in that verse, and if that explanation were merely Ven. Buddhaghosa's own opinion, then that investigation would be appropriate. But in that verse, the first line 'The brahmin was his mouth' (brāhmaṇosya mukhamāsīt) does not directly yield the meaning 'Brahmins were born from Brahmā's mouth.' However, it is quite evident that during the Buddha's time, the view among brahmins was indeed that 'Brahmins were born from Brahmā's mouth.' This is clear from the Aggañña Sutta in the Pāthikavagga of the Dīghanikāya:

“Dissanti kho pana vāseṭṭha brāhmaṇānaṃ brāhmaṇiyo utuniyopi gabbhiniyopi vijāyamānāpi pāyamānāpi. Te ca brāhmaṇā [M 0.0001]0 yonijāva samānā evamāhaṃsu – brāhmaṇova seṭṭho vaṇṇo, hīnā aññe vaṇṇā. Brāhmaṇova sukko vaṇṇo, kaṇhā aññe vaṇṇā. Brāhmaṇāva sujjhanti, no abrāhmaṇā. Brāhmaṇāva brahmuno puttā orasā mukhato jātā brahmajā brahmanimmitā brahmadāyādāti. Te brahmānañceva abbhācikkhanti, musā ca bhāsanti, bahuñca apuññaṃ pasavantī”ti –

"Vāseṭṭha, brahmin women are seen to have their periods, to become pregnant, to give birth, and to nurse their children. Yet those brahmins, though they are themselves born from a womb, say: 'The brahmin caste is the best caste; other castes are inferior. The brahmin caste is the fair caste; other castes are dark. Only brahmins are purified, not non-brahmins. Brahmins alone are the sons of Brahmā, his legitimate offspring, born from his mouth, born of Brahmā, created by Brahmā, heirs of Brahmā.' In this, they misrepresent Brahmā, speak falsely, and generate much demerit."

Bhagavatā mahākāruṇikena vāseṭṭhabhāradvājānaṃ brāhmaṇamāṇavakānaṃ bhāsitaṃ, tehi ca taṃ abhinanditaṃ. Te pana dvepi māṇavakā jātivasena parisuddhabrāhmaṇā ceva honti tiṇṇampi vedānaṃ pāraguno ca. Tasmā “brāhmaṇā brahmuno mukhato nikkhantā”ti vacanassa taṃkālikānaṃ brāhmaṇānaṃ laddhivasena vuttabhāvo pākaṭoyeva. Yathā cetaṃ, evaṃ “khattiyā urato, vessā nābhito, suddā jāṇuto, samaṇā piṭṭhipādato”ti vacanampi “taṃkālikabrāhmaṇānaṃ laddhiññūhi porāṇaṭṭhakathācariyehi vutta”nti saddahitvā ācariyabuddhaghosena taṃ sabbaṃ porāṇaṭṭhakathāto bhāsāparivattanamattena visesetvā pakāsitaṃ bhaveyya. Tasmā tāyapi vedagāthāya ācariyassa abrāhmaṇabhāvasādhanaṃ anupapannamevāti.

This was spoken by the Blessed One, the Greatly Compassionate One, to the brahmin youths Vāseṭṭha and Bhāradvāja, and they accepted that statement. Those two youths were both pure brahmins by birth and masters of the three Vedas. Therefore, it is clear that the statement 'Brahmins issued from Brahmā’s mouth' was spoken in accordance with the doctrine of the brahmins of the Buddha's time. Similarly, the statements 'Khattiyas from the chest, Vessas from the navel, Suddas from the knees, and ascetics from the instep' should be seen as having been presented by Ven. Buddhaghosa, who believed them to be the words of the ancient commentary teachers who knew the doctrine of the brahmins of that time, and he merely translated all those explanations from the old commentaries. Therefore, using that Vedic verse to prove Ven. Buddhaghosa's non-brahmin status is both inappropriate and insufficient. This concludes one explanation.

(Kha) punapi tena ācariyabuddhaghosattherassa abrāhmaṇabhāvasādhanatthaṃ dutiyampi kāraṇaṃ evamāhaṭaṃ –

(b) Furthermore, Dhammānanda also brought forward a second reason to prove Ven. Buddhaghosa Thera's non-brahmin status, as follows:

“Brāhmaṇaganthesu gabbhaghātavācakaṃ bhrūnahāti padaṃ pāḷiyaṃ bhūnahu (bhūnahano) iti dissati. Māgaṇḍiyasutte bhariyāya methunasaṃvāsābhāvena uppajjanārahagabbhassa nāsakattaṃ sandhāya māgaṇḍiyo paribbājako bhagavantaṃ ‘bhūnahu (bhūnahano) samaṇo gotamo’ti ma. ni. 2.207 ādayo āha. Taṃ buddhaghoso na jānātīti pākaṭoyeva tadatthasaṃvaṇṇanāya. Tattha hi tena bhūnahunoti (bhūnahanassā) padaṃ ‘hatavaḍḍhino mariyādakārakassā’ti ma. ni. aṭṭha. 2.207 vaṇṇita”nti.

"In Brahmanical texts, the word 'bhrūṇahā', signifying an embryo-killer, is seen in Pāli as 'bhūnahu' (or 'bhūnahano'). In the Māgaṇḍiya Sutta, the wandering ascetic Māgaṇḍiya, referring to the destruction of the potential for conception due to the absence of sexual intercourse, called the Blessed One 'bhūnahu' (the destroyer of growth). It is evident from his explanation of that word that Buddhaghosa did not know this meaning. For in that Māgaṇḍiya Sutta, he explained the word 'bhūnahu' (or 'bhūnahanassa') as 'one who destroys growth, one who sets a limit.'"

Tampi ayuttameva. Na hi māgaṇḍiyena phoṭṭhabbārammaṇāparibhogamattameva sandhāya bhūnahubhāvo vutto, atha kho channampi lokāmisārammaṇānaṃ aparibhogaṃ sandhāya vutto. Tasmiñhi sutte –

That statement is also incorrect. For Māgaṇḍiya did not speak of being a 'bhūnahu' merely in reference to the non-enjoyment of tactile objects. In fact, he spoke in reference to the non-enjoyment of all six worldly sensual objects. As follows in that Sutta:

“Cakkhuṃ [M 0.0011] kho māgaṇḍiya rūpārāmaṃ rūparataṃ rūpasammuditaṃ, taṃ tathāgatassa dantaṃ guttaṃ rakkhitaṃ saṃvutaṃ, tassa ca saṃvarāya dhammaṃ deseti, idaṃ nu te etaṃ māgaṇḍiya sandhāya bhāsitaṃ ‘bhūnahu samaṇo gotamo’ti. Etadeva kho pana me bho gotama sandhāya bhāsitaṃ ‘bhūnahu samaṇo gotamo’ti. Taṃ kissa hetu, evañhi no sutte ocaratīti…pe… mano kho māgaṇḍiya dhammārāmo dhammarato dhammasammudito, so tathāgatassa danto gutto rakkhito saṃvuto, tassa ca saṃvarāya dhammaṃ deseti, idaṃ nu te etaṃ māgaṇḍiya sandhāya bhāsitaṃ ‘bhūnahu samaṇo gotamo’ti. Etadeva kho pana me bho gotama sandhāya bhāsitaṃ ‘bhūnahu samaṇo gotamo’ti. Taṃ kissa hetu, evañhi no sutte ocaratī”ti ma. ni. 2.207 ādayo.

"Māgaṇḍiya, the eye delights in forms, is fond of forms, rejoices in forms. That, in the Tathāgata, is tamed, guarded, protected, and restrained. And he teaches the Dhamma for its restraint. Was it in reference to this, Māgaṇḍiya, that you said: 'The ascetic Gotama is a destroyer of growth (bhūnahu)'? 'Indeed, Master Gotama, it was in reference to this that I said, "The ascetic Gotama is a destroyer of growth." For what reason? Because this is how it is found in our scriptures.' ... 'Māgaṇḍiya, the mind delights in mental objects, is fond of mental objects, rejoices in mental objects. That, in the Tathāgata, is tamed, guarded, protected, and restrained. And he teaches the Dhamma for its restraint. Was it in reference to this, Māgaṇḍiya, that you said: "The ascetic Gotama is a destroyer of growth"?' 'Indeed, Master Gotama, it was in reference to this that I said, "The ascetic Gotama is a destroyer of growth." For what reason? Because this is how it is found in our scriptures.'"

Evaṃ bhagavato ca anuyogo māgaṇḍiyassa ca paṭiññā āgatā.

Thus, the Blessed One's investigation and Māgaṇḍiya's admission are found in that Sutta.

Ettha hi methunappaṭisevanavasena phoṭṭhabbārammaṇaparibhogahetu eva gabbhapatiṭṭhānaṃ sambhavatīti tadaparibhogameva sandhāya “bhūnahū”ti vattuṃ arahati, tadaññesaṃ pana pañcannaṃ rūpādiārammaṇānaṃ, tatthāpi visesato dhammārammaṇassa suddhamanoviññāṇena paribhogahetu natthi kiñci gabbhapatiṭṭhānanti tesaṃ aparibhogaṃ sandhāya bhūnahūti vattuṃ na arahatiyeva, māgaṇḍiyena pana sabbānipi tāni sandhāya vuttabhāvo paṭiññāto, kāraṇañcassa dassitaṃ “evañhi no sutte ocaratī”ti. Tasmā kiñcāpi dāni brāhmaṇaganthesu bhūnahu- (bhrūnahā) saddo gabbhaghātanatthe dissati, māgaṇḍiyasutte paneso attho na yujjatīti ācariyena “hatavaḍḍhi mariyādakārako”ti ayamevattho porāṇaṭṭhakathāya bhāsāparivattanavasena pakāsitoti veditabbo.

To explain further: In this context, the establishment of an embryo only occurs due to the enjoyment of tactile objects through sexual intercourse. Therefore, it would be appropriate to say 'bhūnahu'—destroyer of growth—only in reference to that non-enjoyment of tactile objects. Apart from that, there is no establishment of an embryo whatsoever through the enjoyment of the other five objects, such as visible forms, and especially through the enjoyment of mental objects by pure mind-consciousness alone. Thus, it is not appropriate to say 'bhūnahu'—destroyer of growth—in reference to the non-enjoyment of those objects other than tactile objects. However, Māgaṇḍiya admitted that he spoke in reference to all those objects, and he gave the reason for his admission: 'Because this is how it is found in our scriptures.' Therefore, although the word 'bhūnahu' (bhrūṇahā) is currently understood in Brahmanical texts to mean the destruction of an embryo, that meaning is not appropriate in the Māgaṇḍiya Sutta. Thus, it should be understood that Ven. Buddhaghosa presented this very meaning—'one who destroys growth, one who sets a limit'—only by way of translating from the ancient commentaries. This concludes another explanation.

(Ga) punapi tena “idampana buddhaghosassa abrāhmaṇabhāvasādhakaṃ pacchimakāraṇaṃ, so hi visuddhimagge sīlaniddese (1, 31) brāhmaṇānaṃ parihāsaṃ karonto ‘evaṃ iminā piṇḍapātapaṭisevanena purāṇañca jighacchāvedanaṃ paṭihaṅkhāmi, navañca vedanaṃ aparimitabhojanapaccayaṃ āharahatthaka alaṃsāṭaka tatravaṭṭaka kākamāsaka bhuttavamitakabrāhmaṇānaṃ aññataro [M 0.0012] viya na uppādessāmīti paṭisevatī’ti āha. Idaṃ pana ekassa bhinnabrāhmaṇaladdhikassāpi vacanaṃ siyāti tadeva daḷhakāraṇaṃ katvā na sakkā ‘buddhaghoso abrāhmaṇo’ti vattu”nti tatiyaṃ kāraṇaṃ vuttaṃ. Taṃ pana atisaṃvejanīyavacanameva. Na hetaṃ ācariyena brāhmaṇānaṃ parihāsaṃ kātukāmena vuttaṃ, na ca taṃ parihāsavacanena saṃyojetabbaṭṭhānaṃ, aññadatthu yathābhūtamatthaṃ dassetvā sabrahmacārīnaṃ ovādānusāsanidānavasena vattabbaṭṭhānaṃ, tathāyeva ca ācariyena vuttaṃ. Tathā hi ye loke paradattūpajīvino samaṇā vā brāhmaṇā vā aññe vāpi ca puggalā, te paccavekkhaṇañāṇarahitā asaṃvare ṭhitā kadāci atipaṇītaṃ rasaṃ pahūtaṃ laddhā aparimitampi bhuñjeyyuṃ, visesato pana brāhmaṇā lokikavatthuvasena ca, jātakādisāsanikavatthuvasena ca tādisā ahesunti pākaṭā. Imasmiñhi loke vassasatasahassehi vā vassakoṭīhi vā aparicchinnaddhāne ko sakkā vattuṃ “nedisā bhūtapubbā”ti. Tasmā tādisehi viya na aparimitabhojanehi bhavitabbanti ovādānusāsanidānavaseneva vuttaṃ. Tadevaṃ atthasaṃhitampi samānaṃ ayonisomanasikaroto anatthameva jātaṃ, yathā sabhariyassa māgaṇḍiyabrāhmaṇassa anāgāmimaggaphalatthāyapi desitā gāthā dha. pa. aṭṭha. 1.sāmāvatīvatthu tesaṃ dhītuyā anatthāya saṃvattatīti saṃvegoyevettha brūhetabboti.

Again, it was said by him: "This is the final reason proving Buddhaghosa's non-Brahmin status. For in the Visuddhimagga, in the section on virtue (1, 31), mocking Brahmins, he says: ‘Thus, by partaking of almsfood in this way, I shall ward off the old feeling of hunger and shall not produce a new feeling—that which is conditioned by unlimited eating—like one of those Brahmins [known as] the hand-lifter, the loincloth-unfastener, the rolling-about, the crow-pecked, and the vomiter.’ However, this could be the statement even of one who has abandoned Brahmin views; therefore, taking this as strong evidence, it is not possible to say, ‘Buddhaghosa was not a Brahmin.’" Thus, the third reason was stated. That statement, however, is indeed most stirring. For that was not spoken by the teacher with the intention of mocking Brahmins, nor is it a place to be associated with words of mockery; rather, it is a place for him to speak by way of instruction and admonition to his fellow practitioners of the holy life, revealing the truth as it is, and it was indeed spoken by the teacher in that manner. For indeed, in this world, whatever ascetics or Brahmins or other individuals live on what is given by others, if they are devoid of the knowledge of reflection and are established in lack of restraint, they might sometimes eat excessively when they obtain very excellent and abundant food—especially Brahmins, who are well-known to have been such, both in terms of worldly matters and in terms of matters related to the Dispensation, such as the Jātakas. Who in this world, in an immeasurable span of hundreds of thousands or even millions of years, can say, 'Such persons have never existed before'? Therefore, it was spoken solely by way of instruction and admonition, that one should not be like those who indulge in limitless eating. Thus, though it was connected with benefit, for one who attends unwisely, it leads only to harm, just as the verse taught to the Brahmin Māgaṇḍiya and his wife, though meant for the path and fruit of non-return, resulted in harm for their daughter. Therefore, here too, a sense of urgency (saṃvega) should be cultivated.

Patañjalivādavicāraṇā

An Examination of the Doctrine of Patañjali

3. Atha tena “pātañjalīmataṃ parivattetī”ti vacanampi evaṃ vicāritaṃ.

3. Then that statement, 'He studied the doctrine of Patañjali,' was also examined in this way.

(Ka) “buddhaghoso patañjalissa vā aññesaṃ vā uttaraindiyaraṭṭhikānaṃ vādaṃ appakameva aññāsi. Patañjalivādesu hi aṇimā laghimāti idameva dvayaṃ dassesi visuddhi. 1.144 tatuttari yogasuttaṃ ajānanto, patañjalivādassa ca tuletvā dīpanā tassa ganthesu na dissati, patañjalinā katapakaraṇañca patañjalīti nāmamattampi ca tattha dīpitaṃ natthi. Visuddhimagge pana paññābhūminiddese ‘pakativādīnaṃ pakati viyā’ti visuddhi. 2.584 pakativāda (saṃkhyāvāda) nāmamattaṃ pakāsitaṃ, tattheva ca ‘paṭiññā hetūtiādīsu hi loke vacanāvayavo [M 0.0013] hetūti vuccatī’ti visuddhi. 2.595 udāharitaṃ, tena ñāyati ‘buddhaghoso indiyatakkanayadīpake ñāyaganthasmiṃ kiñci mūlabhāgamattaṃ aparipuṇṇaṃ jānātī’ti”.

(Ka) "Buddhaghosa knew very little of the doctrine of Patañjali or of others from the northern Indian region. For in the doctrines of Patañjali, he presented only these two—aṇimā and laghimā—without knowing the Yoga Sūtras that go beyond that. Moreover, no explanation weighing the doctrine of Patañjali is found in his works, and not even the mere name 'Patañjali' or the treatises composed by Patañjali are mentioned there. However, in the Visuddhimagga, in the section on the ground of wisdom (2, 156), the mere name of the doctrine of nature (Sāṅkhya doctrine) is mentioned with the phrase 'like the nature of the proponents of nature.' And in that same section, he also cited the example: 'Indeed, in the world, in statements such as "proposition, reason," etc., the part of a statement is called "reason."' From this, it is known that Buddhaghosa knew only a certain incomplete, foundational portion of the Nyāya texts illustrating Indian logic."

Taṃ pana sabbampi kevalaṃ ācariyassa abbhācikkhaṇamattameva. Atigambhīrassa hi atigarukātabbassa suparisuddhassa piṭakattayassa atthasaṃvaṇṇanaṃ karontena suparisuddhoyeva pāḷinayo ca aṭṭhakathānayo ca porāṇatheravādā cāti īdisāyeva atthā pakāsetabbā, yaṃ vā pana atthasaṃvaṇṇanāya upakārakaṃ saddavinicchayapaṭisaṃyuttaṃ lokiyaganthavacanaṃ, tadeva ca yathārahaṃ pakāsetabbaṃ, na pana anupakārānipi taṃtaṃganthatakkattunāmāni ca, tehi vuttavacanāni ca bahūni, na ca tesaṃ appakāsanena “na te aṭṭhakathācariyo jānātī”ti vattabbo. Yadi hi yaṃ yaṃ lokiyaganthaṃ attanā jānāti, taṃ sabbaṃ anupakārampi attano aṭṭhakathāyamānetvā pakāseyya, ativitthārā ca sā bhaveyya aparisuddhā ca asammānitā ca sāsanikaviññūhīti ācariyena patañjalivādādayo na vitthārena pakāsitāti ñātabbaṃ, aññadatthu yehi yehi lokiyaganthehi kiñci kiñci ācariyena ānetvā pakāsitaṃ, te te ca ganthā, aññepi ca tādisā ācariyena ñātātveva jānitabbā viññūhi, yathā samuddassa ekadesaṃ disvā sabbopi samuddo edisoti ñāyati. Ācariyo pana yattha yattha vedapaṭisaṃyuttavacanāni āgatāni, tattha tattha vedaganthehipi kiñci kiñci ānetvā pakāsesiyeva. Tathā hi ācariyena sumaṅgalavilāsiniyaṃ nāma dīghanikāyaṭṭhakathāyaṃ –

However, all that is merely a false accusation against the teacher. For one who explains the meaning of the exceedingly profound, highly revered, and supremely pure Tipiṭaka, only such meanings should be expounded as are in accordance with the supremely pure Pāli method, the commentary method, and the traditions of the ancient Elders. Or, whatever text from worldly literature connected with linguistic analysis may be helpful in explaining the meaning, that alone should be appropriately presented; but not those that are unhelpful, nor the names of various treatises and their authors, nor their many statements. Nor, on account of their not being mentioned, should it be said, 'The commentator does not know them.' For if one were to present every worldly text one knows—even if unhelpful—by bringing it into one’s own commentary, it would become excessively lengthy, impure, and unesteemed by the wise in the Dispensation. Thus, it should be understood that the teacher did not elaborate on the doctrines of Patañjali and others. Rather, whatever worldly texts the teacher has drawn upon to explain certain points, those texts—and others like them—should be recognized by the wise as known to the teacher, just as upon seeing one part of the ocean, it is known that the entire ocean is of such a nature. Moreover, wherever statements connected with the Vedas appear, the teacher has indeed drawn upon and explained things from the Vedic texts. For example, in the Dīghanikāya commentary called the Sumaṅgalavilāsinī, the teacher states:

“Tiṇṇaṃ vedānanti iruvedayajuvedasāmavedāna”nti dī. ni. aṭṭha. 1.256 ca,

'"Of the three Vedas" means of the Rigveda, Yajurveda, and Sāmaveda.'

“Itihāsapañcamānanti athabbaṇavedaṃ catutthaṃ katvā itiha āsa itiha āsāti īdisavacanapaṭisaṃyutto purāṇakathāsaṅkhāto itihāso pañcamo etesanti itihāsapañcamā, tesaṃ itihāsapañcamānaṃ vedāna”nti dī. ni. aṭṭha. 1.256 ca,

'"Those with the Itihāsa as fifth" means that, taking the Atharvaveda as the fourth, the Itihāsa—which is reckoned as ancient lore connected with such statements as "thus it was, thus it was"—is the fifth of these; hence they are called "those with the Itihāsa as fifth," of those Vedas.'

“Yiṭṭhaṃ vuccati mahāyāgo”ti dī. ni. aṭṭha. 1.170-172 ca,

'Yiṭṭha is said to be a great sacrifice.'

“Aggihomanti [M 0.0014] evarūpena dārunā evaṃ hute idaṃ nāma hotīti aggijuhanaṃ. Dabbihomādīnipi aggihomāneva, evarūpāya dabbiyā īdisehi kaṇādīhi hute idaṃ nāma hotīti evaṃ pavattivasena pana visuṃ vuttānī”ti dī. ni. aṭṭha. 1.21 ca,

'Aggihoma is the offering into fire, with the thought, "When an offering is made in this way with such-and-such wood, such-and-such a result occurs." Dabbihoma and the like are also fire offerings; however, they are mentioned separately on account of the manner of their performance, with the thought, "When an offering is made with such-and-such a ladle and with such-and-such grains, such-and-such a result occurs."'

“Sāsapādīni pana mukhena gahetvā aggimhi pakkhipanaṃ, vijjaṃ parijappitvā juhanaṃ vā mukhahoma”nti dī. ni. aṭṭha. 1.21 ca –

And, ‘Taking mustard seeds and the like in the mouth and casting them into the fire, or muttering a spell and making an offering, is called mukhahoma.’

Evamādinā vedapaṭisaṃyuttavacanāni vedaganthānurūpato vaṇṇitāni. Tāni ca porāṇaṭṭhakathāto bhāsāparivattanavasena vuttānipi bhaveyyuṃ, vedaganthesu pana akovidena yāthāvato bhāsāparivattanaṃ kātumpi na sukarameva, tasmā ācariyassa vedaganthesu kovidabhāvopi pākaṭoyeva. Evaṃ vedaganthesu ca tadaññalokiyaganthesu ca sukovidasseva samānassa tesaṃ vitthārato appakāsanaṃ yathāvuttakāraṇenevāti veditabbaṃ.

In this way, statements connected with the Vedas have been explained in accordance with the Vedic texts. And these might also have been stated by way of translation from the ancient commentaries; however, for one unskilled in the Vedic texts, it is indeed not easy to make a translation that is true to the original. Therefore, the teacher's expertise in the Vedic texts is clearly evident. Thus, it should be understood that the non-exposition in detail of these, as well as other worldly texts, by one who was so skilled in them, is for the very reason stated above.

Api ca ācariyo attano ganthārambheyeva –

Moreover, the teacher, at the very beginning of his work, stated:

“Tato ca bhāsantarameva hitvā,

Vitthāramaggañca samāsayitvā;

Vinicchayaṃ sabbamasesayitvā…pe…

Yasmā ayaṃ hessati vaṇṇanāpī”ti pārā. aṭṭha. 1.ganthārambhakathā ca.

And, ‘Having abandoned that other language, and having condensed the extensive path; leaving no analysis out… for this too shall be an exposition.’

“Apanetvāna tatohaṃ, sīhaḷabhāsaṃ manoramaṃ bhāsaṃ;

Tantinayānucchavikaṃ, āropetvā vigatadosaṃ.

‘Having removed from it the Sinhalese language, I shall adopt the pleasing language that is faultless and in keeping with the textual method.

Samayaṃ avilomento, therānaṃ theravaṃsapadīpānaṃ;

Sunipuṇavinicchayānaṃ, mahāvihāre nivāsinaṃ;

Hitvā punappunāgata-matthaṃ atthaṃ pakāsayissāmī”ti dī. ni. aṭṭha. 1.ganthārambhakathā ca–

Not violating the doctrine of the elders—the lamps of the elders’ lineage, of subtle judgment, dwelling in the Mahāvihāra—and omitting repetitive matter, I shall explain the meaning.’

Evaṃ porāṇaṭṭhakathānaṃ bhāsāparivattanasaṃkhipanavaseneva visesetvā abhinavaṭṭhakathāyo karissāmīti paṭiññaṃ katvā yathāpaṭiññātameva akāsi, na attano ñāṇappabhāvena visesetvātipi veditabbaṃ. Tasmā aṭṭhakathāsu [M 0.0015] patañjalivādādīnaṃ vitthārato appakāsanamārabbha “buddhaghoso patañjalivādādīni paripuṇṇaṃ na jānātī”ti vacanaṃ kevalaṃ ācariyassa abbhācikkhaṇamattamevāti.

Thus, having made the promise, ‘I will compose new commentaries, distinguishing them specifically by way of translating and condensing the ancient commentaries,’ he acted exactly as he had promised; and it should be understood that he did not distinguish them through the power of his own knowledge. Therefore, concerning the non-exposition in detail of the doctrines of Patañjali and so forth in the commentaries, the statement, ‘Buddhaghosa does not fully know the doctrines of Patañjali and so forth,’ is merely a slander against the teacher.

Kabbasatthavicāraṇā

An Examination of the Poetic Treatises

4. Punapi so evamāha “kiñcāpi buddhaghoso rāmāyaṇamahābhāratasaṅkhātānaṃ mahākabbasatthānaṃ sukusalo viya na dissati, tathāpi tāni dassesi. Kathaṃ? Akkhānanti bhāratayujjhanādikaṃ, taṃ yasmiṃ ṭhāne kathīyati, tattha gantumpi na vaṭṭatīti dī. ni. aṭṭha. 1.13 ca, tassa (samphapalāpassa) dve sambhārā bhāratayuddhasītāharaṇādiniratthakakathāpurekkhāratā tathārūpikathākathanañcāti dī. ni. aṭṭha. 1.8 ca dassesī”ti.

4. Again, he said thus: “Although Buddhaghosa does not appear to be very skilled in the great poetic treatises known as the Rāmāyaṇa and the Mahābhārata, he nevertheless mentioned them. How? By stating, ‘It is not proper even to go to a place where an Ākhyāna, such as the Bhārata war, is recited,’ and by showing that the two components of that frivolous talk are giving precedence to useless tales such as the Bhārata war and the abduction of Sītā, and the telling of such tales.”

Taṃ pana purimavacanatopi ahetukataraṃ kevalaṃ anādarīkaraṇamattameva. Atigambhīratthassa hi atigarukaraṇīyassa piṭakattayassa atthasaṃvaṇṇanāyaṃ niratthakassa samphapalāpasamudāyabhūtassa kabbasatthassa vitthārato pakāsanena kiṃ siyā payojanaṃ, aññadatthu sāyevassa asammānitā, anādariyā ca viññūhīti.

But that statement is even more groundless than the previous one; it is merely an act of disrespect. For, in an explanation of the meaning of the Tipiṭaka, which has an exceedingly profound meaning and is worthy of great respect, what purpose would be served by an extensive exposition of poetic treatises, which are useless and a collection of frivolous talk? On the contrary, that very act would be disrespectful and disregarded by the wise.

Bāhusaccaguṇamakkhanaṃ

The Disparagement of the Virtue of Great Learning

5. Punapi dhammānando ācariyassa bāhusaccaguṇaṃ makkhetukāmo evamāha – “tassa (buddhaghosassa) samayantarakovidasaṅkhātaṃ bāhusaccaṃ na tato uttaritaraṃ hoti, yaṃ ādhunikānaṃ ganthantarakovidānaṃ sīhaḷikabhikkhūnaṃ yaṃ vā ekādasame kharistavassasatake (1001-1100) uppannānaṃ dakkhiṇaindiyaraṭṭhikānaṃ anuruddha-dhammapālādīnaṃ bhikkhūna”nti.

5. Again, Dhammānanda, wishing to disparage the teacher’s virtue of great learning, said thus: “His (Buddhaghosa’s) great learning, known as expertise in other systems, is not superior to that of modern Sinhalese monks who are experts in other texts, nor to that of monks such as Anuruddha and Dhammapāla from the South Indian region, who arose in the eleventh Christian century (1001-1100).”

Taṃ pana sabbathāpi ayuttavacanameva. Yadi hi ādhunikā vā sīhaḷikabhikkhū, porāṇā vā ācariyaanuruddha-dhammapālattherādayo samayantarabāhusaccavasena ācariyabuddhaghosena samānā vā uttaritarā vā bhaveyyuṃ, te ācariyabuddhaghosattherassa aṭṭhakathāhi anāraddhacittā hutvā tato sundaratarā paripuṇṇatarā ca abhinavaṭṭhakathāyo kareyyuṃ, na pana te tathā karonti, na kevalaṃ na karontiyeva, atha [M 0.0016] kho tesaṃ ekopi na evaṃ vadati “ahaṃ buddhaghosena bāhusaccavasena samasamoti vā uttaritaro”ti vā, aññadatthu te ācariyassa aṭṭhakathāyoyeva saṃvaṇṇenti ca upatthambhenti ca, ācariyaṭṭhāne ca ṭhapenti. Tenetaṃ ñāyati sabbathāpi ayuttavacananti.

But that is in every way an inappropriate statement. For if modern Sinhalese monks, or the ancient teachers, the elders Anuruddha, Dhammapāla, and others, were equal or superior to the teacher Buddhaghosa by way of great learning in other systems, they, being dissatisfied with the Elder Buddhaghosa’s commentaries, would have composed new commentaries that were more beautiful and more complete. But they do not do so. Not only do they not do so, but not a single one of them says, “I am equal to or superior to Buddhaghosa by way of great learning.” On the contrary, they only praise and support the teacher’s commentaries and place him in the position of a teacher. By this it is known that it is in every way an inappropriate statement.

Mahāyānikanayavicāraṇā

An Examination of the Mahāyāna System

6. Puna so tāvattakenāpi asantuṭṭho ācariyaṃ avamaññanto evamāha – “mahāyānanikāyassa padhānācariyabhūtānaṃ assa ghosa-nāgajjunānaṃ nayaṃ vā, nāmamattampi vā tesaṃ na jānāti maññe buddhaghoso”ti. Taṃ pana ativiya adhammikaṃ niratthakañca niggahavacanamattameva. Na hi nikāyantarikānaṃ vādanayānaṃ attano aṭṭhakathāyaṃ appakāsanena so te na jānātīti sakkā vattuṃ. Nanu ācariyena āgamaṭṭhakathāsu ganthārambheyeva –

6. Again, not satisfied even with that, he, disrespecting the teacher, said thus: “I think Buddhaghosa does not know the system or even the mere names of Aśvaghoṣa and Nāgārjuna, who were the chief teachers of the Mahāyāna school.” But that is an extremely unjust and meaningless statement, merely a word of censure. For it cannot be said that he does not know them simply because he does not expound upon the doctrinal systems of other schools in his own commentaries. Indeed, did not the teacher, at the very beginning of the commentaries on the Āgamas, state:

“Samayaṃ avilomento, therānaṃ theravaṃsapadīpānaṃ;

Sunipuṇavinicchayānaṃ, mahāvihāre nivāsina”nti ca,

‘Not violating the doctrine of the elders, the lamps of the elders’ lineage, of subtle judgment, dwelling in the Mahāvihāra…’

Idhāpi visuddhimagge –

Here also, in the Visuddhimagga:

“Mahāvihāravāsīnaṃ, desanānayanissitaṃ;

Visuddhimaggaṃ bhāsissa”nti visuddhi. 1.2 ca,

‘Relying on the method of teaching of the residents of the Mahāvihāra, I shall expound the Visuddhimagga.’

“Tassā atthasaṃvaṇṇanaṃ karontena vibhajjavādimaṇḍalaṃ otaritvā ācariye anabbhācikkhantena sakasamayaṃ avokkamantena parasamayaṃ anāyūhantena suttaṃ appaṭibāhantena vinayaṃ anulomentena mahāpadese olokentena dhammaṃ dīpentena atthaṃ saṅgāhentena tamevatthaṃ punarāvattetvā aparehipi pariyāyantarehi niddisantena ca yasmā atthasaṃvaṇṇanā kātabbā hotī”ti visuddhi. 2.581 ca,

‘Because an explanation of its meaning is to be made, it should be made by one who, having entered the circle of the Vibhajjavādins, does not slander the teachers, does not deviate from his own doctrine, does not strive after the doctrine of others, does not reject the Suttas, conforms to the Vinaya, consults the Great References, illuminates the Dhamma, summarizes the meaning, and, repeating that very meaning, points it out through other alternative methods.’

“Sāsanaṃ panidaṃ nānā-desanānayamaṇḍitaṃ;

Pubbācariyamaggo ca, abbocchinno pavattati;

Yasmā tasmā tadubhayaṃ, sannissāyatthavaṇṇanaṃ;

Ārabhissāmi etassā”ti visuddhi. 2.581 ca,

‘Now this Dispensation is adorned with various methods of teaching, and the path of the ancient teachers continues unbroken; therefore, relying on both, I shall begin the explanation of its meaning.’

Paṭiññaṃ [M 0.0017] katvā yathāpaṭiññātappakāreneva aṭṭhakathāyo katā. Evametāsaṃ karaṇe kāraṇampettha pakāsetabbaṃ, tasmā dāni tampakāsanatthaṃ sammāsambuddhassa parinibbutikālato paṭṭhāya yāva ācariyabuddhaghosassa kālo, tāva sāsanappavattikkamampi vakkhāma.

Having made this promise, the commentaries were composed in the very manner promised. The reason for their composition should also be explained here; therefore, for the purpose of explaining that, we shall now speak of the sequence of the Dispensation's progress from the time of the Parinibbāna of the Perfectly Enlightened One up to the time of the teacher Buddhaghosa.

Sāsanappavattikkamo

The Sequence of the Dispensation’s Progress

Bhagavato hi parinibbutikālato pacchā vassasatabbhantare buddhasāsane kocipi vādabhedo nāma natthi. Vassasatakāle pana dutiyasaṅgītikārehi therehi nikkaḍḍhitā vajjiputtakā bhikkhū pakkhaṃ labhitvā dhammañca vinayañca aññathā katvā mahāsaṅgītināmena visuṃ saṅgītimakaṃsu. Tadā saṅgītidvayārūḷhapurāṇadhammavinayameva sampaṭicchantānaṃ therānaṃ gaṇo theravādoti ca tadaññesaṃ mahāsaṅghikoti ca voharīyanti.

Indeed, within a hundred years after the Parinibbāna of the Blessed One, there was no division of views in the Buddha's Dispensation. But at the hundredth year, the Vajjiputtaka monks, who had been expelled by the elders who conducted the Second Council, gained a following and, having altered the Dhamma and the Vinaya, held a separate council under the name ‘Mahāsaṅgīti.’ At that time, the group of elders who accepted only the ancient Dhamma and Vinaya as established in the two councils were called ‘Theravāda,’ while the others were called ‘Mahāsaṅghika.’

Puna mahāsaṅghikato (1) gokuliko (2) ekabyohārikoti dve ācariyagaṇā uppannā. Puna gokulikato (3) paññattivādo (4) bāhuliko (bahussutiko)ti dve uppannā. Puna bāhulikatopi (5) cetiyavādigaṇo uppannoti ete pañca mūlabhūtena mahāsaṅghikena saha cha pāṭiyekkā ācariyagaṇā ahesuṃ.

Again, from the Mahāsaṅghika, two schools of teachers arose: (1) the Gokulika and (2) the Ekabyohārika. Again, from the Gokulika, two arose: (3) the Paññattivāda and (4) the Bāhulika (or Bahussutika). Again, from the Bāhulika, the (5) Cetiyavāda school arose. These five, together with the original Mahāsaṅghika, were six distinct schools of teachers.

Visuddhattheravādatopi (1) mahisāsako (2) vajjiputtakoti dve ācariyagaṇā uppannā. Puna mahisāsakato (3) sabbatthivādo (4) dhammaguttikoti dve uppannā. Puna sabbatthivādatopi (5) kassapiyo, tatopi (6) saṅkantiko, tatopi (7) suttavādīti tayo uppannā. Vajjiputtakatopi (8) dhammottariyo (9) bhaddayāniko (10) channāgāriko (11) sammitiyoti cattāro uppannāti te ekādasa mūlabhūtena visuddhattheravādena saha dvādasa ācariyagaṇā ahesuṃ. Iti ime ca dvādasa purimā ca chāti aṭṭhārasa ācariyagaṇā dutiyatatiyasaṅgītīnaṃ antare jātā ahesuṃ.

From the pure Theravāda, two schools of teachers arose: (1) the Mahisāsaka and (2) the Vajjiputtaka. Again, from the Mahisāsaka, two arose: (3) the Sabbatthivāda and (4) the Dhammaguttika. Again, from the Sabbatthivāda, three arose: (5) the Kassapiya, from them (6) the Saṅkantika, and from them (7) the Suttavādī. From the Vajjiputtaka, four arose: (8) the Dhammottariya, (9) the Bhaddayānika, (10) the Channāgārika, and (11) the Sammitiya. Thus, these eleven, together with the original pure Theravāda, were twelve schools of teachers. So these twelve and the previous six were eighteen schools of teachers that arose between the Second and Third Councils.

Tesu mūlabhūto theravādagaṇoyeva porāṇadhammavinayagaruko hutvā anūnamanadhikaṃ kevalaparipuṇṇaṃ parisuddhaṃ porāṇikaṃ dhammavinayaṃ dhāresi. Itare pana sattarasa bhinnagaṇā porāṇikaṃ dhammavinayaṃ aññathā [M 0.0018] akaṃsu. Tena tesaṃ dhammavinayo katthaci ūno katthaci adhiko hutvā aparipuṇṇo ceva ahosi aparisuddho ca. Tena vuttaṃ dīpavaṃse pañcamaparicchede –

Among them, only the original Theravāda school, revering the ancient Dhamma and Vinaya, preserved the ancient Dhamma and Vinaya as entirely complete and pure, without deficiency or excess. But the other seventeen schismatic schools altered the ancient Dhamma and Vinaya. Consequently, their Dhamma and Vinaya became deficient in some places and excessive in others, being both incomplete and impure. Therefore, it is said in the fifth chapter of the Dīpavaṃsa—

30.

30.

“Nikkaḍḍhitā pāpabhikkhū, therehi vajjiputtakā;

Aññaṃ pakkhaṃ labhitvāna, adhammavādī bahū janā.

“The wicked Vajjiputtaka monks were expelled by the elders; having gained another following, many people became proponents of non-Dhamma.

31.

31.

Dasasahassā samāgantvā, akaṃsu dhammasaṅgahaṃ;

Tasmāyaṃ dhammasaṅgīti, mahāsaṅgītīti vuccati.

Ten thousand having assembled, they made a compilation of the Dhamma; therefore, this Dhamma council is called the Mahāsaṅgīti.

32.

32.

Mahāsaṅgītikā bhikkhū, vilomaṃ akaṃsu sāsane;

Bhinditvā mūlasaṅgahaṃ, aññaṃ akaṃsu saṅgahaṃ.

The monks of the Mahāsaṅgīti acted contrary to the Dispensation; having broken the original compilation, they made another compilation.

33.

33.

Aññatra saṅgahitaṃ suttaṃ, aññatra akariṃsu te;

Atthaṃ dhammañca bhindiṃsu, vinaye nikāyesu ca pañcasu…pe…

They placed a sutta compiled in one place in another place; they corrupted the meaning and the doctrine in the Vinaya and in the five Nikāyas... etc...

49.

49.

Atthaṃ dhammañca bhindiṃsu, ekadesañca saṅgahaṃ;

Ganthañca ekadesañhi, chaḍḍetvā aññaṃ akaṃsu te.

They corrupted the meaning and the doctrine, and a portion of the compilation; and having discarded a portion of the texts, they made others.

50.

50.

Nāmaṃ liṅgaṃ parikkhāraṃ, ākappakaraṇīyāni ca;

Pakatibhāvaṃ jahitvā, tañca aññaṃ akaṃsu te.

Having abandoned the original state, they made these otherwise: the names, the outward appearance, the requisites, and the modes of conduct.

51.

51.

Sattarasa bhinnavādā, ekavādo abhinnako;

Sabbevaṭṭhārasa honti, bhinnavādena te saha.

There are seventeen schismatic doctrines, and one non-schismatic doctrine; altogether they are eighteen, along with the schismatic ones.

52.

52.

Nigrodhova mahārukkho, thera vādānamuttamo;

Anūnaṃ anadhikañca, kevalaṃ jinasāsanaṃ;

Kaṇṭakā viya rukkhamhi, nibbattā vādasesakā.

The Theravāda, supreme among doctrines, is like a great banyan tree; it is the entire teaching of the Conqueror, without deficiency or excess. The remaining doctrines have arisen like thorns on a tree.

53.

53.

Paṭhame vassasate natthi, dutiye vassasatantare;

Bhinnā sattarasa vādā, uppannā jinasāsane”ti kathā. aṭṭha. nidānakathā.

In the first century there were none; within the second century, seventeen schismatic doctrines arose in the Dispensation of the Conqueror.”

Asokarañño ca kāle parihīnalābhasakkārā aññatitthiyā lābhasakkāraṃ patthayamānā bhikkhūsu pabbajitvā sakāni sakāni diṭṭhigatāni dīpenti “ayaṃ dhammo, ayaṃ vinayo, idaṃ satthusāsana”nti. Bhikkhūnaṃ santike pabbajjaṃ alabhamānāpi sayameva kese chinditvā kāsāyāni vatthāni acchādetvā vihāresu vicarantā uposathakammādikaraṇakāle saṅghamajjhaṃ [M 0.0019] pavisanti, te bhikkhusaṅghena dhammena vinayena satthusāsanena niggayhamānāpi dhammavinayānulomāya paṭipattiyā asaṇṭhahantā anekarūpaṃ sāsanassa abbudañca malañca kaṇṭakañca samuṭṭhāpenti. Keci aggiṃ paricaranti, keci pañcātape tapanti, keci ādiccaṃ anuparivattanti, keci dhammañca vinayañca vobhindissāmāti tathā tathā paggaṇhanti. Tadā bhikkhusaṅgho na tehi saddhiṃ uposathaṃ vā pavāraṇaṃ vā akāsi, asokārāme satta vassāni uposatho upacchijji kathā. aṭṭha. nidānakathā; pārā. aṭṭha. 1.tatiyasaṅgītikathā.

And in the time of King Asoka, heretics of other sects who were deprived of gain and honor, desiring gain and honor, went forth among the monks and proclaimed their own views, saying: ‘This is the Dhamma, this is the Vinaya, this is the Teacher’s Dispensation.’ Even those who did not obtain ordination from the monks, having shaved their own hair and donned saffron robes, wandered through the monasteries and entered the midst of the Sangha at the time of performing the Uposatha ceremony and so on. Though they were restrained by the community of monks with the Dhamma, the Vinaya, and the Teacher’s Dispensation, they did not stand firm in practice conforming to the Dhamma and Vinaya, and they gave rise to many excrescences, stains, and thorns in the Dispensation. Some tended the fire, some performed the fivefold heat penance, some revolved following the sun, and some strove in various ways, thinking: ‘We will split the Dhamma and the Vinaya.’ At that time, the community of monks did not perform the Uposatha or the Pavāraṇa with them; for seven years, the Uposatha was interrupted in the Asokārāma.

Imañca pana pavattiṃ upādāya evampi sakkā gahetuṃ “sattarasannaṃ bhinnavādagaṇānaṃ dhammavinayassa pacchimakālesu aparisuddhatarabhāvo īdisenapi kāraṇena ahosī”ti. Kiñcāpi hi buddhasāsanabhūte parisuddhadhammavinaye “kocipi nicco dhuvo sassato nāma natthi aññatra nibbānadhātuyā, paramatthato attāpi natthi, sabbepi saṅkhārā aniccā addhuvā asassatā anattāyevā”ti attho ativiya pākaṭo hoti, tathāpi dāni atheravādikānaṃ ganthesu ca pubbe vetullavādādīsu ca “buddho nicco dhuvo sassato attā”ti ca, “sabbepi sattā niccā dhuvā sassatā attā”ti ca attho dissati.

And considering this situation, it can also be held thus: "The more impure state of the Dhamma and Vinaya of the seventeen divergent sects in later times was also due to such a reason." For although in the pure Dhamma and Vinaya that is the Buddha's Dispensation, the meaning is extremely clear—"There is nothing whatever permanent, stable, or eternal except for the Nibbāna element; in the ultimate sense there is no self; all conditioned things are indeed impermanent, unstable, non-eternal, and not-self"—nevertheless, now in the books of those who are not Theravādins and previously among the Vetullavādins and others, such meanings as "The Buddha is permanent, stable, eternal, and the self" and "All beings are permanent, stable, eternal, and selves" are found.

Atha asoko dhammarājā sāsanaṃ visodhetukāmo moggaliputtatissattherassa santike paṭhamameva samayaṃ uggaṇhitvā ekaladdhike ekaladdhike bhikkhū ekato kāretvā ekamekaṃ bhikkhusamūhaṃ pakkosāpetvā pucchi “kiṃ vādī bhante sammāsambuddho”ti. Tato ye ye “sammāsambuddho sassatavādī”ti vā, “ekaccasassatavādī”ti vā evamādinā attano attano vādānurūpaṃ micchāvādaṃ āhaṃsu, te te “nayime bhikkhū, aññatitthiyā ime”ti tathato ñatvā tesaṃ setakāni vatthāni datvā uppabbājesi. Te sabbepi saṭṭhisahassamattā ahesuṃ.

Then King Asoka, the Dhamma-King, wishing to purify the Dispensation, first having learned the doctrine in the presence of the Elder Moggaliputtatissa, gathered together the monks of each respective view. Having summoned each group of monks, he asked: "Venerable sirs, what view did the Perfectly Enlightened One hold?" Then those who, according to their own views, stated a wrong view, such as "The Perfectly Enlightened One is an eternalist" or "He is a partial-eternalist" and so forth—having recognized them as they truly were, that "These are not monks; these are outsiders belonging to other sects"—he gave them white clothes and expelled them. All of them numbered about sixty thousand.

Athaññe bhikkhū pucchitvā tehi “vibhajjavādī mahārāja sammāsambuddho”ti vutte “suddhaṃ dāni bhante sāsanaṃ, karotu bhikkhusaṅgho uposatha”nti vatvā ārakkhañca datvā nagaraṃ pāvisi. Samaggo saṅgho sannipatitvā uposathaṃ akāsi. Tasmiṃ samāgame moggaliputtatissatthero yāni ca [M 0.0020] tadā uppannāni vatthūni yāni ca āyatiṃ uppajjissanti, sabbesampi tesaṃ paṭibāhanatthaṃ satthārā dinnanayavaseneva tathāgatena ṭhapitamātikaṃ vibhajanto parappavādamaddanaṃ kathāvatthuṃ nāma abhidhammapiṭake pañcamaṃ pakaraṇaṃ abhāsi. Tato moggaliputtatissattherappamukhā tipiṭakapariyattidharā pabhinnapaṭisambhidāpattā sahassaṃ bhikkhū theravādino saṅgītidvayārūḷhaṃ parisuddhaṃ porāṇadhammavinayaṃ puna saṅgāyitvā surakkhitaṃ rakkhiṃsu pārā. aṭṭha. 1.tatiyasaṅgītikathā.

Then, having asked other monks, and when they replied, "Great King, the Perfectly Enlightened One is a proponent of the analytic doctrine (Vibhajjavādin)," he said: "Venerable sirs, the Dispensation is now pure; let the Sangha of monks perform the Uposatha." Having said this and provided protection, he entered the city. The united Sangha assembled and performed the Uposatha. At that assembly, the Elder Moggaliputtatissa—for the purpose of refuting all those points that had arisen then and those that would arise in the future—expounded the Kathāvatthu, the fifth book in the Abhidhamma Piᙣaka, which crushes the doctrines of others, by analyzing the Mātikā established by the Tathāgata solely through the method given by the Teacher. Thereafter, a thousand Theravāda monks, headed by the Elder Moggaliputtatissa—who were bearers of the Three Piᙣakas and had attained the analytical knowledges—again recited and well-preserved the pure, ancient Dhamma and Vinaya that had been established in the previous two Councils.

Atha moggaliputtatissatthero navasu paccantaṭṭhānesu sāsanapatiṭṭhāpanatthaṃ nava nāyakatthere uccinitvā pesesi. Tesu aṭṭhahi therehi attano attano pattaṭṭhānaṃ gantvā buddhasāsane patiṭṭhāpite mahāmahindatthero chattiṃsādhikadvisate (236) buddhavasse jambudīpato sīhaḷadīpaṃ gantvā devānaṃpiyatissarājappamukhaṃ dīpakajanasamūhaṃ pasādetvā buddhasāsanaṃ sampatiṭṭhāpesi, tena ca raññā dinnaṃ mahāmeghavanuyyānaṃ paṭiggahetvā tattha mahāvihāraṃ nāma saṅghārāmaṃ patiṭṭhāpesi pārā. aṭṭha. 1.tatiyasaṅgītikathā. Tato pabhuti sīhaḷadīpe buddhasāsanaṃ yāva vaṭṭagāmaṇirājakālā nikāyantaravādākularahitaṃ nimmalaṃ suparisuddhaṃ hutvā samujjalittha. Vaṭṭagāmaṇirājakālato pana paṭṭhāya nikāyantaravādāpi sīhaḷadīpamupāgamiṃsu. Tadā visuddhattheravādino yathā purāṇadhammavinayo tehi nikāyantaravādehi asammisso amalīno pakatiparisuddho hutvā tiṭṭheyya, tathā taṃ mahussāhena surakkhitaṃ rakkhiṃsu. Kathaṃ?

Then the Elder Moggaliputtatissa, for the purpose of establishing the Dispensation in nine outlying regions, selected and sent nine leading elders. When the Buddha’s Dispensation had been established by eight of those elders after they had gone to their respective regions, the Great Elder Mahinda, in the 236th year of the Buddha era, went from JambudĦpa to the island of SĦhaጶa. Having inspired faith in the people of the island, headed by King Devānampiyatissa, he firmly established the Buddha’s Dispensation. Accepting the Mahāmeghavana Park given by that king, he established there the great monastery called the Mahāvihāra. From then on, the Buddha’s Dispensation in SĦhaጶadĦpa, free from the confusion of other sectarian views, remained stainless, perfectly pure, and radiant until the time of King Vaᙣᙣagāmaፓi. However, from the time of King Vaᙣᙣagāmaፓi onward, other sectarian views also reached SĦhaጶadĦpa. At that time, the pure Theravāda elders, in order that the ancient Dhamma and Vinaya might remain unmixed with those sectarian views, unstained, and naturally pure, guarded it with great effort. How?

Abhayagirinikāyuppatti

The Origin of the Abhayagiri Sect

Vaṭṭagāmaṇirājā hi (425-buddhavasse) rajjaṃ patvā pañcamāsamattakāle brāhmaṇatissadāmarikena sattahi ca damiḷayodhehi upadduto saṅgāme ca parājito palāyitvā sādhikāni cuddasavassāni nilīyitvā aññataravesena vasati mahāvaṃse 33-paricchede 37-gāthāto paṭṭhāya. Tadā laṅkādīpe manussā corabhayena dubbhikkhabhayena ca upaddutā bhikkhūnaṃ catūhi paccayehi upaṭṭhātuṃ na sakkonti, tena bhikkhū yebhuyyena tato jambudīpaṃ gantvā dhammavinayaṃ dhārentā viharanti. Laṅkādīpeyeva ohīnāpi therā yathāladdhehi kandamūlapaṇṇehi yāpentā kāye vahante nisīditvā pariyattidhammaṃ sajjhāyaṃ karonti, avahante [M 0.0021] vālukaṃ ussāpetvā taṃ parivāretvā sīsāni ekaṭṭhāne katvā pariyattiṃ sammasanti. Evaṃ dvādasa saṃvaccharāni sāṭṭhakathaṃ tepiṭakaṃ ahāpetvā dhārayiṃsu. Yadā pana vaṭṭagāmaṇirājā damiḷarājānaṃ hantvā (455-466 buddhavassabbhantare) punapi rajjaṃ kāresi mahāvaṃse 33, 78-gāthā. Tadā te therā jambudīpato paccāgatattherehi saddhiṃ tepiṭakaṃ sodhentā ekakkharampi asamentaṃ nāma na passiṃsu a. ni. aṭṭha. 1.1.130; vibha. aṭṭha. 810. Yopi ca mahāniddeso tasmiṃ kāle ekasseva dussīlabhikkhuno paguṇo ahosi, sopi mahātipiṭakattherena mahārakkhitattheraṃ tassa santikā uggaṇhāpetvā rakkhito ahosi pārā. aṭṭha. 2.585. Evaṃ dubbhikkharaṭṭhakkhobhupaddavehi pīḷitattā duddharasamayepi dhammavinayaṃ sakkaccaṃ dhārayiṃsu.

For King Vaᙣᙣagāmaፓi (in the 425th year of the Buddha era), having attained the throne, was harassed five months later by the rebel Brahmin Tissa and seven Damiጶa warriors. Defeated in battle, he fled and lived in hiding in disguise for over fourteen years. At that time, the people in the island of Laፅkā, oppressed by the fear of thieves and the fear of famine, were unable to attend to the monks with the four requisites. Therefore, most monks went from there to JambudĦpa and lived there, upholding the Dhamma and Vinaya. Even the elders who remained in the island of Laፅkā, sustaining themselves on whatever roots, tubers, and leaves they could obtain, would sit and recite the scriptural Dhamma when their bodies could bear it; when they could not, they would pile up sand, surround it, and place their heads together in one place to review the scriptures. In this way, for twelve years, they upheld the Three Piᙣakas together with the Commentaries without any loss. When King Vaᙣᙣagāmaፓi killed the Damiጶa kings and ruled the kingdom again (between the 455th and 466th years of the Buddha era), those elders, together with the elders who returned from JambudĦpa, collated the Three Piᙣakas and found not even a single syllable that was inconsistent. Even the Mahāniddesa, which at that time was known by heart by only a single immoral monk, was preserved by the great Tipiᙣaka Elder having the Elder Mahārakkhita learn it from him. Thus, although afflicted by famine and the upheaval of the kingdom, they respectfully upheld the Dhamma and Vinaya even in a time when it was difficult to do so.

Rājā abhayagiriṃ nāma vihāraṃ kāretvā attano katūpakārapubbassa mahātissattherassa adāsi. So pana thero kulasaṃsaggabahulattā mahāvihāravāsīhi bhikkhūhi pabbājanīyakammaṃ katvā nīhaṭo. Tadāssa sisso bahalamassutissanāmako thero taṃ kammaṃ paṭibāhi, tenassa saṅgho ukkhepanīyakammaṃ akāsi. So mahāvihāravāsīnaṃ kujjhitvā abhayagirivihārameva gantvā tena mahātissattherena ekato hutvā visuṃ gaṇaṃ vahanto vasi. Te ca dve therā na mahāvihāraṃ punāgamiṃsu mahāvaṃse 33, 79-gāthādīsu. nikāyasaṅgahe. Tato paṭṭhāya sīhaḷadīpe mahāvihāravāsī, abhayagirivāsīti dve nikāyājātā. Idaṃ tāva sīhaḷadīpe sāsanaparihāniyā paṭhamaṃ kāraṇaṃ.

The king, having had a monastery named Abhayagiri built, gave it to the Elder Mahātissa, who had previously rendered him service. However, because that elder associated excessively with families, the monks residing at the Mahāvihāra performed an act of expulsion against him and expelled him. At that time, his pupil, an elder named Bahalamassutissa, opposed that act; for this, the Saṅgha performed an act of suspension against him. Angered by the monks residing at the Mahāvihāra, he went to the Abhayagiri Monastery and, joining with that Elder Mahātissa, lived there leading a separate group. And those two elders never returned to the Mahāvihāra. From then on, in the island of Lanka, two Nikāyas arose: the Mahāvihāra dwellers and the Abhayagiri dwellers. This was the first cause of the decline of the Dispensation in the island of Lanka.

Dhammarucinikāyuppatti

The Origin of the Dhammaruci Nikāya

Tadā ca rājā abhayagirivāsīsuyeva bhikkhūsu visesato pasanno hutvā teyeva catūhi paccayehi pavāretvā paggaṇhāti, rājamahāmattādayopi abhiññātā abhiññātā bahū janā tasmiñca ārāme aññattha ca bahū āvāse katvā tesaṃ denti. Evaṃ abhayagirivāsino bhikkhū bahūnaṃ abhiññātajanānaṃ sakkatā ceva honti pūjitā ca mānitā ca. Puna ca abhayagirivāsino bahalamassutissattherādayoindiyaraṭṭhato āgataṃ vajjiputtakagaṇapariyāpannassa dhammarucinikāyassa dhammavinayabhūtaṃ sakkatabhāsāropitaṃ [M 0.0022] abhinavampi piṭakaṃ sampaṭicchanti mahāvaṃse 33, 99 gāthāsu. nikāyasaṅgahe, tena tepi dhammarucinikāyikā nāma ahesuṃ. Idaṃ sīhaḷadīpe sāsanaparihāniyā dutiyaṃ kāraṇaṃ.

At that time, the king, being especially pleased with only the monks dwelling at Abhayagiri, patronized them by inviting them to accept the four requisites. Likewise, royal ministers and other many prominent people built many residences in that monastery and elsewhere and gave them to them. Thus, the monks of Abhayagiri were respected, honored, and esteemed by many renowned people. Moreover, the Abhayagiri monks, led by the Elder Bahalamassutissa and others, also accepted a new Piṭaka that had come from India—the Dhamma and Vinaya of the Dhammaruci Nikāya, which belonged to the Vajjiputtaka group, rendered into the Sanskrit language. Thereby, they too became known as Dhammarucikas. This was the second cause of the decline of the Dispensation in the island of Lanka.

Piṭakattayassa potthakāropanaṃ

Committing the Tipiṭaka to Writing

Mahāvihāravāsino pana porāṇikaṃ pāḷibhāsāya saṇṭhitaṃ parisuddhapiṭakameva paṭiggaṇhanti, tañca mukhapāṭheneva dhārenti. Tadā pana therā pacchimajanānaṃ satipaññāhāniṃ disvā buddhakālato paṭṭhāya yāva taṃkālā mukhapāṭhenābhataṃ sāṭṭhakathaṃ piṭakattayaṃ potthake āropetuṃ samārabhiṃsu. Samārabhamānā ca te anurādharājadhānipurato aṭṭhasaṭṭhimilappamāṇe malayajanapade mātula mātale iti etarahi vohāro nagare ālokaleṇe vasantā ekassa tandesikassa janapadādhipatino ārakkhaṃ gahetvā taṃ potthakāropanakammamakaṃsu mahāvaṃse 33, 100-101-gāthāsu.
