Tenidaṃ ñāyati “tadā mahāvihāravāsino therā rājarājamahāmattehi aladdhūpakārā hutvā attano baleneva piṭakattayassa potthakāropanakammamakaṃsū”ti ca, “tañca yatheva pacchimajanānaṃ satipaññāhāniṃ disvā kataṃ, tatheva dubbhikkharaṭṭhakkhobhādibhayupaddutakālesu duddharabhāvampi disvā”ti ca, tathā “abhayagirivāsīnaṃ sampaṭicchitasamayantaravādehi anākulanatthampi kata”nti ca. Evaṃ mahāvihāravāsino therā parisuddhattheravādapiṭakaṃ samayantarehi asammissanatthāya yathā pure, tathā pāḷibhāsāya eva potthake āropetvāpi surakkhitaṃ rakkhiṃsu. Yadi hi tadā tepiṭakaṃ potthakesu anāropitamassa, pacchākālesu samayantarato āgatasuttāni “netāni amhāka”nti paṭikkhipituṃ na sukarāni bhaveyyuṃ. Yato ca kho tadā sāṭṭhakathaṃ tepiṭakaṃ potthakesu āropitaṃ, tatoyeva anāgatakālesu samayantarāgatasuttāni tehi potthakehi saṃsandetvā paṭikkhipituṃ sukarāni honti.

The monks of the Mahāvihāra, however, accepted only the ancient and pure Tipiṭaka established in the Pāli language, which they maintained through oral recitation alone. Observing the decline in mindfulness and wisdom of later generations, the elders then began the task of committing the Tipiṭaka along with its commentaries, which had been transmitted orally from the Buddha’s time up to that time, into books. While undertaking this task, they resided in the Aloka Cave in Mātula town in the Malaya country, located sixty-eight miles from the royal capital of Anurādhapura. There, under the protection of a local provincial chief, they carried out the work of committing the Tipiṭaka to writing. From this, it is evident that the Mahāvihāra elders, without receiving support from the king or royal ministers, accomplished the task of writing down the Tipiṭaka through their own efforts alone. Moreover, they did this not only foreseeing the decline in mindfulness and wisdom of later generations, but also seeing the difficulty of maintaining it in times afflicted by famine, state upheaval, and other dangers. Additionally, they acted for the sake of it not being confused with the differing sectarian views accepted by the Abhayagiri monks. Thus, the Mahāvihāra elders preserved the pure Theravāda Tipiṭaka well by committing it to writing in the Pāli language, just as before, for the sake of it not being mixed with other sectarian views. Had the Tipiṭaka not been committed to writing at that time, it would not have been easy in later ages to reject suttas coming from other sects, saying, 'These are not ours.' But because the Tipiṭaka along with its commentaries was written down then, in future times it was easy to compare suttas coming from other sects with those books and reject them.

Tathā hi bhātiyarājakāle (524-552-bu-va) mahāvihāravāsīnaṃ abhayagirivāsīhi vinaye vivādo uppajji. Tadā rājā dīghakārāyanaṃ nāma brāhmaṇajātikaṃ amaccaṃ therānaṃ santikaṃ pesesi. So ubhinnaṃ suttaṃ [M 0.0023] sutvā vinicchayaṃ adāsi pārā. aṭṭha. 2.384. Tathā vohārakatissarājakāle ca (758-780 bu-va) goṭhābhayarājakāle ca (797-810 bu-va) theravādikā potthakārūḷhena dhammavinayena saṃsandetvā adhammavādaṃ paṭikkhipiṃsu nikāyasaṅgahe 12-piṭṭhe.

For during the reign of King Bhātiya (524–552 B.E.), a dispute regarding the Vinaya arose between the residents of the Mahāvihāra and those of the Abhayagiri. Then the king sent a minister named Dīghakārāyana, who was of Brahmin birth, to the presence of the elders. Having heard the texts of both sides, he rendered a judgment. Likewise, during the reign of King Vohārakatissa (758–780 B.E.) and the reign of King Goṭhābhaya (797–810 B.E.), the Theravādins, by comparing with the Dhamma and Vinaya committed to writing, rejected the false doctrines.

Adhammavāduppatti

The Origin of False Doctrine

Ayaṃ pana ādito paṭṭhāya sāsanamalabhūtānaṃ adhammavādānaṃ uppatti. Asokarañño hi kāle uppabbājetvā nikkaḍḍhitā aññatitthiyā buddhasāsane aladdhapatiṭṭhā kodhābhibhūtā pāṭaliputtato nikkhamitvā rājagahasamīpe nālandāyaṃ sannipatitvā evaṃ sammantayiṃsu “mahājanassa buddhasāsane anavagāhatthāya sakyānaṃ dhammavinayo nāsetabbo, tañca kho tesaṃ samayaṃ ajānantehi na sakkā kātuṃ, tasmā yena kenaci upāyena punapi tattha pabbajitabbamevā”ti. Te evaṃ sammantayitvā puna āgantvā visuddhattheravādīnamantaraṃ pavisituṃ asakkontā tadaññesaṃ sattarasannaṃ mahāsaṅghikādinikāyānaṃ santikaṃ upasaṅkamitvā attano aññatitthiyabhāvaṃ ajānāpetvā pabbajitvā piṭakattayamuggaṇhitvā tañca viparivattetvā tato kosambiṃ gantvā dhammavinayanāsanāya upāyaṃ mantayitvā 253-buddhavasse chasu ṭhānesu vasantā (1) hemavatiko (2) rājagiriko (3) siddhatthiko (4) pubbaseliyo (5) aparaseliyo (6) vājiriyo (7) vetullo (8) andhako (9) aññamahāsaṅghikoti nava abhinave nikāye uppādesuṃ nikāyasaṅgahe 9-piṭṭhe. Tesaṃ nāmāni ca laddhiyo ca kathāvatthuaṭṭhakathāyaṃ āgatāyeva.

Now, this is the origin, from the very beginning, of the false doctrines which became stains upon the Dispensation. During the time of King Asoka, certain sectarians, having been expelled from the Saṅgha and cast out, unable to gain a foothold in the Buddha’s Dispensation, were overcome by anger. They left Pāṭaliputta and gathered near Rājagaha at Nālandā. There, they deliberated thus: 'To prevent the masses from deeply engaging with the Buddha’s Dispensation, the Dhamma-Vinaya of the Sakyans must be destroyed. But it is not possible to do that by those who do not know their doctrine and discipline. Therefore, by whatever means necessary, we must re-ordain there.' Having thus deliberated, they returned but could not infiltrate among the pure Theravādins. Instead, they approached seventeen other sects, such as the Mahāsaṅghikas, without making known their sectarian identity. After ordaining, they learned the Three Piṭakas and distorted them. Then, going to Kosambī, they devised a plan to destroy the Dhamma-Vinaya. In the 253rd year of the Buddhist Era, dwelling in six places, they gave rise to nine new sects: (1) Hemavatika, (2) Rājagirika, (3) Siddhatthika, (4) Pubbaseliya, (5) Aparaseliya, (6) Vājiriya, (7) Vetulla, (8) Andhaka, and (9) another Mahāsaṅghika. Their names and doctrines are recorded in the Kathāvatthu Commentary.

Tesu hemavatikā saddhammapatirūpakaṃ buddhabhāsitabhāvena dassetvā

Among them, the Hemavatikas, presenting a counterfeit of the true Dhamma as the word of the Buddha,

(1) Vaṇṇapiṭakaṃ nāma ganthaṃ akaṃsu.

(1) composed a book called the Vaṇṇapiṭaka.

Rājagirikā (2) aṅgulimālapiṭakaṃ,

The Rājagirikās (2) composed the Aṅgulimālapiṭaka,

Siddhatthikā (3) gūḷhavessantaraṃ,

The Siddhatthikās (3) composed the Gūḷhavessantara,

Pubbaseliyā (4) raṭṭhapālagajjitaṃ,

The Pubbaseliyās (4) composed the Raṭṭhapālagajjita,

Aparaseliyā (5) āḷavakagajjitaṃ,

The Aparaseliyās (5) composed the Āḷavakagajjita,

Vajirapabbatavāsino [M 0.0024] vājiriyā (6) gūḷhavinayaṃ nāma ganthaṃ akaṃsu.

The Vājiriyas, residents of Vajira Mountain, (6) composed a text called the Gūḷhavinaya.

Teyeva sabbe māyājālatanta-samājatantādike aneke tantaganthe ca, marīcikappa-herambhakappādike aneke kappaganthe ca akaṃsu.

All of them also composed many tantra texts such as the Māyājālatanta and the Samājatanta, and many kappa texts such as the Marīcikappa and the Herambhakappa.

Vetullavādino pana (7) vetullapiṭakamakaṃsu.

The Vetullavādins, however, (7) composed the Vetullapiṭaka.

Andhakā ca (8) ratanakūṭādike ganthe,

And the Andhakas (8) composed texts such as the Ratanakūṭa,

Aññamahāsaṅghikā ca (9) akkharasāriyādisuttante akaṃsu nikāyasaṅgahe 9-piṭṭhe.

And the other Mahāsaṅghikas (9) composed suttas such as the Akkharasāriya.

Tesu pana saddhammapatirūpakesu vetullavādo, vājiriyavādo, ratanakūṭasatthanti imāniyeva tīṇi laṅkādīpamupāgatāni, aññāni pana vaṇṇapiṭakādīni jambudīpeyeva nivattantīti nikāyasaṅgahe vuttaṃ. Vaṇṇapiṭakādīnampi pana laṅkādīpamupāgatacchāyā dissateva. Tathā hi samantapāsādikāya vinayaṭṭhakathāyaṃ (3, 9-piṭṭhe)

Among these counterfeits of the true Dhamma, only the Vetullavāda, Vājiriyavāda, and Ratanakūṭa scriptures reached the island of Laṅkā, while the others, such as the Vaṇṇapiṭaka, remained in Jambudīpa itself. This is stated in the Nikāyasaṅgaha. However, even traces of the Vaṇṇapiṭaka and others having reached the island of Laṅkā are seen. For instance, in the Samantapāsādikā, the Vinaya Commentary (3, p. 9):

“Vaṇṇapiṭaka aṅgulimālapiṭakaraṭṭhapālagajjitaāḷavakagajjitagūḷhamaggagūḷhavessantara gūḷhavinaya vedallapiṭakāni ettha “vepulla, vedallaṃ, vetullanti atthako ekaṃ, bodhisattapiṭakasseva nāma”nti veditabbaṃ. tathā hi vuttaṃ asaṅgena nāma ācariyena abhidhammasamuccaye nāma mahāyānikagante (79-piṭṭhe) “vepullaṃ katamaṃ? bodhisattapiṭakasampayuttaṃ bhāsitaṃ. yaduccate vepullaṃ, taṃ vedallamapyuccate, vetullamapyuccate. kimatthaṃ vepullamuccate? sabbasattānaṃ hitasukhādhiṭṭhānato, udāragambhīradhammadesanāto ca. kimatthamuccate vedallaṃ? sabbāvaraṇavidalanato. kimatthamuccate vetullaṃ? upamānadhammānaṃ tulanābhāvato”ti pana abuddhavacanāniyevāti vutta”nti ca.

'The Vaṇṇapiṭaka, Aṅgulimālapiṭaka, Raṭṭhapālagajjita, Āḷavakagajjita, Gūḷhamagga, Gūḷhavessantara, Gūḷhavinaya, and Vedallapiṭakas are indeed said to be not the words of the Buddha in the ancient commentaries.'

Sāratthappakāsiniyā saṃyuttaṭṭhakathāyampi (2, 186-piṭṭhe)

Also in the Sāratthappakāsinī, the Saṃyutta Commentary (2, p. 186):

“Gūḷhavinayaṃ gūḷhavessantaraṃ gūḷhamahosadhaṃ vaṇṇapiṭakaṃ aṅgulimālapiṭakaṃ raṭṭhapālagajjitaṃ āḷavakagajjitaṃ vedallapiṭakanti abuddhavacanaṃ saddhammapatirūpakaṃ nāmā”ti ca–

'The Gūḷhavinaya, Gūḷhavessantara, Gūḷhamahosadha, Vaṇṇapiṭaka, Aṅgulimālapiṭaka, Raṭṭhapālagajjita, Āḷavakagajjita, and Vedallapiṭaka are called not the word of the Buddha, counterfeits of the true Dhamma.'

Tesaṃ paṭikkhepo dissati. Na hi tāni asutvā, tesañca atthaṃ ajānitvā sīhaḷaṭṭhakathācariyehi tāni paṭikkhipituṃ sakkā, nāpi taṃ paṭikkhepavacanaṃ jambudīpikaṭṭhakathācariyānaṃ vacanaṃ bhavituṃ, mahāmahindattherassa sīhaḷadīpaṃ [M 0.0025] gamanasamaye tesaṃyeva abhāvato. Tasmā tāni ca tadaññāni ca mahāyānikapiṭakāni taṃkālikāni yebhuyyena sīhaḷadīpamupāgatānīti gahetabbāni. Tesu ca vajjiputtakagaṇapariyāpannassa dhammarucinikāyassa piṭakānaṃ tadupāgamanaṃ pubbeva vuttaṃ. Tadaññesaṃ pana tadupāgamanaṃ evaṃ veditabbaṃ.

Their rejection is evident. For it was not possible for the Sinhalese commentary teachers to reject them without hearing them or understanding their meaning, nor could such a rejection be the words of the mainland commentary teachers, since those texts were not present at the time of the great elder Mahāmahinda’s journey to the island of Laṅkā. Therefore, these and other Mahāyāna Piṭakas of that time must be understood to have mostly reached the island of Laṅkā. And the arrival of the scriptures of the Dhammarucinikāya, belonging to the Vajjiputtaka group, has already been mentioned. However, the arrival of the others should be understood as follows.

Vetullavādassa paṭhamaniggaho

The First Suppression of the Vetulla Doctrine

Vohārakatissarañño kāle (758-780-bu-va) abhayagirivāsino dhammarucinikāyikā pubbe vuttappakārena sāsanavināsanatthāya bhikkhuvesadhārīhi vetullavādibrāhmaṇehi racitaṃ vetullapiṭakaṃ sampaṭiggahetvā “idaṃ buddhabhāsita”nti dassenti. Taṃ mahāvihāravāsino theravādikā dhammavinayena saṃsandetvā adhammavādoti paṭikkhipiṃsu. Taṃ sutvā rājā sabbasatthapāraguṃ kapilaṃ nāma amaccaṃ pesetvā vinicchayaṃ kārāpetvā abuddhabhāsitabhāvaṃ ñatvā sabbaṃ vetullapotthakaṃ jhāpetvā talladdhike ca pāpabhikkhū niggahetvā buddhasāsanaṃ jotesi nikāyasaṅgahe 12-piṭṭhe. Vuttañhetaṃ mahāvaṃse –

During the reign of King Vohārakatissa (758–780 B.E.), the Dhammarucinikāya adherents residing in Abhayagiri, in the manner previously stated, accepted the Vetulla Piṭaka, which was composed by Vetullavāda brahmins disguised as monks with the intention of destroying the Dispensation, and presented it as "the word of the Buddha." The Theravādins of the Mahāvihāra, comparing it with the Dhamma and Vinaya, rejected it as false doctrine. Hearing this, the king sent his minister Kapila, who was well-versed in all the scriptures, to investigate and decide the matter. Upon determining that it was not the Buddha’s word, he had all the Vetulla books burned and suppressed the wicked monks who adhered to it, thus illuminating the Buddha’s Dispensation. As stated in the Mahāvaṃsa:

36-41.

36-41.

“Vetullavādaṃ madditvā, kāretvā pāpaniggahaṃ;

Kapilena amaccena, sāsanaṃ jotayī ca so”ti.

'Having suppressed the Vetullavāda and carried out the suppression of the wicked, through the minister Kapila, he illuminated the Dispensation.'

Sāgaliyanikāyuppatti

The Origin of the Sāgaliya Sect

Punapi te abhayagirivāsino goṭhābhayarañño kāle (797-810-bu-va) vetullavādaṃ tatheva dassenti. Tadā pana tesu ussiliyātisso nāma mahāthero vohārakatissarājakāle vetullavādīnaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ kataniggahaṃ sutvā “vicāraṇasampannassa rañño samaye tatheva bhaveyya, na bhaddakameta”nti cintetvā “na mayaṃ tehi ekato homā”ti tisatamatte bhikkhū gahetvā dakkhiṇagirivihāraṃ gantvā dhammarucinikāyato visuṃ hutvā vasi. Tesu sāgalo nāma mahāthero tattheva dakkhiṇagirimhi vasanto āgamabyākhyānamakāsi. Tato paṭṭhāya taṃ theramārabbha tassantevāsino sāgaliyā nāma ahesuṃ. Tesampi [M 0.0026] vādo pacchā mahāsenarājakāle jetavanavihāre patthari nikāya 13-piṭṭhe.

Moreover, during the reign of King Goṭhābhaya (797–810 B.E.), those residents of the Abhayagiri Monastery continued to promote the Vetulla doctrine just as before. At that time, a great elder named Ussiliyātissa among them, having heard of the suppression of the Vetulla-adherent monks during the reign of King Vohārakatissa, thought: 'Such a thing might likewise happen in the time of a king endowed with discernment; this is not good.' Thinking, 'We shall not be one with them,' he took about three hundred monks and went to the Dakkhiṇagiri Monastery, living separately from the Dhammaruci sect. Among them, a great elder named Sāgala, residing right there at Dakkhiṇagiri, composed the Āgama-byākhyāna (Exposition of the Āgamas). From that time onwards, his disciples became known as the Sāgaliyas, named after that elder. Their doctrine later spread at the Jetavana Monastery during the reign of King Mahāsena.

Vetullavādassa dutiyaniggaho

The Second Suppression of the Vetulla Doctrine

Goṭhābhayo pana rājā pañcasu mahāvihāra, cetiya, thūpārāma, issarasamaṇaka, vessagirivihārasaṅkhātesu vihāresu mahābhikkhusaṅghaṃ ekato sannipātetvā taṃ pavattiṃ pucchitvā vetullavādassa abuddhabhāsitabhāvaṃ ñatvā taṃvādino saṭṭhi pāpabhikkhū lakkhaṇāhate katvā raṭṭhato pabbājesi, vetullapotthakāni ca jhāpetvā buddhasāsanaṃ jotesi mahāvaṃse 36, 111-112-gāthāsu, nikāya 13-piṭṭhe.

King Goṭhābhaya, however, having assembled the great Saṅgha of monks from the five large monasteries, inquired into the matter. Upon learning that the Vetulla doctrine was not the word of the Buddha, he had sixty wicked monks who held that view branded and banished from the kingdom; he also had the Vetulla books burned, thus illuminating the Buddha's Dispensation.

Tadā raṭṭhato pabbājitesu tesu bhikkhūsu keci kāvīrapaṭṭanaṃ gantvā tattha vasanti. Tasmiñca samaye eko aññatitthiyamāṇavako desantarato kāvīramāgantvā paṭṭanagāmikehi tesaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ katūpahāraṃ disvā lābhasakkāraṃ nissāya tesaṃ santike pabbajitvā saṅghamittoti nāmena pākaṭo ahosi. So mahāvihāravāsīnaṃ dhammavinicchayaṃ nissāya goṭhābhayaraññā vetullavādahetu tesaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ raṭṭhā pabbājitabhāvaṃ ñatvā mahāvihāravāsīnaṃ kuddho hutvā “vetullavādaṃ vā ne gāhāpessāmi, vihāre vā nesaṃ ummūletvā vināsessāmī”ti sīhaḷadīpaṃ gantvā rājānaṃ pasādetvā tassa dve putte sippaṃ sikkhāpessāmīti ārabhi. Tathāpi attano vādassa jānanasamatthaṃ jeṭṭhatissaṃ ohāya anāgate attano vacanaṃ kārāpetuṃ sakkuṇeyyaṃ kaniṭṭhaṃ mahāsenakumārameva saṅgaṇhitvā sippaṃ sikkhāpesi. Vituno accayena jeṭṭhatissakumāre rajjaṃ patte (810-819-bu-va) so tassa rañño bhīto kāvīrapaṭṭanameva gato mahāvaṃse 36, 113-gāthādīsu, nikāya 14-piṭṭhe.

Among those monks banished from the kingdom, some went to the port of Kāvīra and lived there. At that time, a young man of another sect came to Kāvīra from another region. Seeing the offerings made by the port-townspeople to those monks, he, for the sake of gain and honor, ordained in their presence and became famous by the name Saṅghamitta. Having learned that those monks had been banished from the kingdom by King Goṭhābhaya for the sake of the Vetulla doctrine, based on a legal decision of the Mahāvihāra residents, he became angry with the Mahāvihāra residents, vowing: 'I will either make them accept the Vetulla doctrine, or I will uproot and destroy their monastery.' He then went to the island of Lanka, won the king's favor, and began to teach his two sons the arts. Nevertheless, leaving aside the elder prince, Jeṭṭhatissa, who was capable of discerning his doctrine, he supported and taught the arts only to the younger prince, Mahāsena, who would be compliant with his words in the future. Upon the death of the father, when Prince Jeṭṭhatissa attained the kingship (810–819 B.E.), Saṅghamitta, fearing that king, returned to the port of Kāvīra.

Mahāsenarañño pana kāle (819-845-bu-va) so puna sīhaḷadīpamāgantvā abhayagirivihāre vasanto mahāvihāravāsīhi vetullavādaṃ gāhāpetuṃ nānāpakārehi vāyāmamakāsi. Tathāpi tehi taṃ gāhāpetuṃ asakkonto rājānaṃ upasaṅkamitvā nānākāraṇehi saññāpetvā [M 0.0027] “yo koci ekassapi bhikkhussa mahāvihāravāsino āhāraṃ dadeyya, tassa sataṃ daṇḍo”ti rañño āṇāya nagare bheriṃ carāpesi. Tadā mahāvihāravāsino nagare piṇḍāya carantā tayo divase bhikkhamaladdhā mahāpāsāde sannipatitvā “sace mayaṃ khudāhetu adhammaṃ dhammoti gaṇheyyāma, bahū janā taṃ gahetvā apāyagāmino bhavissanti, mayañca sabbe sāvajjā bhavissāma, tasmā na mayaṃ jīvitahetupi vetullavādaṃ paṭiggaṇhissāmā”ti sammantayitvā mahāvihārādike sabbavihāre chaḍḍetvā rohaṇajanapadañca malayapadesañca agamiṃsu mahāvaṃse 37, 2-6-gāthāsu. nikāyasaṅgahe 14-piṭṭhe.

During the time of King Mahāsena (819–845 B.E.), however, he returned to the island of Lanka and, staying at the Abhayagiri Monastery, made various efforts to persuade the Mahāvihāra residents to accept the Vetulla doctrine. Even so, being unable to make them accept it, he approached the king and, having convinced him through various reasons, had the drum beaten in the city by the king's command, proclaiming: 'Whoever should give food to even a single monk who is a resident of the Mahāvihāra, for him there is a fine of one hundred.' Then the Mahāvihāra residents, wandering for alms in the city, received no alms for three days. Gathering at the Great Edifice (Lohapāsāda), they deliberated: 'If we, on account of hunger, should take what is not Dhamma as Dhamma, many people, following that, would go to the plane of misery, and we all shall be blameworthy. Therefore, we will not accept the Vetulla doctrine even for the sake of our lives.' Having thus decided, they abandoned all the monasteries, including the Mahāvihāra, and departed for the Rohaṇa district and the Malaya region.

Vetullavādo

The Vetulla Doctrine

Kīdiso vetullavādo nāma, yato mahāvihāravāsino ativiya jigucchiṃsūti? Idāni vetullavādassa sarūpaṃ sabbākārena pakāsetuṃ na sakkā, vetullanāmena potthakānaṃ vā nikāyassa vā etarahi apākaṭabhāvato. Abhidhammapiṭake pana kathāvatthuaṭṭhakathāyaṃ kathā. aṭṭha. 793-794 ādayo katipayā vetullavādā āgatā. Kathaṃ? –

What sort of thing is this so-called Vetulla doctrine, which the residents of the Mahāvihāra so greatly loathed? At present, it is not possible to fully declare the nature of the Vetulla doctrine in all its aspects, because books or a sect by the name of 'Vetulla' are no longer well-known. However, in the Commentary on the Kathāvatthu of the Abhidhamma Piṭaka, some Vetulla doctrines are mentioned. How? —

“Paramatthato maggaphalāneva saṅgho, maggaphalehi añño saṅgho nāma natthi, maggaphalāni ca na kiñci paṭiggaṇhanti, tasmā na vattabbaṃ saṅgho dakkhiṇaṃ paṭiggaṇhātī”ti ca (1).

"In the ultimate sense, the Saṅgha is only the paths and fruits; there is no Saṅgha apart from the paths and fruits. And the paths and fruits do not receive anything; therefore, it should not be said that the Saṅgha receives a worthy gift." (1)

“Maggaphalāneva saṅgho nāma, na ca tāni dakkhiṇaṃ visodhetuṃ sakkonti, tasmā na vattabbaṃ saṅgho dakkhiṇaṃ visodhetī”ti ca (2).

"The Saṅgha is only the paths and fruits, and these are not able to purify a worthy gift. Therefore, it should not be said that the Saṅgha purifies a worthy gift." (2)

“Maggaphalāneva saṅgho nāma, na ca tāni kiñci bhuñjanti, tasmā na vattabbaṃ saṅgho bhuñjati pivati khādati sāyatī”ti ca (3).

"The Saṅgha is merely the paths and fruits, and these do not consume anything. Therefore, it should not be said: 'The Saṅgha eats, drinks, chews, or savors.'" (3)

Maggaphalāneva saṅgho nāma, na ca sakkā tesaṃ kiñci dātuṃ, na ca tehi paṭiggaṇhituṃ, nāpi tesaṃ dānena koci upakāro ijjhati, tasmā na vattabbaṃ saṅghassa dinnaṃ mahapphala”nti ca (4).

"The Saṅgha is merely the paths and fruits; it is not possible to give anything to them, nor can they receive anything, nor is any benefit accomplished by giving to them. Therefore, it should not be said that what is given to the Saṅgha is of great fruit." (4)

“Buddho bhagavā na kiñci paribhuñjati, lokānuvattanatthaṃ pana paribhuñjamānaṃ viya attānaṃ dasseti, tasmā nirupakārattā na vattabbaṃ tasmiṃ dinnaṃ mahapphala”nti ca (5).

"The Buddha, the Blessed One, consumes nothing, but for the sake of conforming to the world, he shows himself as if consuming. Therefore, because no benefit is derived [from him], it should not be said that what is given to the Buddha is of great fruit." (5)

“Bhagavā [M 0.0028] tusitabhavane nibbatto tattheva vasati, na manussalokaṃ āgacchati, nimmitarūpamattakaṃ panettha dassetī”ti ca (6).

"The Blessed One was reborn in the Tusita heaven and dwells right there; he does not come to the human world, but here he only shows a created form." (6)

“Tusitapure ṭhito bhagavā dhammadesanatthāya abhinimmitaṃ pesesi, tena ceva, tassa ca desanaṃ sampaṭicchitvā āyasmatā ānandena dhammo desito, na buddhena bhagavatā”ti ca (7).

"The Blessed One, staying in the Tusita city, sent forth a specially created image for the purpose of teaching the Dhamma. By that image and by the Venerable Ānanda, who received the teaching from it, was the Dhamma taught, and not by the Buddha, the Blessed One." (7)

“Ekādhippāyena methuno dhammo paṭisevitabbo. Ayaṃ panettha attho – kāruññena vā ekena adhippāyena ekādhippāyo, saṃsāre vā ekato bhavissāmāti itthiyā saddhiṃ buddhapūjādīni katvā paṇidhivasena eko adhippāyo assāti ekādhippāyo, evarūpo dvinnampi janānaṃ ekādhippāyo methuno dhammo paṭisevitabbo”ti ca (8) evaṃ vetullavādīnaṃ laddhiyo āgatā, ettakāyeva nesaṃ vādā theravādaganthavasena dāni paññāyanti.

"Sexual intercourse should be engaged in with a single intention. Here the meaning is this: 'single intention' (ekādhippāya) might be an intention of compassion. Or, it might be the single intention that arises from making an aspiration together with a woman—after performing such acts as venerating the Buddha—with the thought, 'May we be together in saṃsāra.' Sexual intercourse based on such a single intention on the part of both persons is to be engaged in." (8) Such are the tenets of the Vetullavādins that have come down, and only this many of their doctrines are now known from the Theravāda texts.

Ettha ca ādito catūhi vādehi suttantāgatasaṅgho ca micchā gahito, vinayāgatasaṅgho ca sabbathā paṭikkhitto. Tadanantaraṃ tayo vādā issaranimmānavādānuvattakā. Antimassa pana asaddhammavādabhāvo ativiya pākaṭoti.

Here, by the first four theses, the concept of the Saṅgha found in the Suttas is wrongly grasped, and the concept of the Saṅgha found in the Vinaya is completely rejected. The next three theses are followers of a doctrine of creation by a supreme lord. As for the last thesis, its nature as a false doctrine is exceedingly evident.

Abhidhammasamuccaye pana vetullapiṭakassa bodhisattapiṭakabhāvo pakāsito, tasmā saddhammapuṇḍarikasuttādike bodhisattapiṭake āgatavādopi “vetullavādo”ti veditabbo abhidhammasamuccaye 79-piṭṭhe.

In the Abhidhammasamuccaya, however, it is declared that the Vetullapiṭaka is the Bodhisattapiṭaka. Therefore, the doctrines found in the Bodhisattapiṭaka, such as in the Saddhammapuṇḍarika Sutta and others, should also be understood as 'Vetullavāda'.

Mahāvihāranāsanaṃ

The Destruction of the Mahāvihāra

Mahāvihāravāsīsu pana vuttappakārena sabbavihāre chaḍḍetvā gatesu saṅghamitto pāpabhikkhu rājānaṃ saññāpetvā lohapāsādādike catusaṭṭhyādhike tisatamatte pariveṇapāsāde nāsetvā samūlaṃ uddharāpetvā abhayagirivihāraṃ ānayāpesi. Vihārabhūmiyañca kasāpetvā aparaṇṇe vapāpesi. Evaṃ tadā mahāvihāro nava vassāni bhikkhūhi suñño ahosi āvāsavirahito ca. Atha rājā meghavaṇṇābhayassa nāma kalyāṇamittabhūtassa amaccassa santajjanapubbaṅgamena vacanena [M 0.0029] mahāvihāraṃ puna pākatikaṃ katvā te cāpi apakkante bhikkhū ānetvā catūhi paccayehi upaṭṭhahi mahāvaṃse 37-30-gāthāsu. nikāyasaṅgahe 14-15-piṭṭhesu.

Now, when the residents of the Mahāvihāra had abandoned all the monasteries in the manner described and departed, the evil monk Saṅghamitta, having persuaded the king, had the three hundred and sixty-four monastic residences, including the Lohapāsāda, destroyed. Having had them completely uprooted, he had the materials brought to the Abhayagiri Monastery. He also had the monastery grounds plowed and sown with beans. Thus, for nine years the Mahāvihāra was empty of monks and devoid of dwellings. Then the king, on the advice—preceded by a threat—of his minister and good friend named Meghavaṇṇābhaya, restored the Mahāvihāra to its original condition. He also brought back those monks who had departed and supported them with the four requisites.

Jetavanavāsinikāyuppatti

The Origin of the Jetavanavāsin Nikāya

Punapi rājā dakkhiṇārāmavāsimhi jimhamānase kuhakatissatthere pasanno hutvā tassatthāya mahāvihārasīmabbhantare jotivanuyyāne jetavanavihāraṃ kāretumārabhi. Mahāvihāravāsino bhikkhū taṃ nivāretuṃ asakkontā punapi tato apakkamiṃsu. Tadāpi mahāvihāro nava māsāni bhikkhūhi suñño ahosi. Rājā pana attano ajjhāsayavaseneva tattha jetavanavihāraṃ kāretvā tassa kuhakatissattherassa adāsiyeva. Tattha dakkhiṇagirivihārato sāgaliyā bhikkhū āgantvā vasiṃsu. Pacchā ca te ambasāmaṇerasilākālarañño kāle (1067-1080-bu-va) vetullavādino ahesuṃ mahāvaṃse 37, 32-gāthādīsu, nikāyasaṅgahe 15-piṭṭhe.

Again the king, pleased with the fraudulent, crooked-minded Elder Tissa, a resident of the Dakkhiṇārāma, began to have the Jetavana Monastery built for him in the Jotivana park, within the boundary of the Mahāvihāra. The monks of the Mahāvihāra, unable to prevent this, again departed from there. At that time too, the Mahāvihāra was empty of monks for nine months. The king, however, following his own inclination, had the Jetavana Monastery built there and gave it to that fraudulent Elder Tissa. Monks of the Sāgaliya school from the Dakkhiṇagiri Monastery came and resided there. Later, during the time of King Ambasāmaṇera Silākāla (1067–1080 B.E.), they became adherents of the Vetullavāda.

Evaṃ ācariyabuddhaghosattherassa sīhaḷadīpamāgamanakālato (965-bu-va) pubbeyeva visuddhattheravādīhi mahāvihāravāsīhi viruddhasamayā abhayagirivāsino (455-bu-va) sāgaliyā (797-810-bu-va) jetavanavāsino (829-845-bu-va) cāti tayo nikāyā uppannā ahesuṃ. Tesu pana abhayagirivāsinoyeva visesato pākaṭā ceva honti balavanto ca. Tathā hi te visuddhattheravādapiṭakañca vajjiputtakapariyāpannadhammarucinikāyapiṭakañca mahisāsakādinikāyapiṭakañca mahāyānapiṭakañca sampaṭicchanti. Tesu dhammarucinikāyapiṭakassa sampaṭicchitabhāvo pākaṭoyeva. Mahisāsakādinikāyapiṭakassa sampaṭicchitabhāvo pana phāhiyannāmassa cinabhikkhuno addhānakkamasallakkhaṇakathāya ceva aṭṭhakathāsu paṭikkhittavaṇṇapiṭakādināmavasena ca veditabbo, tathā mahāyānapiṭakassa sampaṭicchitabhāvopi.

Thus, even before the arrival of the Elder Ācariya Buddhaghosa on the island of Sīhaḷa (965 B.E.), three nikāyas had arisen with doctrines opposed to the pure Theravādins of the Mahāvihāra: the Abhayagirivāsins (455 B.E.), the Sāgaliyas (797–810 B.E.), and the Jetavanavāsins (829–845 B.E.). Among them, the Abhayagirivāsins were especially prominent and powerful. For they accepted the pure Theravāda Piṭaka, the Piṭaka of the Dhammaruci Nikāya which is included among the Vajjiputtakas, the Piṭakas of the Mahīsāsaka and other nikāyas, and the Mahāyāna Piṭaka. Among these, their acceptance of the Dhammaruci Nikāya Piṭaka is quite evident. Their acceptance of the Piṭakas of the Mahīsāsaka and other nikāyas, however, is to be understood from the travel account of the Chinese monk named Faxian, and from the names of such works as the Vaṇṇa Piṭaka, which are rejected in the commentaries; likewise their acceptance of the Mahāyāna Piṭaka.

Phāhiyamaddhānakkamakathā

The Travel Account of Faxian

Phāhiyannāmena hi cinabhikkhunā 956-buddhavasse sīhaḷadīpato sakkatabhāsāropitaṃ mahisāsakavinayapiṭakañca dīghāgamo ca saṃyuttāgamo ca sannipātapiṭakañca attanā saha cinaraṭṭhamānītanti tassa addhānakkamakathāyaṃ [M 0.0030] dassitaṃ. Tañca sabbaṃ abhayagirivihāratoyeva laddhamassa, mahāvihāravāsīnaṃ sakkatāropitapiṭakābhāvato. Aṭṭhakathāyaṃ paṭikkhittavaṇṇapiṭakādīni ca tattheva bhaveyyuṃ, mahāvihāravāsīhi tesaṃ appaṭiggahitabhāvato. Tathā “phāhiyambhikkhussa sīhaḷadīpe paṭivasanakāle (954-956-bu-va) mahāvihāre tisahassamattā bhikkhū vasanti, te theravādapiṭakameva uggaṇhanti, na mahāyānapiṭakaṃ. Abhayagirivihāre pañcasahassamattā bhikkhū vasanti, te pana dvepi piṭakāni uggaṇhanti mahāyānapiṭakañceva theravādapiṭakañcā”ti ca teneva cinabhikkhunā dassitaṃ.

For it is shown in his travel account that the Chinese monk named Faxian, in the Buddhist year 956, brought with him to China from the island of Sīhaḷa the Mahīsāsaka Vinaya Piṭaka, the Dīrgha Āgama, the Saṃyukta Āgama, and the Saṃnipāta Piṭaka, which had been rendered into the Sanskrit language. All this must have been obtained by him from the Abhayagiri Monastery, as the residents of the Mahāvihāra did not possess Piṭakas rendered into Sanskrit. The Vaṇṇa Piṭaka and other works rejected in the commentaries would also have been there, since they were not accepted by the residents of the Mahāvihāra. Similarly, it was reported by that same Chinese monk: 'During the monk Faxian's residence on the island of Sīhaḷa (954–956 B.E.), about three thousand monks reside in the Mahāvihāra; they study only the Theravāda Piṭaka, not the Mahāyāna Piṭaka. In the Abhayagiri Monastery about five thousand monks reside; they, however, study both Piṭakas, the Mahāyāna Piṭaka and the Theravāda Piṭaka.'

Yasmā pana abhayagirivāsino mahāyānapiṭakampi uggaṇhanti, tasmā tasmiṃ vihāre mahāyānikānaṃ padhānācariyabhūtehi assaghosanāgajjunehi kataganthāpi saṃvijjamānāyeva bhaveyyuṃ, tatoyeva tesaṃ nayañca nāmañca ācariyabuddhaghosattheropi aññepi taṃkālikā mahāvihāravāsino sutasampannā therā jāneyyuṃyeva. Apica dakkhiṇaindiyaraṭṭhe samuddasamīpe guntājanapade nāgārajunakoṇḍaṃ nāma ṭhānamatthi, yattha nāgajjuno mahāyānikānaṃ padhānācariyabhūto vasanto buddhasāsanaṃ patiṭṭhāpesi. Ācariyabuddhaghosassa ca tandesikabhāvanimittaṃ dissati, taṃ pacchato (33-piṭṭhe) āvibhavissati. Tasmāpi ācariyabuddhaghosatthero nāgajjunassa ca assaghosassa ca nayañca nāmañca jāneyyayevāti sakkā anuminituṃ.

And since the residents of Abhayagiri also study the Mahāyāna Piṭaka, it is likely that texts composed by Aśvaghoṣa and Nāgārjuna, the principal teachers of the Mahāyānists, were available in that monastery. For that very reason, the Elder Ācariya Buddhaghosa and other learned elders of that time residing at the Mahāvihāra would surely have known their doctrines and their names. Moreover, in South India, near the sea, in the Guntur district, there is a place called Nāgārjunakoṇḍa, where Nāgārjuna, the principal teacher of the Mahāyānists, lived and established the Buddha's Dispensation. A sign is seen indicating that Ācariya Buddhaghosa was from that country, which will become clear later (on page 33). Therefore, it can be inferred that the Elder Ācariya Buddhaghosa would have known both the doctrines and the names of Nāgārjuna and Aśvaghoṣa.

Jānatoyeva pana tesaṃ nayassa vā nāmassa vā attano aṭṭhakathāyamappakāsanaṃ tesaṃ nikāyantarabhāvatoyevassa. Tathā hi tesaṃ assaghosanāgajjunānaṃ assaghoso (570-670-buddhavassabbhantare) theravādato bhinnesu ekādasasu gaṇesu sabbatthivādagaṇe pariyāpanno, nāgajjuno ca mahāsaṅghika-cetiyavādigaṇādīhi jāte mahāyānanikāye pariyāpanno, [M 0.0031] mahāvihāravāsino ca āditoyeva paṭṭhāya nikāyantarasamayehi asammissanatthaṃ attano piṭakaṃ atīva ādaraṃ katvā rakkhanti, ayañca ācariyabuddhaghoso tesamaññataro. Vuttañhi tassa ganthanigamanesu “mahāvihāravāsīnaṃ vaṃsālaṅkārabhūtenā”ti. Tasmā “ācariyabuddhaghoso tesaṃ nayaṃ jānantoyeva attano ganthesu nikāyantarasamayehi asammissanatthaṃ nappakāsesī”ti veditabbaṃ.

However, his not mentioning their doctrine or name in his own commentaries, though he knew them, must have been because they belonged to other nikāyas. For of Aśvaghoṣa and Nāgārjuna, Aśvaghoṣa was included in the Sarvāstivāda school, one of the eleven schools that split from the Theravāda, while Nāgārjuna was included in the Mahāyāna nikāya, which arose from the Mahāsaṅghika, Cetiyavāda, and other schools. The residents of the Mahāvihāra, from the very beginning, guarded their own Piṭaka with great care so as not to mix it with the doctrines of other nikāyas, and this Ācariya Buddhaghosa was one of them. For it is stated in the colophons of his works: 'by him who was an ornament to the lineage of the Mahāvihāra residents.' Therefore, it should be understood that Ācariya Buddhaghosa, though knowing their doctrine, did not mention it in his own works in order not to mix them with the doctrines of other nikāyas.

Ettāvatā ca yāni “bodhimaṇḍasamīpamhi, jāto brāhmaṇamāṇavo”tiādinā vuttassa mahāvaṃsavacanassa vicāraṇamukhena ācariyabuddhaghosassa vambhanavacanāni dhammānandakosambinā vuttāni, tāni amūlakabhāvena anuvicāritāni. Tathāpi “ācariyabuddhaghoso bodhimaṇḍasamīpe jāto”ti etaṃ pana atthaṃ sādhetuṃ daḷhakāraṇaṃ na dissateva ṭhapetvā taṃ mahāvaṃsavacanaṃ, yampi buddhaghosuppattiyaṃ vuttaṃ, tampi mahāvaṃsameva nissāya vuttavacanattā na daḷhakāraṇaṃ hotīti.

Thus far, the disparaging words about Ācariya Buddhaghosa spoken by Dhammānanda Kosambi, by way of an investigation into the Mahāvaṃsa statement beginning, 'A brahmin youth born near the Bodhimaṇḍa,' have been examined and found to be baseless. Nevertheless, to establish the point that 'Ācariya Buddhaghosa was born near the Bodhimaṇḍa,' no strong reason is seen apart from that Mahāvaṃsa statement. And what is said in the Buddhaghosuppatti, since it is a statement made in reliance on the Mahāvaṃsa itself, does not constitute a strong reason.

Marammaraṭṭhikabhāvakathā

The Account of His Being a Native of the Maramma Country

Ekacce pana marammaraṭṭhikā “ācariyabuddhaghoso marammaraṭṭhe sathuṃ nāma nagarato sīhaḷadīpaṃ gantvā saṅgahaṭṭhakathāyo akāsī”ti vadanti. Taṃ dhammānandena anujānitvā “tampi thokaṃ yuttisampannaṃ, ahaṃ evaṃ saddahāmi ‘buddhaghoso dakkhiṇaindiyaraṭṭhe telaṅgajātiko’ti, telaṅgajātikā ca bahū janā marammaraṭṭhe ca indocina raṭṭhe ca gantvā vasanti, talhiṅa? Iti vohāro ca tatoyeva telaṅgapadato uppanno. Tathā ‘buddhaghoso aṭṭhakathāyo katvā sīhaḷadīpato marammaraṭṭhaṃ gantvā pacchimabhāge tattheva vasī’tipi gahetuṃ sakkā, tassa hi ganthā marammaraṭṭhe sīhaḷaraṭṭhatopi surakkhitatarā hontī”ti ca vatvā patiṭṭhāpitaṃ.

However, some natives of the Maramma country say: “The Teacher Buddhaghosa went from the city named Sathuṃ in the Maramma country to the island of Sīhaḷa and composed the Saṅgaha commentaries.” Conceding this, Dhammānanda stated: “That too is somewhat reasonable. I believe thus: ‘Buddhaghosa was of Telugu origin in the South Indian country.’ And many people of Telugu origin go and settle in the Maramma country and in the Indochina country. Has not the term ‘Talhiṅa’ arisen from that very word ‘Telaṅga’? Similarly, it can also be accepted that ‘Buddhaghosa, after composing the commentaries, went from the island of Sīhaḷa to the Maramma country and dwelt there in the latter part of his life.’ For his books are even better preserved in the Maramma country than in the country of Sīhaḷa.” Thus it was established.

Dakkhiṇaindiyaraṭṭhikabhāvayutti

The Argument for His Being a Native of South India

Bahū pana ādhunikā vicakkhaṇā dhammānandādayo “ācariyabuddhaghosatthero dakkhiṇaindiyaraṭṭhiko”ti vadanti. Ayaṃ panettha yutti, yebhuyyena hi aṭṭhakathāṭīkākārā therā dakkhiṇaindiyaraṭṭhikāyeva. Tathā [M 0.0032] hi buddhavaṃsaṭṭhakathāya ca abhidhammāvatāraṭṭhakathāya ca vinayavinicchayaṭṭhakathāya ca kārako ācariyabuddhadattatthero coḷaraṭṭhe tambapaṇṇinadiyaṃ uraganagare jāto ācariyabuddhaghosena ekakāliko ca. Paramatthavinicchaya-nāmarūpapariccheda-abhidhammatthasaṅgahānaṃ kārako ācariyaanuruddhatthero ekacce pana vadanti-paramatthavinicchayakārako eko, nāmarūpaparicchedaabhidhammatthasaṅgahānaṃ kārako ekoti dve anuruddhattherāti kañcivararaṭṭhe kāverinagarajātiko. Khuddakanikāyapariyāpannaudānādipāḷiyā saṃvaṇṇanābhūtāya paramatthadīpaniyā kārako ācariyadhammapālattheropi dakkhiṇaindiyaraṭṭhe kañcipurajātiko. Tathevāyampīti veditabbo. Vuttañhi manorathapūraṇiyā nāma aṅguttaraṭṭhakathāya nigamane –

However, many modern discerning scholars such as Dhammānanda say: “The Venerable Teacher Buddhaghosa was from South India.” This is the reasoning here: for the most part, the elder-authors of commentaries and subcommentaries were indeed from South India. For instance, the author of the commentary on the Buddhavaṃsa, the commentary on the Abhidhammāvatāra, and the commentary on the Vinayavinicchaya, the Venerable Teacher Buddhadatta, was born in the city of Uraga on the Tambapaṇṇi River in the Coḷa country and was a contemporary of the Teacher Buddhaghosa. The author of the Paramatthavinicchaya, the Nāmarūpapariccheda, and the Abhidhammatthasaṅgaha, the Venerable Teacher Anuruddha, was a native of the city of Kāverī in the Kañcivara country. The Venerable Teacher Dhammapāla, author of the Paramatthadīpanī, which is a commentary on the Pāli of the Udāna and other texts included in the Khuddaka Nikāya, was also a native of the city of Kañcipura in South India. In the same way, this one too should be understood. For it is stated in the conclusion of the commentary on the Aṅguttara Nikāya named the Manorathapūraṇī:

“Āyācito sumatinā, therena bhadantajotipālena;

Kañcipurādīsu mayā, pubbe saddhiṃ vasantenā”ti.

“Requested by the wise elder, the venerable Jotipāla, with whom I formerly dwelt in Kañcipura and other places.”

Ettha ca kañcipuraṃ nāma madarasanagarassa īsakaṃ pacchimanissite dakkhiṇadisābhāge pañcacattālīsamilappamāṇe padese idāni kañjīvara iti voharitanagarameva.

Here, the city named Kañcipura, in a place about forty-five miles away, situated slightly to the west of the city of Madarasa in the southern direction, is the very city now known as Kañjīvara.

Tathā papañcasūdaniyā nāma majjhimaṭṭhakathāya nigamanepi –

Similarly, also in the conclusion of the commentary on the Majjhima Nikāya, the Papañcasūdanī:

“Āyācito sumatinā, therena buddhamittena;

Pubbe mayūradūta mayūrarūpa (sī.), mayūrasutta (syā.) paṭṭanamhi saddhiṃ vasantenā”ti – vuttaṃ.

“Requested by the wise elder Buddhamitta, with whom I formerly dwelt together in the port-town of Mayūradūtapaṭṭana”—so it is said.

Ettha ca mayūradūtapaṭṭanaṃ nāma idāni madarasanagarasamīpe milapora iti voharitaṭṭhānanti porāṇappavattigavesīhi vuttaṃ.

Here, it is said by investigators of ancient traditions that the port-town named Mayūradūtapaṭṭana is the place near the city of Madarasa now called Milapora.

Imāhi pana nigamanagāthāhi dakkhiṇaindiyaraṭṭheyeva nivutthapubbataṃ pakāseti, bodhimaṇḍasamīpe vā, marammaraṭṭhe vā nivutthapubbatāya pakāsanañca na dissati. Tena ācariyabuddhaghoso dakkhiṇaindiyaraṭṭhiko na hotīti na sakkā paṭikkhipituṃ.

These concluding verses, however, reveal only that he had previously resided in the South Indian country; no mention of a previous residence near the Bodhi-maṇḍa or in the Maramma country is seen. Therefore, it cannot be denied that the Teacher Buddhaghosa was from the South Indian country.

Samantapāsādikāyampi vinayaṭṭhakathāyaṃ (3, 13) ācariyena evaṃ vuttaṃ –

Also in the Samantapāsādikā, the commentary on the Vinaya (3, 13), the following was said by the Teacher:

“Yaṃ [M 0.0033] pana andhakaṭṭhakathāyaṃ ‘aparikkhitte pamukhe anāpattīti bhūmiyaṃ vinā jagatiyā pamukhaṃ sandhāya kathita’nti vuttaṃ, taṃ andhakaraṭṭhe pāṭekkasannivesā ekacchadanā gabbhapāḷiyo sandhāya vutta”nti.

“But as for what is stated in the Andhaka Commentary, namely, ‘There is no offense in an unenclosed frontage,’ this was said with reference to the frontage on the terrace, excluding the ground. That was said with reference to the rows of inner chambers that are single-roofed and have separate arrangements in the Andhaka country.”

Iminā pana vacanena “andhakaṭṭhakathā andhakaraṭṭhikehi therehi katā”ti pākaṭā hoti, ācariyabuddhaghosopi ca andhakaṭṭhakathāya sandhāyabhāsitampi tandesikagabbhapāḷisannivesākārampi suṭṭhu jānāti, tasmā tandesiko na hotīti na sakkā vattunti.

By this statement, it becomes clear that ‘the Andhaka Commentary was composed by the elders of the Andhaka country.’ And the Teacher Buddhaghosa also knows well both the intended meaning of the Andhaka Commentary and the manner of arrangement of the rows of inner chambers of that region. Therefore, it cannot be said that he was not a native of that country.

Tathā imassapi visuddhimaggassa nigamane – “moraṇḍakheṭakavattabbenā”ti vuttaṃ. Ettha ca kheṭoti padassa gāmoti vā, jānapadānaṃ kassakānaṃ nivāsoti vā, khuddakanagaranti vā tayo atthā sakkatābhidhāne pakāsitā, dakkhiṇaindiyaraṭṭhesu ca yāvajjatanāpi gāmo kheḍāti voharīyati. Tasmā moraṇḍavhaye kheṭe jāto moraṇḍakheṭako, moraṇḍakheṭako iti vattabbo moraṇḍakheṭakavattabbo, tena moraṇḍakheṭakavattabbenāti vacanatthaṃ katvā “moraṇḍagāme jātoti vattabbena therenā”ti attho gahetabbo. Idāni pana dakkhiṇaindiyaraṭṭhe guntājanapade nāgārajunakoṇḍato ekapaṇṇāsamilamatte (51) amaravatito ca aṭṭhapaṇṇāsamilamatte (58) padese kotanemalipurīti ca gundalapallīti ca voharitaṃ ṭhānadvayamatthi, tattha ca bahūni buddhasāsanikaporāṇasantakāni diṭṭhāni, nemalīti telaguvohāro ca morassa, gundalu iti ca aṇḍassa, tasmā taṃ ṭhānadvayameva pubbe moraṇḍakheṭoti voharito ācariyabuddhaghosassa jātigāmo bhaveyyāti porāṇaṭṭhānagavesīhi gahito. Yasmā panetaṃ “moraṇḍakheṭakavattabbenā”ti padaṃ “moraṇḍagāmajātenā”ti padaṃ viya pāḷinayānucchavikaṃ na hoti, aññehi ca bahūhi visesanapadehi ekato aṭṭhatvā visesyapadassa pacchato visuṃ ṭhitaṃ, āgamaṭṭhakathādīsu ca na dissati, tasmā etaṃ kenaci taṃkālikena ācariyassa jātiṭṭhānaṃ sañjānantena pakkhittaṃ viya dissatīti.

Similarly, in the conclusion of this Visuddhimagga too, it is said: ‘by one who should be called a Moraṇḍakheṭaka.’ Here, three meanings of the word ‘kheṭa’ have been declared in Sanskrit dictionaries: a village, a dwelling of country farmers, or a small town. Moreover, in the South Indian countries, even today a village is called ‘kheḍā.’ Therefore, one born in a kheṭa named Moraṇḍa is a Moraṇḍakheṭaka; one who should be called a Moraṇḍakheṭaka is a Moraṇḍakheṭakavattabba. Thus, having construed the meaning of the expression ‘by one who should be called a Moraṇḍakheṭaka,’ the meaning should be understood as ‘by the elder who should be called one born in the village of Moraṇḍa.’ Now, in the South Indian country, in the Guntā district, at a distance of about 51 miles from Nāgārajunakoṇḍa and about 58 miles from Amarāvatī, there are two places known as Kotanemalipuri and Gundalapalli. There, many ancient ruins belonging to the Buddhist Dispensation have been found scattered about. ‘Nemalī’ is the Telugu word for peacock, and ‘gundalu’ is for egg. Therefore, it is held by archaeologists investigating ancient sites that these two places were formerly called Moraṇḍakheṭa and might have been the native village of the Teacher Buddhaghosa. However, since this term ‘moraṇḍakheṭakavattabbenā’ is not in conformity with Pāli usage like the term ‘moraṇḍagāmajātenā,’ and since it stands separately after the word it qualifies instead of with the other adjectives, and is not found in the commentaries on the Agamas, etc., it appears as if it was inserted by someone of that time who knew the Teacher's birthplace.

Imesu pana tīsu “ācariyabuddhaghoso bodhimaṇḍasamīpe jātoti ca marammaraṭṭhikoti ca dakkhiṇaindiyaraṭṭhiko”ti ca vuttavacanesu pacchimameva balavataraṃ hoti ācariyasseva vacananissitattā, tasmā [M 0.0034] tadeva nissāya ācariyabuddhaghosattherassa uppatti evaṃ veditabbā.

However, among these three statements—that ‘the Teacher Buddhaghosa was born near the Bodhi-maṇḍa,’ that ‘he was a native of the Maramma country,’ and that ‘he was a native of the South Indian country’—the last one is the strongest because it is based on the Teacher's own words. Therefore, relying on that very one, the biography of the Venerable Teacher Buddhaghosa should be understood as follows:

Ācariyabuddhaghosattherassa aṭṭhuppatti

The Biography of the Teacher, the Elder Buddhaghosa

Ācariyabuddhaghoso dasame buddhavassasatake (901-1000-bu-va) dakkhiṇaindiyaraṭṭhe moraṇḍagāme brāhmaṇakule jāto, so tīsu vedesu ceva sabbavijjāsippaganthesu ca pāraṅgato hutvā buddhasāsanadhammaṃ sutvā tampi uggaṇhitukāmo tasmiṃyeva dakkhiṇaindiyaraṭṭhe ekasmiṃ theravādikavihāre mahāvihāravāsīnaṃ revatattherappamukhānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ santike pabbajjañceva upasampadañca gaṇhitvā piṭakattayapāḷimuggaṇhi. So evaṃ piṭakattayapāḷimuggaṇhantoyeva aññāsi “ayamekāyanamaggo dassanavisuddhiyā nibbānasacchikiriyāyā”ti. Ācariyupajjhāyā ca tassa visiṭṭhañāṇappabhāvasampannabhāvaṃ ñatvā “imassa buddhasāsane kittighoso buddhassa viya pavattissatī”ti sampassamānā “buddhaghoso”ti nāmamakaṃsu. Tena vuttaṃ “buddhaghosoti garūhi gahitanāmadheyyenā”ti.

The Teacher Buddhaghosa was born in the tenth century of the Buddhist Era (901–1000 B.E.) in a brahmin family in the village of Moraṇḍa in South India. Having mastered the three Vedas and all treatises on sciences and arts, he heard the Dhamma of the Buddha's Dispensation. Desiring to learn that too, he went to a Theravāda monastery in that same land of South India and, in the presence of the monks of the Mahāvihāra lineage headed by the Elder Revata, received the going forth and the higher ordination, and learned the Pāli Tipiṭaka. While learning the Pāli Tipiṭaka, he realized: ‘This is the only way for the purification of vision and for the realization of Nibbāna.’ His teachers and preceptors, recognizing that he was endowed with the power of exceptional wisdom and foreseeing that ‘in the Buddha's Dispensation, his fame will spread like that of the Buddha,’ gave him the name ‘Buddhaghosa.’ Therefore it is said: ‘by the one named Buddhaghosa by his revered teachers.’

So evaṃ piṭakattayapāḷimuggaṇhitvā madarasa nagarasamīpaṭṭhānabhūte mayūradūtapaṭṭanamhi ca kañcipurādīsu ca vasanto andhakaṭṭhakathāya paricayaṃ katvā tāya asantuṭṭhacitto sīhaḷaṭṭhakathāsupi paricayaṃ kātukāmo tā ca pāḷibhāsamāropetvā abhinavīkātumāsīsanto sīhaḷadīpamagamāsi. Tasmiñca kāle sīhaḷadīpe mahānāmo nāma rājā rajjaṃ kāreti, so ca rājā abhayagirivāsīsu pasanno teyeva visesato paggaṇhāti.

Thus, having learned the Pāli Tipiṭaka, while living in Mayūradūtapaṭṭana near the city of Madras, and in Kañcipura and other places, he became familiar with the Andhaka Commentary. Dissatisfied with it and desiring to become familiar with the Sinhalese commentaries as well, and wishing to revise them by translating them into the Pāli language, he went to the island of Sīhaḷa. At that time, a king named Mahānāma was ruling on the island of Sīhaḷa, and this king was devoted to the residents of Abhayagiri, supporting them especially.

Ekacce pana ādhunikā vicakkhaṇā evaṃ vadanti “ācariyabuddhaghosassa sīhaḷadīpāgamanena sirimeghavaṇṇarājakālato (846-bu-va) puretaraṃyeva bhavitabba”nti. Idañca nesaṃ kāraṇaṃ, tassa rañño navavassakāle (855-bu-va) buddhassa dāṭhādhātukaliṅgaraṭṭhato sīhaḷadīpamānītā, tato paṭṭhāya sīhaḷarājāno anusaṃvaccharaṃ mahantaṃ dhātupūjāussavaṃ karonti. Yadi ca ācariyabuddhaghoso tato pacchā sīhaḷadīpamāgaccheyya, tampi pāsādikaṃ mahussavaṃ disvā attano ganthesu pakāseyya yathā phāhiyaṃ nāma cinabhikkhu mahānāmarājakāle (953-975-bu-va) taṃ disvā attano addhānakkamakathāyaṃ pakāsesi, na pana ācariyassa ganthesu [M 0.0035] taṃpakāsanā dissati, tenetaṃ ñāyati “ācariyabuddhaghoso dāṭhādhātusampattakālato (855-bu-va) puretaraṃyeva sīhaḷadīpamāgantvā aṭṭhakathāyo akāsī”ti. Taṃ pana na daḷhakāraṇaṃ hoti, tipiṭakapāḷiyā hi atthasaṃvaṇṇanāya yaṃ vā taṃ vā attano paccakkhadiṭṭhaṃ pakāsetabbaṃ na hoti, na ca atthasaṃvaṇṇanā addhānakkamakathāsadisā. Kiñca bhiyyo, samantapāsādikāya vinayaṭṭhakathāyaṃ dīpavaṃsatopi kiñci ānetvā pakāsitaṃ, dīpavaṃse ca yāva mahāsenarājakālā (819-845-bu-va) pavatti pakāsitāti sirimeghavaṇṇarājakālato (845-873-bu-va) pubbe dīpavaṃsoyeva likhito na bhaveyya. Yadi ca aṭṭhakathāyo tato pubbeyeva katā bhaveyyuṃ, kathaṃ tattha dīpavaṃso sakkā pakāsetunti.

However, some modern discerning scholars say: “The Teacher Buddhaghosa’s arrival on the island of Sīhaḷa must have been even before the time of King Sirimeghavaṇṇa (846 B.E.).” And this is their reason: In the ninth year of that king’s reign (855 B.E.), the Buddha’s tooth relic was brought from the country of Kaliṅga to the island of Sīhaḷa; from then on, the kings of Sīhaḷa have held a great annual festival in honor of the relic. If the Teacher Buddhaghosa had come to the island of Sīhaḷa after that, then having seen that splendid great festival, he would have mentioned it in his books, just as the Chinese monk named Faxian, having seen it in the time of King Mahānāma (953–975 B.E.), mentioned it in his travel account. But since no such mention is seen in the Teacher’s books, it is inferred that “the Teacher Buddhaghosa arrived on the island of Sīhaḷa and wrote the commentaries even before the time of the arrival of the tooth relic (855 B.E.).” But this is not a strong reason. For in a commentary explaining the meaning of the Pāli Tipiṭaka, it is not necessary to mention whatever one has personally seen, nor is a commentary like a travelogue. Furthermore, in the Samantapāsādikā, the Vinaya commentary, something is cited from the Dīpavaṃsa and explained. Since the history in the Dīpavaṃsa is recorded up to the time of King Mahāsena (819–845 B.E.), the Dīpavaṃsa itself could not have been completed before the time of King Sirimeghavaṇṇa (845–873 B.E.). And if the commentaries were composed before that time, how could the Dīpavaṃsa be cited in them?

Ācariyabuddhaghoso pana sīhaḷadīpaṃ pattakāle (965-bu-va) mahāvihārameva gantvā tattha sīhaḷamahātherānaṃ santike sīhaḷaṭṭhakathāyo suṇi. Vuttañhi samantapāsādikāyaṃ –

When the Teacher Buddhaghosa arrived on the island of Sīhaḷa (965 B.E.), he went to the Mahāvihāra and there, in the presence of the great elders of Sīhaḷa, he heard and learned the Sinhala commentaries. For it is said in the conclusion of the Samantapāsādikā:

“Mahāaṭṭhakathañceva, mahāpaccarimeva ca;

Kurundiñcāti tissopi, sīhaḷaṭṭhakathā imā.

“These three—the Mahā-aṭṭhakathā, the Mahāpaccari, and the Kurundī—are the Sinhala commentaries.

Buddhamittoti nāmena, vissutassa yasassino;

Vinayaññussa dhīrassa, sutvā therassa santike”ti pari. aṭṭha. nigamanakathā.

Having heard them in the presence of the elder named Buddhamitta, who was renowned and famous, wise, and an expert in the Vinaya...”

Iminā pana aṭṭhakathāvacanena mahāaṭṭhakathādīnaṃ tissannaṃyeva aṭṭhakathānaṃ sutabhāvo dassito. Samantapāsādikāyaṃ pana saṅkhepaandhakaṭṭhakathānampi vinicchayo dassitoyeva, kasmā pana tā ācariyena sīhaḷattherānaṃ santike na sutāti? Tāsu hi andhakaṭṭhakathā tāva andhakaraṭṭhikabhāvato, kataparicayabhāvato ca na sutāti pākaṭoyevāyamattho. Saṅkhepaṭṭhakathā pana mahāpaccariṭṭhakathāya saṃkhittamattabhāvato na sutāti veditabbā. Tathā hi vajirabuddhiṭīkāyaṃ ganthārambhasaṃvaṇṇanāyaṃ vijira. ṭī. ganthārambhakathāvaṇṇanā cūḷapaccariṭṭhakathāandhakaṭṭhakathānampi ādi-saddena saṅgahitabhāvo vutto, sāratthadīpanī-vimativinodanīṭīkāsu sārattha. ṭī. 1.92 pācittiyakaṇḍa; vi. vi. ṭī. 1.ganthārambhakathāvaṇṇanā pana andhakasaṅkhepaṭṭhakathānaṃ saṅgahitabhāvo vutto, samantapāsādikāyañca cūḷapaccarīti nāmaṃ kuhiñcipi na dissati, mahāṭṭhakathā mahāpaccarī kurundī andhakasaṅkhepaṭṭhakathāti [M 0.0036] imāniyeva nāmāni dissanti, bahūsu ca ṭhānesu “saṅkhepaṭṭhakathāyaṃ pana mahāpaccariyañca vutta”ntiādinā pārā. aṭṭha. 1.94 dvinnampi samānavinicchayo dassito. Tasmā vajirabuddhiyaṃ cūḷapaccarīti vuttaṭṭhakathā mahāpaccarito uddharitvā saṅkhepena kataṭṭhakathā bhaveyya, sā ca saṅkhepena katattā saṅkhepaṭṭhakathā nāma jātā bhaveyya. Evañca sati mahāpaccariyā sutāya sāpi sutāyeva hotīti na sā ācariyena sutāti veditabbā.

By this statement from the commentary, it is shown that he heard and learned only these three commentaries, the Mahā-aṭṭhakathā and the others. However, in the Samantapāsādikā, the decisions of the Saṅkhepa-aṭṭhakathā and the Andhaka-aṭṭhakathā are also presented. Why then were these not heard and learned by the Teacher from the Sinhala elders? Regarding these, the Andhaka-aṭṭhakathā was not heard because it originated from the Andhaka country and he was already familiar with it while living in India; this point is quite clear. As for the Saṅkhepa-aṭṭhakathā, it should be understood that it was not heard separately because it is merely an abridgement of the Mahāpaccari-aṭṭhakathā. To explain: in the Vajirabuddhi-ṭīkā, in the introductory explanation of the book, it is stated that the Cūḷapaccari-aṭṭhakathā and the Andhaka-aṭṭhakathā are included by the word ‘etc.’ (ādi). In the Sāratthadīpanī and Vimativinodanī-ṭīkās, however, it is stated that the Andhaka- and Saṅkhepa-aṭṭhakathās are included. In the existing Samantapāsādikā, the name ‘Cūḷapaccari’ is not found anywhere; only the names Mahā-aṭṭhakathā, Mahāpaccari, Kurundī, Andhaka-aṭṭhakathā, and Saṅkhepa-aṭṭhakathā are seen. And in many places, a similar decision for both is shown, as in: ‘But in the Saṅkhepa-aṭṭhakathā and in the Mahāpaccari it is said...’ and so on. Therefore, the commentary called ‘Cūḷapaccari’ in the Vajirabuddhi must be the commentary composed in brief by extracting from the Mahāpaccari, and because it was composed in brief, it came to be known as the Saṅkhepa-aṭṭhakathā. This being so, since the Mahāpaccari was heard, that Saṅkhepa-aṭṭhakathā was also effectively heard; thus it should be understood that it was not heard separately by the Teacher.

Evaṃ sīhaḷaṭṭhakathāyo suṇantasseva ācariyabuddhaghosassa tikkhagambhīrajavanañāṇappabhāvavisesasampannabhāvañca paramavisuddhasaddhābuddhivīriyapaṭimaṇḍitasīlācārajjavamaddavādiguṇasamudaya- samuditabhāvañca sakasamayasamayantaragahanajjhogāhaṇasamatthapaññāveyyatti- yasamannāgatabhāvañca anekasatthantarocitasaṃvaṇṇanānayasukovidabhāvañca ñatvā taṃsavanakiccapariniṭṭhitakāle saṅghapālādayo therā taṃ visuddhimaggādiganthānaṃ karaṇatthāya visuṃ visuṃ āyāciṃsu. Ettha ca ācariyassa yathāvuttaguṇehi sampannabhāvo attano vacaneneva pākaṭo. Vuttañhi attano ganthanigamanesu –

Thus, while he was hearing the Sinhala commentaries, it was recognized that the Teacher Buddhaghosa was endowed with the special power of keen, profound, and swift knowledge; was a collection of virtues such as uprightness and gentleness and was adorned with conduct, moral behavior, and supremely pure faith, wisdom, and energy; was possessed of the proficiency of wisdom capable of plumbing the depths of his own tenets and the tenets of other systems; and was highly skilled in the methods of exposition suitable for various other treatises. Recognizing this, when the task of hearing was completed, the elders headed by Saṅghapāla individually requested him to compose the Visuddhimagga and other works. Here, the Teacher’s possession of the aforementioned qualities is made clear by his own words. For it is said in the colophons of his own books:

“Paramavisuddhasaddhābuddhivīriyapaṭimaṇḍitena sīlācārajjavamaddavādiguṇasamudayasamuditena sakasamayasamayantaragahanajjhogāhaṇasamatthena paññāveyyattiyasamannāgatenā”tiādi.

“By one adorned with supremely pure faith, wisdom, and energy; endowed with a collection of virtues such as moral conduct, good behavior, uprightness, and gentleness; capable of plumbing the depths of his own and other systems of thought; possessed of the proficiency of wisdom...,” and so on.

Tattha sakasamayasamayantaragahanajjhogāhaṇasamatthenāti padena ācariyabuddhaghosatthero mahāvihāravāsīnaṃ visuddhattheravādīnaṃ desanānayasaṅkhāte sakasamaye ca mahāsaṅghikādimahāyānikapariyosānānaṃ nikāyantarabhūtānaṃ paresaṃ piṭakaganthantaravādanayasaṅkhāte parasamaye ca tathā taṃkālikaaññatitthiyasamaṇabrāhmaṇānaṃ vedattayādisaṅkhāte parasamaye ca kovido, tesaṃ sakasamayaparasamayānaṃ durogāhadubbodhatthasaṅkhāte gahanaṭṭhānepi ca ogāhituṃ samatthoti dīpeti. Paññāveyyattiyasamannāgatoti padena ācariyabuddhaghosatthero porāṇaṭṭhakathāyo saṅkhipituñca paṭisaṅkharituñca samatthoti dīpetīti veditabbo.

In that context, by the phrase “capable of plumbing the depths of his own and other systems of thought,” it is shown that the Teacher, the Elder Buddhaghosa, was an expert in his own system—namely, the teaching method of the pure Theravādin residents of the Mahāvihāra—and also in other systems, namely the doctrinal methods and different Piṭaka texts of other schools starting from the Mahāsaṅghikas and ending with the Mahāyānists, as well as the systems of contemporary ascetics and brahmins of other faiths, such as the three Vedas. It is also shown that he was capable of plumbing even the profound points of those systems, his own and others’, which are defined as being difficult to penetrate and difficult to understand. By the phrase “possessed of the distinction of wisdom,” it should be understood that it is shown that the Teacher, the Elder Buddhaghosa, was capable of both abridging and revising the ancient commentaries.

Āyācanakāraṇaṃ

The Reason for the Request

Kasmā [M 0.0037] pana te taṃ āyāciṃsūti? Vuccate, mahāvihāravāsino hi āditoyeva paṭṭhāya piṭakattayaṃ yathā tīsu saṅgītīsu pāḷibhāsāya saṅgītaṃ, yathā ca vaṭṭagāmaṇirājakāle (455-467-bu-va) potthakesu āropitaṃ, tathā porāṇaṃ pāḷipiṭakameva uggaṇhanti ceva vācenti ca, na sakkatāropitapiṭakaṃ. Aṭṭhakathāyo ca tivassasatamattato pure katā. Tathā hi aṭṭhakathāsu vasabharājakālato (609-653-bu-0) pacchā sīhaḷikattherānañceva aññesañca vatthu na dissati ṭhapetvā mahāsenarājavatthuṃ pārā. aṭṭha. 2.236-237, yāva ācariyabuddhaghosakālāpi ca tā eva porāṇaṭṭhakathāyo atthi na abhinavīkatā. Tena tesaṃ piṭakesu yebhuyyena janā paricayaṃ kātuṃ asañjātābhilāsā honti asañjātussāhā. Dīpantaresu ca attano piṭakaṃ pattharāpetuṃ na sakkonti aṭṭhakathānaṃ dīpabhāsāya abhisaṅkhatattā. Abhayagirivāsino pana vaṭṭagāmaṇirājakālato paṭṭhāya sakkatabhāsāropitaṃ dhammarucinikāyādipiṭakampi mahāyānapiṭakampi navaṃ navaṃ pariyāpuṇanti ceva vācenti ca, tena tesaṃ piṭakesu yebhuyyena janā paricayaṃ kātuṃ sañjātābhilāsā honti sañjātussāhā, navaṃ navameva hi sattā piyāyanti. Tatoyeva te dīpantaresupi attano vādaṃ pattharāpetuṃ sakkonti. Tasmā te mahāvihāravāsino therā attano sīhaḷaṭṭhakathāyo pāḷibhāsāya abhisaṅkharitukāmā tathā kātuṃ samatthaṃ ācariyabuddhaghosattherassa ñāṇappabhāvavisesaṃ yathāvuttaguṇasampannabhāvañca ñatvā āyāciṃsūti veditabbaṃ.

But why did they request this of him? It is said: The residents of the Mahāvihāra, from the very beginning, learned and taught the ancient Pāli Canon just as it was recited in the three Councils in the Pāli language and as it was written down in books during the reign of King Vaṭṭagāmaṇi (455–467 B.E.), but not the canon rendered into Sanskrit. The commentaries, too, had been composed more than three hundred years earlier. Indeed, in the commentaries, no accounts concerning the Sinhalese elders or others are found after the time of King Vasabha (609–653 B.E.), except for the story of King Mahāsena. And up to the time of the Teacher Buddhaghosa, only those same ancient commentaries existed and had not been revised. For this reason, people generally had no desire or enthusiasm to study their texts. Moreover, they were unable to propagate their own canon in other lands because the commentaries were composed in the island's language. The residents of the Abhayagiri, however, from the time of King Vaṭṭagāmaṇi onward, learned and taught both the canon of the Dhammaruci Nikāya and others rendered into Sanskrit, as well as the new Mahāyāna canon. Therefore, people generally had a desire and enthusiasm to study their texts, for beings are always fond of what is new. For this very reason, they were also able to propagate their doctrines in other lands. Therefore, it should be understood that those elders of the Mahāvihāra, wishing to have their Sinhala commentaries revised in the Pāli language, and knowing that the Teacher, the Elder Buddhaghosa, possessed an exceptional power of knowledge and the aforementioned virtues, being capable of such a task, made this request of him.

Visuddhimaggassa karaṇaṃ

The Composition of the Path of Purification

Tesu tāva visuddhimaggaṃ ācariyabuddhaghoso saṅghapālattherena ajjhesito mahāvihārassa dakkhiṇabhāge padhānaghare mahānigamassāmino pāsāde pari. aṭṭha. nigamanakathā vasanto akāsi. Ettāvatā ca “so panesa visuddhimaggo kena kato, kadā kato, kattha kato, kasmā kato”ti imesaṃ pañhānamattho vitthārena vibhāvito hoti.

Among those works, the Teacher Buddhaghosa first composed the Visuddhimagga at the request of the Elder Saṅghapāla, while residing in the mansion of the lord of Mahānigama, in the Padhānaghara precinct to the south of the Mahāvihāra. Thus far, the meaning of these questions—‘By whom was this Visuddhimagga made? When was it made? Where was it made? And for what reason was it made?’—has been explained in detail.

Idāni [M 0.0038] kimatthaṃ katotiādīnaṃ pañhānamatthaṃ pakāsayissāma. Tattha kimatthaṃ katoti etassa pana pañhassa attho ācariyeneva pakāsito. Kathaṃ?

Now we will clarify the meaning of the questions beginning with, ‘For what purpose was it made?’ Therein, the meaning of the question, ‘For what purpose was it made?’ has been clarified by the Teacher himself. How so?

“Sudullabhaṃ labhitvāna, pabbajjaṃ jinasāsane;

Sīlādisaṅgahaṃ khemaṃ, ujuṃ maggaṃ visuddhiyā.

“Having obtained the going forth, so difficult to gain, in the Dispensation of the Conqueror—the secure and straight path to purity, which is the collection of virtue and so on—

Yathābhūtaṃ ajānantā, suddhikāmāpi ye idha;

Visuddhiṃ nādhigacchanti, vāyamantāpi yogino.

those yogis here who, not knowing it as it truly is, even though they desire purity and continue to strive, do not attain purification.

Tesaṃ pāmojjakaraṇaṃ, suvisuddhavinicchayaṃ;

Mahāvihāravāsīnaṃ, desanānayanissitaṃ.

For the purpose of bringing them joy, with its thoroughly pure analysis, and relying on the teaching method of the residents of the Mahāvihāra,

Visuddhimaggaṃ bhāsissaṃ, taṃ me sakkacca bhāsato;

Visuddhikāmā sabbepi, nisāmayatha sādhavo”ti visuddhi. 1.2.

I shall expound the Path of Purity; therefore, all you good people who desire purity, listen attentively to me as I speak.”

Tasmā esa visuddhimaggo visuddhisaṅkhātanibbānakāmānaṃ sādhujanānaṃ sīlasamādhipaññāsaṅkhātassa visuddhimaggassa yāthāvato jānanatthāya katoti padhānappayojanavasena veditabbo. Appadhānappayojanavasena pana catūsu āgamaṭṭhakathāsu ganthasallahukabhāvatthāyapi katoti veditabbo. Tathā hi vuttaṃ āgamaṭṭhakathāsu –

Therefore, from the standpoint of the principal purpose, it should be understood that this Path of Purification was composed so that good people who desire Nibbāna, which is called ‘purification,’ might come to know accurately the Path of Purification consisting of virtue, concentration, and wisdom. From the standpoint of the secondary purpose, however, it should be understood that it was also composed to lighten the textual load in the commentaries on the four Āgamas. For so it is said in the commentaries on the Āgamas:

“Majjhe visuddhimaggo, esa catunnampi āgamānañhi;

Ṭhatvā pakāsayissati, tattha yathābhāsitamatthaṃ;

Icceva me kato”ti dī. ni. aṭṭha. 1.ganthārambhakathā.

“‘This Path of Purification stands in the midst of the four Āgamas; standing there, it will illuminate the meaning as it was spoken in them. For this very purpose it was composed by me.’”

Tannissayo

Its Basis

Kiṃ nissāya katoti etassapi pañhassa attho ācariyeneva pakāsito. Vuttañhi ettha ganthārambhe –

The meaning of this question too—‘On what is it based?’—has been clarified by the Teacher himself. For it is said here at the beginning of the work:

“Mahāvihāravāsīnaṃ, desanānayanissita”nti visuddhi. 1.2.

“‘Relying on the teaching method of the residents of the Mahāvihāra.’”

Tathā nigamanepi –

So too in the conclusion:

“Tesaṃ sīlādibhedānaṃ, atthānaṃ yo vinicchayo;

Pañcannampi nikāyānaṃ, vutto aṭṭhakathānaye.

“‘The analysis of the meanings of those categories starting with virtue has been stated in the commentarial method for all five Nikāyas.

Samāharitvā taṃ sabbaṃ, yebhuyyena sanicchayo;

Sabbasaṅkaradosehi, mutto yasmā pakāsito”ti dī. ni. aṭṭha. 1.ganthārambhakathā.

Having collected all that, and generally with its analysis, it is explained as being free from the faults of confusing different views.’”

Iminā [M 0.0039] pana vacanena ayamattho pākaṭo hoti – “visuddhimaggaṃ kurumāno ācariyo mahāvihāravāsīnaṃ desanānayasaṅkhātā pañcannampi nikāyānaṃ porāṇaṭṭhakathāyo nissāya tāsu vuttaṃ gahetabbaṃ sabbaṃ vinicchayaṃ samāharitvā akāsī”ti. Tasmā yā yā ettha padavaṇṇanā vā vinicchayo vā sādhakavatthu vā dassīyati, taṃ sabbaṃ tassa tassa niddhāritapāḷipadassanikāyasaṃvaṇṇanābhūtāya porāṇasīhaḷaṭṭhakathāto ānetvā bhāsāparivattanavaseneva dassitanti veditabbaṃ. Ayampi hi visuddhimaggo na kevalaṃ attano ñāṇappabhāvena kato, visuṃ pakaraṇabhāvena ca, atha kho catunnampi āgamaṭṭhakathānaṃ avayavabhāveneva kato. Vuttañhi tāsaṃ nigamane –

By this statement, the following meaning becomes clear: ‘The Ācariya, in composing the Visuddhimagga, did so by relying on the ancient commentaries to the five Nikāyas, which are considered the teaching method of the residents of the Mahāvihāra, and by collecting and compiling all the pertinent analyses stated in them.’ Therefore, it should be understood that whatever word-explanations, analyses, or illustrative stories are shown here, all have been taken from the ancient Sinhala commentaries, which serve to explain the Nikāyas by citing the specified Pāli passages, and have been presented simply by means of a change of language. For this Visuddhimagga was not composed solely by the power of his own knowledge, nor as a separate treatise, but rather as an integral part of the commentaries to the four Āgamas. For it is said in their conclusion:

“Ekūnasaṭṭhimatto, visuddhimaggopi bhāṇavārehi;

Atthappakāsanatthāya, āgamānaṃ kato yasmā.

“Since the Visuddhimagga, which has about fifty-nine recitation sections, was also composed to illuminate the meaning of the Āgamas,

Tasmā tena sahāyaṃ, aṭṭhakathā bhāṇavāragaṇanāya;

Suparimitaparicchinnaṃ, cattālīsasataṃ hotī”tiādi dī. ni. aṭṭha. 3.nigamanakathā.

Therefore this commentary, together with it, by the count of recitation sections, becomes a well-measured and defined one hundred and forty, and so on.”

Yā pana visuddhimagge maggāmaggañāṇadassanavisuddhiniddese “ayaṃ tāva visuddhikathāyaṃ nayo. Ariyavaṃsakathāyaṃ panā”tiādinā visuddhi. 2.717 dve kathā vuttā, tāpi mahāvihāravāsīnaṃ desanānaye antogadhā imassa visuddhimaggassa nissayāyevāti veditabbāti.

Furthermore, the two discourses mentioned in the Visuddhimagga, in the Exposition of the Purification by Knowledge and Vision of Path and Not-Path, beginning with the words, ‘This is the method in the discourse on purification. But in the discourse on the noble lineages…,’ and so on—these should be understood as also being included within the teaching method of the residents of the Mahāvihāra and as forming a basis for this Visuddhimagga.

Takkaraṇappakāro

The Manner of Its Composition

Kena pakārena katoti ettha anantarapañhe vuttappakāreneva kato. Tathā hi ācariyo saṃyuttanikāyato

By what manner was it composed? Here, it was composed in the very manner stated in the immediately preceding question. For the Teacher, from the Saṃyutta Nikāya—

“Sīle patiṭṭhāya naro sapañño, cittaṃ paññañca bhāvayaṃ;

Ātāpī nipako bhikkhu, so imaṃ vijaṭaye jaṭa”nti saṃ. ni. 1.23 –

“Established in virtue, a wise person, developing concentration and wisdom; an ardent, prudent bhikkhu, he disentangles this tangle.”

Imaṃ gāthaṃ paṭhamaṃ dassetvā tattha padhānavasena vuttā sīlasamādhipaññāyo visuṃ visuṃ vitthārato vibhajitvā akāsi. Evaṃ kurumāno ca pañcahipi nikāyehi sīlasamādhipaññāpaṭisaṃyuttāni suttapadāni uddharitvā tesaṃ atthañca sīhaḷaṭṭhakathāhi bhāsāparivattanavasena dassetvā tāsu vuttāni sīhaḷikavatthūni ca vinicchaye ca pakāsesi. Visesato pana tasmiṃ [M 0.0040] kāle pākaṭā sakasamayaviruddhā samayantarā ca bahūsu ṭhānesu dassetvā sahetukaṃ paṭikkhittā. Kathaṃ?

Having first presented this verse, he then composed his work by analyzing, separately and in detail, the virtue, concentration, and wisdom mentioned therein as the main subjects. In doing so, he extracted passages connected with virtue, concentration, and wisdom from all five Nikāyas and showed their meaning by way of a change of language from the Sinhala commentaries. He also clarified the Sinhala stories and decisions stated in them. Moreover, in many places he pointed out other doctrines, prevalent at that time and contrary to his own, and refuted them with reasons. How?

Tattha hi cariyāvaṇṇanāyaṃ “tatra purimā tāva tisso cariyā pubbāciṇṇanidānā dhātudosanidānā cāti ekacce vadanti. Pubbe kira iṭṭhappayogasubhakammabahulo rāgacarito hoti, saggā vā cavitvā idhūpapanno. Pubbe chedanavadhabandhanaverakammabahulo dosacarito hoti, nirayanāgayonīhi vā cavitvā idhūpapanno. Pubbe majjapānabahulo sutaparipucchāvihīno ca mohacarito hoti, tiracchānayoniyā vā cavitvā idhūpapannoti evaṃ pubbāciṇṇanidānāti vadanti. Dvinnaṃ pana dhātūnaṃ ussannattā puggalo mohacarito hoti pathavīdhātuyā ca āpodhātuyā ca. Itarāsaṃ dvinnaṃ ussannattā dosacarito. Sabbāsaṃ samattā pana rāgacaritoti. Dosesu ca semhādhiko rāgacarito hoti. Vātādhiko mohacarito. Semhādhiko vā mohacarito. Vātādhiko rāgacaritoti evaṃ dhātudosanidānāti vadantī”ti ekaccevādaṃ dassetvā so “tattha yasmā pubbe iṭṭhappayogasubhakammabahulāpi saggā cavitvā idhūpapannāpi ca na sabbe rāgacaritāneva honti, na itare vā dosamohacaritā. Evaṃ dhātūnañca yathāvutteneva nayena ussadaniyamo nāma natthi. Dosaniyame ca rāgamohadvayameva vuttaṃ, tampi ca pubbāparaviruddhameva. Tasmā sabbametaṃ aparicchinnavacana”nti visuddhi. 1.44 paṭikkhitto. Taṃ paramatthamañjūsāya nāma visuddhimaggamahāṭīkāyaṃ “ekacceti upatissattheraṃ sandhāyāha, tena hi vimuttimagge tathā vutta”ntiādinā vaṇṇitaṃ visuddhi. ṭī. 1.44.

For there, in the explanation of conduct, it is said: “Here, some say that the three types of conduct are caused by past practice and by elements and humors. They say that one who was previously much given to pleasant application and good deeds becomes a lustful type, having passed away from a heavenly world and been reborn here. One who was previously much given to acts of cutting, killing, binding, and enmity becomes a hateful type, having passed away from the hells or the nāga-womb and been reborn here. One who was previously much given to drinking intoxicants and was deficient in listening and inquiry becomes a deluded type, having passed away from the animal womb and been reborn here. Thus they say it is caused by past practice. Further, they say that a person becomes a deluded type due to the predominance of two elements—the earth and water elements. Due to the predominance of the other two, one becomes a hateful type. When all are in equilibrium, one becomes a lustful type. And among the humors, one with a predominance of phlegm is a lustful type; one with a predominance of wind is a deluded type. Or one with a predominance of phlegm is a deluded type, and one with a predominance of wind is a lustful type. Thus they say it is caused by elements and humors.” Having presented this view of ‘some,’ it is refuted: “But since not all who were previously much given to pleasant application and good deeds, having passed away from a heavenly world and been reborn here, are necessarily lustful types—nor are the others necessarily hateful or deluded types—and since there is no fixed rule for the predominance of elements as described, and since in the rule of humors only lust and delusion are mentioned, which is itself internally contradictory, therefore all this is an indefinite statement.” This is explained in the Paramatthamañjūsā, the Great Subcommentary to the Visuddhimagga, as follows: “The phrase ‘some’ refers to the Elder Upatissa, for it is stated thus in the Vimuttimagga,” and so on.

Vimuttimaggapakaraṇaṃ

The Treatise on the Path of Liberation

Ko so vimuttimaggo nāma? Visuddhimaggo viya sīlasamādhipaññānaṃ visuṃ visuṃ vibhajitvā dīpako eko paṭipattigantho. Tattha hi –

What is this so-called Vimuttimagga? Like the Visuddhimagga, it is a single treatise on practice that illuminates by analyzing separately virtue, concentration, and wisdom. For therein—

“Sīlaṃ samādhi paññā ca, vimutti ca anuttarā;

Anubuddhā ime dhammā, gotamena yasassinā”ti dī. ni. 2.186; a. ni. 4.1 –

“Virtue, concentration, and wisdom, and unsurpassed liberation: these principles were realized by the glorious Gotama.”

Imaṃ [M 0.0041] gāthaṃ paṭhamaṃ dassetvā tadatthavaṇṇanāvasena sīlasamādhipaññāvimuttiyo visuṃ visuṃ vibhajitvā dīpitā. So pana gantho idāni cinaraṭṭheyeva diṭṭho, cinabhāsāya ca parivattito (1048-bu-va) saṅghapālena nāma bhikkhunā. Kena pana so kuto ca tattha ānītoti na pākaṭametaṃ. Tassa pana saṅghapālassa ācariyo guṇabhadro nāma mahāyāniko bhikkhu majjhimaindiyadesiko, so indiyaraṭṭhato cinaraṭṭhaṃ gacchanto paṭhamaṃ sīhaḷadīpaṃ gantvā tato (978-bu-va) cinaraṭṭhaṃ gato. Tadā so tena ānīto bhaveyya vimuttimagga, visuddhimagga.

Having first presented this verse, virtue, concentration, wisdom, and liberation are illuminated by analyzing them separately by way of an explanation of its meaning. That book, however, is now seen only in China, and it was translated into the Chinese language by a bhikkhu named Saṅghapāla (in B.E. 1048). But by whom and from where it was brought there is not clear. Saṅghapāla's teacher, however, was a Mahāyāna bhikkhu named Guṇabhadra, a native of Central India. While traveling from India to China, he first went to the island of Sri Lanka and from there went to China (in B.E. 978). It might have been brought by him at that time.

Tasmiñhi vimuttimagge pubbāciṇṇanidānadassanaṃ dhātunidānadassanañca yatheva visuddhimagge ekaccevādo, tathevāgataṃ. Dosanidānadassane pana “semhādhiko rāgacarito, pittādhiko dosacarito, vātādhiko mohacarito. Semhādhiko vā mohacarito, vātādhiko rāgacarito”ti tiṇṇampi rāgadosamohānaṃ dosaniyamo vutto. Ācariyabuddhaghosena diṭṭhavimuttimaggapotthake pana “pittādhiko dosacarito”ti pāṭho ūno bhaveyya.

For in that Vimuttimagga, the account of causation by past practice and the account of causation by the elements are presented just as in the view of 'some' in the Visuddhimagga. In the account of causation by the humors, however, it is said: “One with a predominance of phlegm is a lustful type; one with a predominance of bile is a hateful type; one with a predominance of wind is a deluded type. Or, one with a predominance of phlegm is a deluded type; one with a predominance of wind is a lustful type.” Thus the rule of the humors is stated for all three—lust, hatred, and delusion. However, in the copy of the Vimuttimagga seen by the Teacher Buddhaghosa, the reading “one with a predominance of bile is a hateful type” may have been missing.

Aññānipi bahūni visuddhimagge paṭikkhittāni tattha vimuttimagge gahetabbabhāvena dissanti. Kathaṃ?

Many other things rejected in the Visuddhimagga are seen in the Vimuttimagga as acceptable. How?

Sīlaniddese (1, 8-piṭṭhe) “aññe pana siraṭṭho sīlattho, sītalattho sīlatthoti evamādināpi nayenettha atthaṃ vaṇṇayantī”ti paṭikkhitto atthopi tattha gahetabbabhāvena dissati.

In the Exposition of Virtue (vol. 1, p. 8), the meaning that was rejected with the words, “But others explain the meaning here by such methods as: the meaning of sīla is ‘being a head’ (sira); the meaning of sīla is ‘being cool’ (sītala),” is also seen there as acceptable.

Tathā dhutaṅganiddese (1, 78-piṭṭhe) “yesampi kusalattikavinimuttaṃ dhutaṅgaṃ, tesaṃ atthato dhutaṅgameva natthi, asantaṃ kassa dhunanato dhutaṅgaṃ nāma bhavissati, dhutaguṇe samādāya vattatīti vacanavirodhopi ca nesaṃ āpajjati, tasmā taṃ na gahetabba”nti paṭikkhittaṃ paññattidhutaṅgampi tattha dissati. Mahāṭīkāyaṃ (1-104) pana “yesanti abhayagirivāsike sandhāyāha, te hi dhutaṅgaṃ nāma paññattīti vadantī”ti vaṇṇitaṃ.

Likewise, in the Exposition of the Ascetic Practices (vol. 1, p. 78), the dhutaṅga as a mere concept, which was rejected with the words, “For those for whom the dhutaṅga is dissociated from the triad of wholesome states, for them there is in reality no dhutaṅga at all. Since it is non-existent, by the shaking off of what will it be a dhutaṅga? Moreover, for them this entails a contradiction with the statement, ‘One lives having undertaken the quality of shaking off.’ Therefore that should not be accepted,” is also seen there. But in the Great Subcommentary (1-104) it is explained: “By ‘for those’ he refers to the Abhayagirivāsins, for they say that a dhutaṅga is a concept.”

Tathā pathavīkasiṇaniddese (1, 144) “paṭipadāvisuddhi nāma sasambhāriko upacāro, upekkhānubrūhanā nāma appanā, sampahaṃsanā nāma [M 0.0042] paccavekkhaṇāti evameke vaṇṇayantī”tiādinā paṭikkhittaekevādopi tattha dissati. Mahāṭīkāyaṃ (1, 172) pana “eketi abhayagirivāsino”ti vaṇṇitaṃ.

Likewise, in the Exposition of the Earth Kasiṇa (vol. 1, p. 144), the view of 'some,' which was rejected with such words as, “Some explain it thus: ‘Purity of the path is access concentration with its preliminaries; development of equanimity is absorption; gladdening is reviewing,’” is also seen there. But in the Great Subcommentary (1, 172) it is explained: “By ‘some’ is meant the Abhayagirivāsins.”

Tathā khandhaniddese (2, 80-piṭṭhe) “balarūpaṃ sambhavarūpaṃ jātirūpaṃ rogarūpaṃ ekaccānaṃ matena middharūpa”nti evaṃ aññānipi rūpāni āharitvā porāṇaṭṭhakathāyaṃ tesaṃ paṭikkhittabhāvo pakāsito. Mahāṭīkāyaṃ “ekaccānanti abhayagirivāsīna”nti vaṇṇitaṃ. Tesu jātirūpaṃ middharūpañca vimuttimagge dassitaṃ. Na kevalaṃ dassanamattameva, atha kho middharūpassa atthibhāvopi “middhaṃ nāma tividhaṃ āhārajaṃ utujaṃ cittajañcāti. Tesu cittajameva nīvaraṇaṃ hoti, sesā pana dve arahatopi bhaveyyu”ntiādinā sādhito.

Likewise, in the Exposition of the Aggregates (vol. 2, p. 80), having adduced other material forms thus: “strength-form, continuance-form, birth-form, disease-form, and, in the opinion of some, torpor-form,” their rejection in the ancient commentaries is clarified. In the Great Subcommentary it is explained: “By ‘some’ is meant the Abhayagirivāsins.” Of these, birth-form and torpor-form are shown in the Vimuttimagga. It is not a mere mention; rather, the existence of torpor-form is established with such words as: “Torpor is of three kinds: that born of food, that born of climate, and that born of consciousness. Of these, only that born of consciousness is a hindrance; the other two can occur even in an arahant.”
