Ettāvatā ca vimuttimagge visuddhimaggena asamānatthānaṃ vuttabhāvo ca abhayagirivāsīhi tassa ganthassa paṭiggahitabhāvo ca sakkā ñātuṃ. Aññānipi pana īdisāni asamānavacanāni bahūni tattha saṃvijjantiyeva, tāni pana sabbāni na sakkā idha dassetuṃ.

By this much it can be known that points dissimilar to the Visuddhimagga have been stated in the Vimuttimagga and that this book was accepted by the Abhayagirivāsins. Moreover, many other such dissimilar statements are indeed found there, but it is not possible to show all of them here.

Yebhuyyena panassa karaṇappakāro visuddhimaggassa viya hoti. Yā yā hi pāḷi abhidhammavibhaṅgato vā paṭisambhidāmaggato vā aññasuttantehi vā ānetvā sādhakabhāvena visuddhimagge dassiyati, tatthapi sā sā pāḷi yebhuyyena dissateva. Tāsu kañcimattaṃ uddharitvā anuminanatthāya dassayissāma.

For the most part, however, its manner of composition is like that of the Visuddhimagga. For whatever Pāli texts are brought from the Vibhaṅga of the Abhidhamma, the Paṭisambhidāmagga, or other suttas and shown in the Visuddhimagga as proof, those same Pāli texts are for the most part seen there as well. We will extract a small number of them and present them for the purpose of inference.

Yā visuddhimagge (1, 47-piṭṭhe) “pañca sīlāni pāṇātipātassa pahānaṃ sīla”ntiādikā paṭisambhidāmaggapāḷi dassitā, sā vimuttimaggepi dissateva.

The Pāli text from the Paṭisambhidāmagga shown in the Visuddhimagga (vol. 1, p. 47), beginning, “The five kinds of virtue: the abandoning of the destruction of life is virtue…,” is also seen in the Vimuttimagga.

Yañca visuddhimagge (1, 137-piṭṭhe) “samādhi kāmacchandassa paṭipakkho…pe… vicāro vicikicchāyā”ti vacanaṃ peṭake vuttanti dassitaṃ, tañca tatthapi tatheva dassetvā “tipeṭake vutta”nti niddiṭṭhaṃ. Tipeṭaketi nāmañca peṭakopadesameva sandhāya vuttaṃ bhaveyya. Tattha hi vivicceva kāmehīti pāṭhasaṃvaṇṇanāyaṃ “alobhassa pāripūriyā kāmehi viveko sampajjati, adosassa. Amohassa pāripūriyā akusalehi dhammehi viveko [M 0.0043] sampajjatī”ti pāṭhassa tipeṭake vuttabhāvo dassito. So ca pāṭho peṭakopadese (262-piṭṭhe) “tattha alobhassa pāripūriyā vivitto hoti kāmehī”tiādinā dissati.

And the statement in the Visuddhimagga (vol. 1, p. 137), “Concentration is the opponent of sensual desire … sustained thought is the opponent of doubt,” which is shown as being stated in the Peṭaka, is also shown in that text in the same way and is designated as “stated in the Tipiṭaka.” The name “Tipiṭaka” may have been stated with reference to the Peṭakopadesa itself. For there, in the explanation of the text “quite secluded from sensual pleasures,” it is shown that the passage, “Through the fulfillment of non-greed, seclusion from sensual pleasures is accomplished; through the fulfillment of non-hatred and non-delusion, seclusion from unwholesome states is accomplished,” is stated in the Tipiṭaka. And that passage is seen in the Peṭakopadesa (p. 262) as, “There, through the fulfillment of non-greed, one becomes secluded from sensual pleasures,” and so on.

Yathā ca visuddhimagge (1, 258-piṭṭhe) “ayampi kho bhikkhave ānāpānassatisamādhi bhāvito”tiādikā pāḷi mahāvaggasaṃyuttakato ānetvā dassitā, tatheva tatthapi.

And just as in the Visuddhimagga (vol. 1, p. 258) the Pāli text beginning, “This concentration by mindfulness of breathing, bhikkhus, when developed…” is brought from the Mahāvagga Saṃyutta and shown, so too is it in that text.

Yathā ca visuddhimagge (1, 272-piṭṭhe) “assāsādimajjhapariyosānaṃ satiyā anugacchato”tiādi pāḷi ca (1, 273-piṭṭhe) kakacūpamapāḷi ca paṭisambhidāmaggato ānetvā dassitā, tatheva tatthapi.

And just as in the Visuddhimagga (vol. 1, p. 272) the Pāli text beginning, “following with mindfulness the beginning, middle, and end of the in-breath,” etc., and (on p. 273) the Pāli text on the Simile of the Saw are shown, having been brought from the Paṭisambhidāmagga, so too it is in that text.

Yathā ca visuddhimagge (2, 69-piṭṭhe) “katamā cintāmayā paññā”tiādikā ca pāḷi “tattha katamaṃ āyakosalla”ntiādikā ca pāḷi (2, 71-piṭṭhe) “dukkhe ñāṇaṃ atthapaṭisambhidā”tiādikā ca pāḷi abhidhammavibhaṅgato ānetvā dassitā, tatheva tatthapi. Sabbāpi ca tattha vuttā ekavidhaduvidhādipaññāpabhedakathā visuddhimagge vuttakathāya yebhuyyena samānāyeva.

Similarly, in the Visuddhimagga (vol. 2, p. 69), the Pāli text beginning, “What is wisdom born of reflection?” etc., the Pāli text beginning, “Therein, what is skill in progress?” etc., and (vol. 2, p. 71) the Pāli text beginning, “Knowledge of suffering is the analytical knowledge of meaning,” etc., are shown, having been brought from the Abhidhammavibhaṅga; so too it is in that text. Moreover, all the discussions stated there on the classifications of wisdom into onefold, twofold, etc., are for the most part the same as the discussions found in the Visuddhimagga.

“Yena cakkhupasādena, rūpāni manupassati;

Parittaṃ sukhumaṃ etaṃ, ūkāsirasamūpama”nti visuddhi. 2.436; dha. sa. aṭṭha. 596 –

“With the eye's sensitive faculty, one perceives forms; this is tiny and subtle, like the head of a louse.”

Ayampi gāthā vimuttimaggepi āyasmatā sāriputtattherena bhāsitabhāveneva dassitā. Ūkāsirasamūpamanti padaṃ pana ūkāsamūpamanti tattha dissati, tañca paramparalekhakānaṃ pamādalekhamattameva siyā.

This verse is also shown in the Vimuttimagga as having been spoken by the Venerable Elder Sāriputta. However, the phrase 'like the head of a louse' (ūkāsirasamūpam) appears there as 'like a louse' (ūkāsamūpam), which may simply be a scribal error in the transmission.

Catūsu saccesu visuddhimagge viya vacanatthato lakkhaṇato anūnādhikato kamato antogadhānaṃ pabhedato upamāto ca vinicchayo dassito, so ca yebhuyyena visuddhimaggena visuddhi. 2.530 samānoyeva.

Regarding the four truths, an analysis is presented, as in the Visuddhimagga, in terms of the meaning of words, characteristics, non-deficiency and non-excess, sequence, inclusion, divisions of the things included, and similes; and this is largely the same as in the Visuddhimagga.

Yathā ca visuddhimagge (2, 242-245) sammasanañāṇakathāyaṃ pañcannaṃ khandhānaṃ atītādiekādasavidhena ca aniccādilakkhaṇattayena ca visuṃ visuṃ sammasananayo dassito, tatheva tatthapi. Cakkhādijarāmaraṇapariyosānesu pana dhammesu dhammavicārapariyosānānaṃ saṭṭhiyā eva dhammānaṃ aniccādilakkhaṇattayena sammasananayo tattha dassito.

Just as in the Visuddhimagga (vol. 2, pp. 242–245), in the discussion on the knowledge of comprehension, the method of comprehending the five aggregates individually through the eleven aspects such as the past, and through the three characteristics such as impermanence, is shown, so too it is in that text. However, regarding the phenomena beginning with the eye and ending with aging and death, the method of comprehending the sixty phenomena that end with sustained thought on phenomena (dhammavicāra) through the three characteristics such as impermanence is shown there.

Visuddhimagge [M 0.0044] pana diṭṭhivisuddhiniddese (2, 230-232-piṭṭhesu) vuttā “yamakaṃ nāmarūpañca…pe… ubho bhijjanti paccayā”ti gāthā ca, “na cakkhuto jāyare”tiādikā cha gāthāyo ca, “na sakena balena jāyare”tiādikā cha gāthāyo ca vimuttimagge bhaṅgānupassanāñāṇakathāyaṃ dassitā. Tāsu appamattakoyeva pāṭhabhedo dissati.

The verse 'Name-and-form is a pair … both are broken up by their conditions,' and the six verses beginning 'They are not born from the eye,' and the six verses beginning 'They are not born by their own power'—which are stated in the Visuddhimagga in the exposition of the purification of view (vol. 2, pp. 230–232)—are shown in the Vimuttimagga in the discussion on the knowledge of contemplation of dissolution. Only slight textual variations are seen among them.

Visuddhimagge (2, 261-2-piṭṭhesu) arūpasattakesu ariyavaṃsakathānayena vutto kalāpato ca yamakato ca sammasananayo vimuttimagge ettheva bhaṅgānupassanāñāṇakathāyaṃ dassito.

The method of comprehension by groups and by pairs—stated in the Visuddhimagga (vol. 2, pp. 261–62) with regard to the sevenfold non-material in the manner of the discourse on the Noble Lineages—is shown right here in the Vimuttimagga in the discussion on the knowledge of contemplation of dissolution.

Vimuttimagge buddhānussatikathāyaṃ lokavidūti padassa atthavaṇṇanāyaṃ sattalokasaṅkhāralokavasena dveyeva lokā dassitā, na pana okāsaloko yathā visuddhimagge (1, 199-200-piṭṭhesu).

In the Vimuttimagga, in the discussion on the recollection of the Buddha, in the explanation of the meaning of the term 'Knower of Worlds,' only two worlds are shown, namely, the world of beings and the world of formations, but not the world of space as in the Visuddhimagga (vol. 1, pp. 199–200).

Ettāvatā ca vimuttimaggo nāma gantho kīdisoti sakkā anuminituṃ. So pana yathā na mahāvihāravāsīnaṃ gantho hoti, evaṃ mahāyānikānampi na hotiyeva theravādapiṭakameva nissāya katabhāvato. Yasmā pana tattha na kiñcipi sīhaḷadīpikaṃ nāmaṃ vā theravādo vā dissati, tasmā so sīhaḷadīpe kataganthopi na hoti. Indiyaraṭṭhikaṃ pana nāmañca vohāro ca tattha bahūsu ṭhānesu dissati, tasmā indiyaraṭṭhe kataganthova bhaveyya. Yasmā cassa peṭakopadesaṃ nissitabhāvo bahūsu ṭhānesu dissati, visesato pana middharūpassa atthibhāvo ca, arahatopi tassa atthibhāvo ca tameva nissāya dassīyati, paṭisambhidāmaggagaṇṭhipade ca peṭaketi padassa paṭi. ma. aṭṭha. 1.1.36 atthavaṇṇanāyaṃ “suttantapiṭakatthāya aṭṭhakathā peṭakaṃ mahisāsakānaṃ gantho”ti vaṇṇito. Tasmā eso vimuttimaggo mahisāsakanikāyikena kato bhaveyyāti amhākaṃ mati.

From this, it can be inferred what kind of text this Vimuttimagga is. Just as it is not a text belonging to the residents of the Mahāvihāra, so too it is certainly not one of the Mahāyānists, since it was composed relying only on the Theravāda Piṭaka. However, because no name such as 'Sīhaḷadīpika' or 'Theravāda' is found in it, it is not a text composed on the island of Sri Lanka. On the other hand, an Indian name and usage are seen in many places in it; therefore, it must be a text composed in the country of India. Furthermore, its reliance on the Peṭakopadesa is seen in many places; especially, the existence of torpor-as-form, and its existence even for an arahant, are shown in reliance upon that very text. And in the glossary to the Paṭisambhidāmagga, in the explanation of the meaning of the word 'peṭaka,' it is explained: 'The Peṭaka, the commentary for the meaning of the Suttanta Piṭaka, is a text of the Mahīśāsakas.' Therefore, it is our opinion that this Vimuttimagga must have been composed by a member of the Mahīśāsaka school.

Nissayaṭṭhakathāvibhāvanā

An Explanation of the Source Commentaries

Visuddhimaggo pana na kevalaṃ pubbe vuttappakāreneva kato, atha kho vuccamānappakārenāpi. Tathā hi ācariyabuddhaghosatthero porāṇaṭṭhakathāhi samāharitvā bhāsāparivattanavasena dassentopi yā yā atthavaṇṇanā [M 0.0045] vā vinicchayo vā saṃsayitabbo hoti, tattha tattha vinayaṭṭhakathāyaṃ vuttanti vā (1, 263), vinayaṭṭhakathāsu vuttaṃ, majjhimaṭṭhakathāsu panāti vā (1, 70), aṅguttarabhāṇakāti vā (1, 72), aṭṭhakathācariyānaṃ matānusārena vinicchayoti vā (1, 99), vuttampi cetaṃ aṭṭhakathāsūti vā (1, 118), taṃ aṭṭhakathāsu paṭikkhittanti vā (1, 134), dīghabhāṇakasaṃyuttabhāṇakānaṃ matanti vā, majjhimabhāṇakā icchantīti vā (1, 267), aṭṭhakathāsu vinicchayoti vā, evaṃ tāva dīghabhāṇakā, majjhimabhāṇakā panāhūti vā (1, 277), aṅguttaraṭṭhakathāyaṃ pana…pe… ayaṃ kamo vutto, so pāḷiyā na sametīti vā (1, 309), evaṃ tāva majjhimabhāṇakā, saṃyuttabhāṇakā panāti vā (2, 62), saṃyuttaṭṭhakathāyaṃ vuttanti vā (2, 63), aṭṭhakathāyaṃ panāti vā (2, 80) evaṃ taṃtaṃatthavaṇṇanāvinicchayānaṃ nissayampi vibhāvetvā pacchimajanānaṃ uppajjamānasaṃsayaṃ vinodentoyeva te dassesi.

The Visuddhimagga, however, was composed not only in the manner previously described, but also in the manner now being explained. For the Teacher, the Venerable Buddhaghosa, while compiling from the ancient commentaries and presenting them by way of a change of language, whenever there was any explanation of meaning or analysis about which doubt might arise, he would present them while dispelling the doubts that might arise for later generations by making clear the source for those various explanations and analyses, indicating in each case: “as stated in the Vinaya commentary” (1:263), or “as stated in the Vinaya commentaries, but in the Majjhima commentaries…” (1:70), or “the Aṅguttara reciters” (1:72), or “the analysis is according to the opinion of the commentary teachers” (1:99), or “this also is stated in the commentaries” (1:118), or “that is rejected in the commentaries” (1:134), or “the opinion of the Dīgha reciters and Saṃyutta reciters,” or “the Majjhima reciters hold…” (1:267), or “the analysis in the commentaries,” or “thus far the Dīgha reciters, but the Majjhima reciters say…” (1:277), or “in the Aṅguttara commentary, however… this order is stated, but it does not accord with the Pāli” (1:309), or “thus far the Majjhima reciters, but the Saṃyutta reciters say…” (2:62), or “as stated in the Saṃyutta commentary” (2:63), or “but in the commentary…” (2:80).

Tenimassa visuddhimaggassa karaṇakāle sabbāpi sīhaḷaṭṭhakathāyo ācariyassa santike santīti ca, pubbeyeva tā ācariyena sīhaḷattherānaṃ santike sutāti ca, tāhi gahetabbaṃ sabbaṃ gahetvā ayaṃ visuddhimaggo ācariyena likhitoti ca ayamattho ativiya pākaṭo hoti. Tasmā yaṃ mahāvaṃse –

By this it becomes exceedingly clear that at the time of the composition of this Visuddhimagga, all the Sinhala commentaries were in the Teacher’s presence; that they had been heard by the Teacher beforehand from the Sinhala elders; and that this Visuddhimagga was written by the Teacher after he had taken from them everything that was to be taken. Therefore, as for what is stated in the Mahāvaṃsa—

“Saṅgho gāthādvayaṃ tassā’dāsi sāmatthiyaṃ tavā”tiādinā “gāthādvayameva oloketvā kiñcipi aññaṃ potthakaṃ anoloketvā ācariyabuddhaghoso visuddhimaggaṃ akāsī”ti adhippāyena abhitthutivacanaṃ vuttaṃ, taṃ abhitthutimattamevāti veditabbaṃ.

“The Saṅgha gave him two verses [saying], ‘[show] your ability,’” and so on—this statement of praise, which was said with the intention that “the Teacher Buddhaghosa composed the Visuddhimagga having looked only at those two verses and not at any other book,” should be understood as being merely a statement of praise.

Porāṇavacanadassanaṃ

Citing the Sayings of the Ancients

Na kevalaṃ ācariyo aṭṭhakathāyoyeva nissayabhāvena dassesi, atha kho “porāṇā panāhū”tiādinā porāṇānaṃ vacanampi dassesiyeva. Tadettha dvāvīsatiyā ṭhānesu diṭṭhaṃ visuddhi. 1.15, 137, 142, 217, 252, 303; 2.581, 675-676, 689, 706, 736, 745, 746, 749,755, 778, 839. Ke panete porāṇā nāma? Yāva catutthasaṅgītikālā saṅgītikāresu pariyāpannā vā [M 0.0046] tādisā vā mahātherāti veditabbā. Tathā hi paṭisambhidāmagge (292-3-piṭṭhesu).

The Teacher did not only cite the commentaries as his authority, but he also cited the sayings of the ancients, with such phrases as, 'But the ancients say….' This is seen in twenty-two places. Who, now, are these ancients? They should be understood as the great elders who were included among the reciters at the councils up to the time of the Fourth Council, or others like them. For so it is in the Paṭisambhidāmagga (pages 292-3):

“Obhāse ceva ñāṇe ca, pītiyā ca vikampati…pe…

Dhammuddhaccakusalo hoti, na ca vikkhepaṃ gacchatī”ti –

‘He wavers amidst light and knowledge, and amidst rapture… (and so on) … He is skilled in spiritual excitement about the Dhamma and does not become distracted.’

Evamāgatā gāthāyo idha (273-4-piṭṭhesu) porāṇānaṃ vacanabhāvena dassitā. Yadi cimā gāthāyo saṅgītikārehi pakkhittā bhaveyyuṃ yathā parivārapāḷiyaṃ (3-piṭṭhe) āgatā ācariyaparamparādīpikā gāthāyo, tā hi samantapāsādikāyaṃ (1, 46-piṭṭhe) porāṇavacanabhāvena dassitā, evaṃ sati teyeva saṅgītikārā porāṇāti veditabbā. Atha paṭisambhidāmaggadesakeneva bhāsitā bhaveyyuṃ, te viya garukaraṇīyā paccayikā saddhāyitabbakā mahātherā porāṇāti veditabbā. Samantapāsādikāsumaṅgalavilāsinīādīsu “porāṇā pana evaṃ vaṇṇayantī”tiādinā vuttaṭṭhānesupi tādisāva ācariyā porāṇāti vuttā.

The verses that have come down in this way are presented here (on pages 273-4) as the sayings of the ancients. If these verses were inserted by the reciters of the canon—just as the verses clarifying the succession of teachers found in the Parivārapāḷi (on page 3) are presented in the Samantapāsādikā (on pages 1, 46) as the sayings of the ancients—then those very reciters of the canon should be understood as 'the ancients.' Alternatively, if they were spoken by the expounder of the Paṭisambhidāmagga himself, then great elders like him—worthy of respect, reliable, and trustworthy—should be understood as 'the ancients.' In the Samantapāsādikā, Sumaṅgalavilāsinī, and other commentaries, in passages where it is said, 'But the ancients explain it thus,' and so on, it is such teachers who are called 'the ancients.'

Vinayaṭṭhakathākaraṇaṃ

The Composition of the Vinaya Commentary

Ācariyo pana imaṃ visuddhimaggapakaraṇaṃ yathāvuttappakārena katvā aññāpi tipiṭakaṭṭhakathāyo anukkamena akāsi. Kathaṃ? Samantapāsādikaṃ nāma vinayaṭṭhakathaṃ buddhasirittherena ajjhesito mahāvihārassa dakkhiṇabhāge padhānagharapariveṇe mahānigamassāmino pāsāde vasanto akāsi. Sā panesā siripāloti nāmantarassa mahānāmarañño vīsatimavasse (973-bu-va) āraddhā ekavīsatimavasse (974-bu-va) niṭṭhānappattā ahosi. Tañca pana karonto mahāmahindattherenābhataṃ sīhaḷabhāsāya saṅkhataṃ mahāaṭṭhakathaṃ tassā sarīraṃ katvā mahāpaccarīkurundīsaṅkhepaandhakaṭṭhakathāhi ca gahetabbaṃ gahetvā sīhaḷadīpe yāva vasabharājakālā pākaṭānaṃ porāṇa vinayadharamahātherānaṃ vinicchayabhūtaṃ theravādampi pakkhipitvā akāsi. Vuttañhetaṃ samantapāsādikāyaṃ –

The Teacher, having composed this treatise, the Visuddhimagga, in the manner described, then composed the other Tipiṭaka commentaries in succession. How so? At the request of the Elder Buddhasiri, he composed the Vinaya commentary named the Samantapāsādikā while dwelling in the mansion of the lord of Mahānigama, located in the Padhānaghara Pariveṇa in the southern part of the Mahāvihāra. This work was begun in the twentieth year of King Mahānāma, who had the other name Siripāla (973 B.E.), and was completed in his twenty-first year (974 B.E.). In composing it, he took as its 'body' the Great Commentary (Mahā-aṭṭhakathā), which was brought by the Elder Mahāmahinda and composed in the Sinhala language. He extracted what was to be taken from the Mahāpaccarī, the Kurundī, the Saṅkhepa, and the Andhakaṭṭhakathā, and also included the Theravāda tradition, which was the definitive judgment of the ancient great elders, masters of the Vinaya, who were renowned in the island of Sri Lanka up to the time of King Vasabha. For this is stated in the Samantapāsādikā:

“Saṃvaṇṇanaṃ tañca samārabhanto, tassā mahāaṭṭhakathaṃ sarīraṃ;

Katvā mahāpaccariyaṃ tatheva, kurundināmādisu vissutāsu.

‘Undertaking this commentary, having made the Mahā-aṭṭhakathā its body, and likewise from the renowned commentaries such as the Mahāpaccarī and the Kurundī,

Vinicchayo [M 0.0047] aṭṭhakathāsu vutto, yo yuttamatthaṃ apariccajanto;

Tatopi antogadhatheravādaṃ, saṃvaṇṇanaṃ samma samārabhissa”nti ca.

[I shall take] the decision stated in those commentaries, which does not abandon the proper meaning, and also the Theravāda view contained therein. Thus I shall properly commence this commentary.’

“Mahāmeghavanuyyāne, bhūmibhāge patiṭṭhito;

Mahāvihāro yo satthu, mahābodhivibhūsito.

‘The Great Monastery of the Teacher, adorned by the Great Bodhi tree, which is established on a tract of land in the Mahāmeghavana Park;

Yaṃ tassa dakkhiṇe bhāge, padhānagharamuttamaṃ;

Sucicārittasīlena, bhikkhusaṅghena sevitaṃ.

In the southern part of which is an excellent meditation hall, frequented by the Sangha of bhikkhus pure in conduct and virtue;

Uḷārakulasambhūto, saṅghupaṭṭhāyako sadā;

Anākulāya saddhāya, pasanno ratanattaye.

One born of a noble family, a constant supporter of the Sangha, with untroubled faith, devoted to the Three Jewels,

Mahānigamasāmīti, vissuto tattha kārayi;

Cārupākārasañcitaṃ, yaṃ pāsādaṃ manoramaṃ.

Renowned as the lord of Mahānigama, had built there a delightful mansion enclosed by a beautiful wall;

Sandacchāyatarūpetaṃ, sampannasalilāsayaṃ;

Vasatā tatra pāsāde, mahānigamasāmino.

Endowed with dense, shady trees and possessing a reservoir of water—while I was dwelling in that mansion of the lord of Mahānigama,

Sucisīlasamācāraṃ, theraṃ buddhasirivhayaṃ;

Yā uddisitvā āraddhā, iddhā vinayavaṇṇanā.

At the request of the Elder named Buddhasiri, a man of pure virtue and conduct, this splendid commentary on the Vinaya was begun;

Pālayantassa sakalaṃ, laṅkādīpaṃ nirabbudaṃ;

Rañño sirinivāsassa siriyā nivāsaṭṭhonabhūtassa siripālanāmakassa rañño (vimati, antimaviṭṭhe), siripālayasassino.

While the glorious King Siripāla, the Abode of Fortune, was ruling the entire island of Laṅkā, keeping it free from affliction;

Samavīsatime vasse, jayasaṃvacchare ayaṃ;

Āraddhā ekavīsamhi, sampatte pariniṭṭhitā.

This work was begun in his twentieth year, the Victory Year, and was fully completed upon the arrival of the twenty-first.

Upaddavākule loke, nirupaddavato ayaṃ;

Ekasaṃvacchareneva, yathā niṭṭhaṃ upāgatā”ti pari. aṭṭha. nigamanakathā ca.

In a world beset by troubles, this work is free from trouble; thus it has reached completion in just a single year.’

Ayañca samantapāsādikā vinayaṭṭhakathā adhunā mudditachaṭṭhasaṅgītipotthakavasena sahassato upari aṭṭhapaṇṇāsādhikatisatamattapiṭṭhaparimāṇā (1358) hoti, tassā ca ekasaṃvaccharena niṭṭhāpitattaṃ upanidhāya catuvīsādhikasattasatamattapiṭṭhaparimāṇo (724) visuddhimaggopi antamaso chappañcamāsehi niṭṭhāpito bhaveyyāti sakkā ñātuṃ. Tasmā yaṃ buddhaghosuppattiyaṃ mahāvaṃsavacanaṃ nissāya “visuddhimaggo ācariyabuddhaghosena [M 0.0048] ekaratteneva tikkhattuṃ likhitvā niṭṭhāpito”ti abhitthutivacanaṃ vuttaṃ, taṃ takkārakassa abhitthutimattamevāti veditabbaṃ.

This Vinaya commentary, the Samantapāsādikā, in the current printed Sixth Council edition, has a length of 1358 pages. Considering that it was completed within a single year, one can infer that the Visuddhimagga, which is 724 pages long, could have been completed in at least five or six months. Therefore, the laudatory statement in the Buddhaghosuppatti, which relies on a saying from the Mahāvaṃsa, that 'the Visuddhimagga was written out three times in a single night by the Teacher Buddhaghosa and thus completed,' should be understood as being merely praise for its author.

Nanu ca imissaṃ aṭṭhakathāyaṃ “sumaṅgalavilāsiniya”ntiādinā visesanāmavasena āgamaṭṭhakathānaṃ atideso dissati pārā. aṭṭha. 1.15, kathamimissā tāhi paṭhamataraṃ katabhāvo veditabboti? Ācariyassa aṭṭhakathāsu aññamaññātidesato, vinayapiṭakassa garukātabbatarabhāvato, mahāvihāravāsīhi visesena garukatabhāvato, saṅgītikkamānurūpabhāvato, idheva paripuṇṇanidānakathāpakāsanato, nigamane ca paṭhamaṃ sīhaḷaṭṭhakathāyo sutvā karaṇappakāsanato ṭhapetvā visuddhimaggaṃ ayameva paṭhamaṃ katāti veditabbā. Visuddhimagge pana vinayaṭṭhakathāyanti vā vinayaṭṭhakathāsūti vā majjhimaṭṭhakathāsūti vā evaṃ sāmaññanāmavaseneva atideso dissati, na samantapāsādikādivisesanāmavasena. Tasmāssa sabbapaṭhamaṃ katabhāvo pākaṭoyeva. Āgamaṭṭhakathānaṃ idhātideso pārā. aṭṭha. 1.15 imissāpi tatthāti dī. ni. aṭṭha. 1.8 evaṃ aññamaññātideso pana ācariyassa manasā suvavatthitavasena vā sakkā bhavituṃ, apubbācarimapariniṭṭhāpanena vā. Kathaṃ? Ācariyena hi visuddhimaggaṃ sabbaso niṭṭhāpetvā samantapāsādikādiṃ ekekamaṭṭhakathaṃ karonteneva yattha yattha atthavaṇṇanā vitthārato aññaṭṭhakathāsu pakāsetabbā hoti, tattha tattha “imasmiṃ nāma ṭhāne kathessāmī”ti manasā suvavatthitaṃ vavatthapetvā tañca atidisitvā yathāvavatthitaṭhānappattakāle taṃ vitthārato kathentena tā katā vā bhaveyyuṃ. Ekekissāya vā niṭṭhānāsannappattakāle taṃ ṭhapetvā aññañca aññañca tathā katvā sabbāpi apubbācarimaṃ pariniṭṭhāpitā bhaveyyunti evaṃ dvinnaṃ pakārānamaññataravasena ācariyassāṭṭhakathāsu aññamaññātideso hotīti veditabbanti.

But is it not so that in this commentary a cross-reference to the Nikāya commentaries is seen by way of specific names, such as 'Sumaṅgalavilāsinī'? How then can it be known that this work was composed before them? It should be known that, apart from the Visuddhimagga, this was indeed the first one composed for the following reasons: the Teacher's mutual cross-referencing in his commentaries; the greater reverence accorded to the Vinaya Piṭaka; the special reverence given to it by the residents of the Mahāvihāra; its conformity with the order of the councils; the fact that the full introductory story is set forth right here; and the statement in the colophon that he composed it after first hearing the Sinhala commentaries. In the Visuddhimagga, however, cross-references are seen only by way of general names, such as 'the Vinaya commentary' or 'in the Majjhima commentary,' not by specific names like 'Samantapāsādikā.' Therefore, it is perfectly clear that it was composed first of all. As for the mutual cross-referencing—that is, the cross-reference here to the Nikāya commentaries and of this one in those—this could be possible either due to the Teacher's well-established mental plan or due to completing them in a non-sequential order. How so? It might be that the Teacher, having completed the Visuddhimagga in its entirety, then, while composing each commentary such as the Samantapāsādikā, whenever an explanation had to be set forth in detail in another commentary, he would establish a firm mental resolve, 'I will discuss this at such-and-such a place.' Then, having made the cross-reference, when he reached the pre-determined place he would explain it in detail, and in this way they were composed. Or, as each one neared completion, he might have set it aside and worked on another and another in the same way, and thus all of them were completed non-sequentially. Thus, it should be understood that the mutual cross-referencing in the Teacher's commentaries occurs in one of these two ways.

Āgamaṭṭhakathākaraṇaṃ

The Composition of the Commentaries on the Nikāyas

Sumaṅgalavilāsiniṃ nāma dīghanikāyaṭṭhakathaṃ pana ācariyo sumaṅgalapariveṇavāsinā dāṭhānāgattherena āyācito akāsi. Vuttaṃ hetametissā nigamane –

The Teacher composed the commentary on the Dīgha Nikāya, named the Sumaṅgalavilāsinī, at the request of the Elder Dāṭhānāga, a resident of the Sumaṅgala monastery. It is stated thus at its conclusion:

“Āyācito [M 0.0049] sumaṅgala-pariveṇanivāsinā thiraguṇena;

Dāṭhānāga saṅgha, ttherena theravaṃsanvayena.

“Requested by the Elder named Dāṭhānāga, a senior elder of the Order belonging to the lineage of elders [from Mahinda onwards], of steadfast virtue, a resident of the Sumaṅgala monastery;

Dīghāgamassa dasabala-guṇagaṇaparidīpanassa aṭṭhakathaṃ;

Yaṃ ārabhiṃ sumaṅgala-vilāsiniṃ nāma nāmena.

I have endeavored to compose the commentary on the Dīgha Āgama, which elucidates the host of qualities of the Ten-Powered One, the work named Sumaṅgalavilāsinī.

Sā hi mahāaṭṭhakathāya, sāramādāya niṭṭhitā esā”ti dī. ni. aṭṭha. 3. nigamanakathā.

This new commentary is now completed, having extracted the essence from the Great Commentary.”

Papañcasūdaniṃ nāma majjhimanikāyaṭṭhakathaṃ bhadantabuddhamittattherena pubbe mayūradūtapaṭṭane attanā saddhiṃ vasantena āyācito akāsi. Vuttaṃ hetametissā nigamane –

He composed the commentary on the Majjhima Nikāya, named the Papañcasūdanī, at the request of the venerable Elder Buddhamitta, who was formerly dwelling with him at the port of Mayūradūta. It is stated thus at its conclusion:

“Āyācito sumatinā, therena bhadantabuddhamittena;

Pubbe mayūradūtapa,ṭṭanamhi saddhiṃ vasantena.

“Requested by the wise and venerable Elder Buddhamitta, who was formerly dwelling with me at the port of Mayūradūta;

Paravādavidhaṃsanassa, majjhimanikāyaseṭṭhassa;

Yamahaṃ papañcasūdani-maṭṭhakathaṃ kātumārabhiṃ.

I began to compose the commentary, the Papañcasūdanī, for the excellent Majjhima Nikāya, the destroyer of rival doctrines.

Sā hi mahāaṭṭhakathāya, sāramādāya niṭṭhitā esā”ti ma. ni. aṭṭha. 3. nigamanakathā.

This new commentary is now completed, having extracted the essence from the Great Commentary.”

Sāratthappakāsiniṃ nāma saṃyuttanikāyaṭṭhakathaṃ bhadantajotipālattherena āyācito akāsi. Vuttaṃ hetametissā nigamane –

He composed the commentary on the Saṃyutta Nikāya, named the Sāratthappakāsinī, at the request of the venerable Elder Jotipāla. It is stated thus at its conclusion:

“Etissā karaṇatthaṃ, therena bhadantajotipālena;

Sucisīlena subhāsitassa pakāsayantañāṇena.

“By the venerable Elder Jotipāla, of pure virtue, with wisdom that illuminates the well-spoken;

Sāsanavibhūtikāmena, yācamānena maṃ subhaguṇena;

Yaṃ samadhigataṃ puññaṃ, tenāpi jano sukhī bhavatū”ti saṃ. ni. aṭṭha. 3.5.nigamanakathā.

desiring the flourishing of the Dispensation, endowed with beautiful qualities, I was requested to compose this. Whatever merit has been acquired thereby, may all people be happy.”

Manorathapūraṇiṃ nāma aṅguttaranikāyaṭṭhakathaṃ bhadantajotipālattherena dakkhiṇaindiyaraṭṭhe kañcipurādīsu ca sīhaḷadīpe mahāvihāramhi ca attanā saddhiṃ vasantena āyācito, tathā jīvakenāpi upāsakena piṭakattayapāragubhūtena vātāhatepi aniñjamānasabhāve dume viya aniñjamānasaddhamme ṭhitena sumatinā parisuddhājīvenābhiyācito akāsi. Vuttaṃ hetametissā nigamane –

He composed the commentary on the Aṅguttara Nikāya, named the Manorathapūraṇī, having been requested by the venerable Elder Jotipāla—who was dwelling with him in the Mahāvihāra on the island of Sri Lanka and in places such as Kañcipura in Southern India—and also having been requested by the lay follower named Jīvaka, a wise man of pure livelihood who had mastered the three Piṭakas and stood firm in the True Dhamma that is as immovable as a great tree struck by the wind. It is stated thus at its conclusion:

“Āyācito sumatinā, therena bhadantajotipālena;

Kañcipurādīsu mayā, pubbe saddhiṃ vasantena.

“Requested by the wise, venerable Elder Jotipāla, who was formerly dwelling with me in Kañcipura and other places,

Varatambapaṇṇidīpe, [M 0.0050] mahāvihāramhi vasanakālepi;

Vātāhate viya dume, aniñjamānamhi saddhamme.

and also during the time of dwelling in the Mahāvihāra on the excellent island of Sri Lanka, in the True Dhamma which is as immovable as a tree struck by the wind,

Pāraṃ piṭakattayasā,garassa gantvā ṭhitena sumatinā;

Parisuddhājīvenā,bhiyācito jīvakenāpi.

and also earnestly requested by the wise Jīvaka, of perfectly pure livelihood, who had reached the far shore of the ocean of the three Piṭakas and stood firm,

Dhammakathānayanipuṇehi, dhammakathikehi aparimāṇehi;

Parikīḷitassa paṭipa,jjitassa sakasamayacitrassa.

which is adorned with the specialized doctrines of the Dispensation, the playground and path for countless Dhamma speakers skilled in the methods of Dhamma exposition—

Aṭṭhakathaṃ aṅguttara,mahānikāyassa kātumāraddho;

Yamahaṃ cirakālaṭṭhiti-micchanto sāsanavarassa.

for the sake of composing the commentary on the great Aṅguttara Nikāya, I, desiring the long-lasting stability of the excellent Dispensation, have endeavored to compose it.

Sā hi mahāaṭṭhakathāya, sāramādāya niṭṭhitā esā;

Catunnavutiparimāṇāya, pāḷiyā bhāṇavārehi.

This new commentary is now completed, having extracted the essence from the Great Commentary, and consists of ninety-four recitation sections in length.

Sabbāgamasaṃvaṇṇana, manoratho pūrito ca me yasmā;

Etāya manoratha pūraṇīti nāmaṃ tato assā”ti a. ni. aṭṭha. 3.11.nigamanakathā.

Since my wish to comment upon all the four great Nikāyas has been fulfilled by this work, its name shall therefore be Manorathapūraṇī [The Fulfiller of Wishes].”

Imā ca pana catasso āgamaṭṭhakathāyo kurumāno ācariyabuddhaghoso mahāmahindattherenābhataṃ mūlaṭṭhakathāsaṅkhātaṃ mahāaṭṭhakathaṃyeva bhāsāparivattanavasena ceva punappunāgatavitthārakathāmaggassa saṃkhipanavasena ca akāsi. Vuttañhetaṃ ganthārambhe –

To further explain the method of composition, in producing these four great Nikāya commentaries, the Teacher Buddhaghosa worked from the Great Commentary itself—the one known as the Root Commentary, which had been brought by the great Elder Mahinda—rendering it by way of translating the language and by condensing the repetitive passages. For this was said in the prologue to the work:

“Sīhaḷadīpaṃ pana ābha,tātha vasinā mahāmahindena;

Ṭhapitā sīhaḷabhāsāya, dīpavāsīnamatthāya.

“It was brought to the island of Sri Lanka by the master, the great Mahinda, and established in the Sinhala language for the benefit of the inhabitants of the island.”

Apanetvāna tatohaṃ, sīhaḷabhāsaṃ manoramaṃ bhāsaṃ;

Tantinayānucchavikaṃ, āropento vigatadosaṃ…pe…

Hitvā punappunāgata-matthaṃ atthaṃ pakāsayissāmī”ti.

Having removed the Sinhala language therefrom, I will render it into the beautiful language that accords with the scriptural method, free from faults... Omitting repetitive material, I will illuminate the meaning.

Tathā nigamanepi –

Similarly, also in the conclusion:

“Sā hi mahāaṭṭhakathāya, sāramādāya niṭṭhitā esā”ti dī. ni. aṭṭha. 3.nigamanakathā ca;

“Mūlaṭṭhakathāsāraṃ, ādāya mayā imaṃ karontenā”ti dī. ni. aṭṭha. 3.nigamanakathā ca.

‘For this was completed by extracting the essence from the Great Commentary,’ and, ‘By me, making this by drawing on the essence of the Root Commentary.’

Imāsaṃ sarīrabhūtapāṭhesu ca samantapāsādikāyaṃ viya “mahāpaccariyaṃ, kurundiya”ntiādinā vinicchayasaṃvaṇṇanābhedappakāsanaṃ na dissati, tathā abhidhammaṭṭhakathāsupi. [M 0.0051] Tenetaṃ ñāyati “suttantābhidhammesu mahāaṭṭhakathāto aññā mahāpaccariādināmikā porāṇikā sīhaḷaṭṭhakathāyo ceva andhakaṭṭhakathā ca natthī”ti. Yāva vasabharājakālā (609-653) pana pākaṭānaṃ sīhaḷikattherānaṃ vinicchayo ca vādā ca vatthūni ca etāsupi dissantiyevāti.

In the main body of the texts of these four Nikayas, unlike in the Samantapāsādikā, there is no indication of differing explanations of legal points or detailed classifications by citing the Mahāpaccarī, the Kurundī, and so on; and similarly in the Abhidhamma commentaries. From this it is known that for the Suttas and Abhidhamma, apart from the Mahā-aṭṭhakathā, there were no other ancient Sinhala commentaries with names like Mahāpaccarī, etc., nor an Andhakaṭṭhakathā. However, up to the time of King Vasabha (609-653 BE), the legal decisions, doctrines, and stories of the well-known Sinhala elders are indeed seen even in these Sutta and Abhidhamma commentaries.

Abhidhammaṭṭhakathākaraṇaṃ

The Composition of the Abhidhamma Commentaries

Aṭṭhasāliniṃ pana sammohavinodaniñca dhātukathādipañcapakaraṇassa aṭṭhakathañcāti tisso abhidhammaṭṭhakathāyo attanā sadisanāmena sotatthakīganthakārakena buddhaghosabhikkhunā āyācito akāsi. Vuttañhetaṃ tāsu –

Furthermore, he composed the three Abhidhamma commentaries—the Aṭṭhasālinī, the Sammohavinodanī, and the Pañcapakaraṇa-aṭṭhakathā (the commentary on the five treatises beginning with the Dhātukathā)—having been requested by the bhikkhu Buddhaghosa, the author of the Sotatthakī, who shared the same name as himself. For it is said in them:

“Visuddhācārasīlena, nipuṇāmalabuddhinā;

Bhikkhunā buddhaghosena, sakkaccaṃ abhiyācito”ti dha. sa. aṭṭha. ganthārambhakathā ca.

‘Having been respectfully requested by the bhikkhu Buddhaghosa, who is of pure conduct and virtue, and of subtle and stainless intellect.’

“Buddhaghosoti garūhi gahitanāmadheyyena therena katā

Ayaṃ aṭṭhasālinī nāma dhammasaṅgahaṭṭhakathā”ti dha. sa. aṭṭha. nigamanakathā ca.

‘This commentary on the Dhammasaṅgaṇī, named the Aṭṭhasālinī, was composed by the elder whose name, Buddhaghosa, was conferred by his teachers.’

“Atthappakāsanatthaṃ, tassāhaṃ yācito ṭhitaguṇena;

Yatinā adandhagatinā, subuddhinā buddhaghosena.

‘For the purpose of explaining the meaning of that Vibhaṅga, I was requested by the ascetic Buddhaghosa, who is of steadfast virtue, of agile intellect, and of good wisdom.’

Yaṃ ārabhiṃ racayituṃ, aṭṭhakathaṃ sunipuṇesu atthesu;

Sammohavinodanato, sammohavinodaniṃ nāmā”ti vibha. aṭṭha. nigamanakathā ca.

‘The commentary on very subtle meanings which I began to compose is named the Sammohavinodanī because it dispels delusion.’

“Buddhaghosoti garūhi gahitanāmadheyyena therena katā

Ayaṃ sammohavinodanī nāma vibhaṅgaṭṭhakathā”ti vibha. aṭṭha. nigamanakathā ca.

‘This commentary on the Vibhaṅga, named the Sammohavinodanī, was composed by the elder whose name, Buddhaghosa, was conferred by his teachers.’

Imāsu pana tīsu pañcapakaraṇaṭṭhakathāya nāmaviseso natthi āyācako ca na pakāsito, kevalaṃ attano saddhāya eva sañcoditena ācariyabuddhaghosena sā katā viya dissati. Vuttañhetaṃ tassā nigamane –

However, among these three, the Pañcapakaraṇa-aṭṭhakathā has no specific name, and the requester is not mentioned. It appears as if it was composed by Ācariya Buddhaghosa, prompted solely by his own faith. For it is said in its conclusion:

“Kusalādidhammabhedaṃ, [M 0.0052] nissāya nayehi vividhagaṇanehi;

Vitthārento sattama-mabhidhammappakaraṇaṃ satthā.

‘The Teacher, expounding the seventh Abhidhamma treatise called the Paṭṭhāna, relied on various methods and enumerations based on the classification of dhammas beginning with the wholesome, and was well-established in his arrangement.’

Suvihitasanniṭṭhāno, paṭṭhānaṃ nāma yaṃ pakāsesi;

Saddhāya samāraddhā, yā aṭṭhakathā mayā tassāti ca.

‘I have well undertaken the composition of this commentary on that Paṭṭhāna with faith.’

“Ettāvatā

‘By thus far,’

Sattappakaraṇaṃ nātho, abhidhammamadesayi;

Devātidevo devānaṃ, devalokamhi yaṃ pure;

Tassa aṭṭhakathā esā, sakalassāpi niṭṭhitā”ti paṭṭhā. aṭṭha. 19-24.1 ca.

‘The Lord, the god of gods, taught the Abhidhamma Piṭaka consisting of seven treatises to the devas in the world of devas. This commentary on that entire Abhidhamma Piṭaka is now completed.’

“Buddhaghosoti garūhi gahitanāmadheyyena therena katā

Ayaṃ sakalassapi abhidhammapiṭakassa aṭṭhakathā”ti paṭṭhā. aṭṭha. 19-24.1 ca.

‘This commentary on the entire Abhidhamma Piṭaka was composed by the elder whose name, Buddhaghosa, was conferred by his teachers.’

Ekacce pana ādhunikā therā “abhidhammaṭṭhakathāyo ācariyabuddhaghosena yācito saṅghapālabuddhamittajotipālādīnaṃ aññataro thero akāsī”ti vadanti. Ayañca nesaṃ vicāraṇā, aṭṭhasālinīsammohavinodanīsu “tā buddhaghosena yācito akāsī”ti ganthakārena vuttaṃ. Tena ñāyati “takkārako añño, ācariyabuddhaghoso pana tāsu yācakapuggaloyevā”ti. Āgamaṭṭhakathāsu ca ācariyabuddhaghosena –

However, some modern elders say: ‘The Abhidhamma commentaries were composed by one of the elders—Saṅghapāla, Buddhamitta, or Jotipāla—having been requested by Ācariya Buddhaghosa.’ And this is their reasoning: In the Aṭṭhasālinī and the Sammohavinodanī, the author himself states that ‘he composed them, having been requested by Buddhaghosa.’ From this it is understood that ‘the author was another, while Ācariya Buddhaghosa was merely the requester.’ And in the Nikaya commentaries, Ācariya Buddhaghosa says:

“Sīlakathā dhutadhammā, kammaṭṭhānāni ceva sabbāni…pe…

Iti pana sabbaṃ yasmā, visuddhimagge mayā suparisuddhaṃ;

Vuttaṃ tasmā bhiyyo, na taṃ idha vicārayissāmī”ti dī. ni. aṭṭha. 1.ganthārambhakathā –

‘The discourse on virtue, the ascetic practices, and all the meditation subjects... since all this has been stated by me in a thoroughly purified manner in the Visuddhimagga, I will not discuss it further here.’

Evaṃ sīlakathādīnaṃ attanā eva visuddhimagge vuttabhāvo mayātipadena pakāsito. Aṭṭhasāliniyaṃ pana –

Thus, the fact that the discourse on virtue and so on was stated by himself in the Visuddhimagga is revealed by the word “mayā” (by me). But in the Aṭṭhasālinī:

“Kammaṭṭhānāni sabbāni, cariyābhiññā vipassanā;

Visuddhimagge panidaṃ, yasmā sabbaṃ pakāsita”nti dha. sa. aṭṭha. ganthārambhakathā –

‘All meditation subjects, temperaments, direct knowledges, and insight; since all this has been explained in the Visuddhimagga.’

Evaṃ mayāti kattupadena vinā vuttaṃ. Tenāpi ñāyati “visuddhimaggakārako añño, abhidhammaṭṭhakathākārako añño”ti. Kiñcāpi abhidhammaṭṭhakathāsu abhiyācako buddhaghoso bhikkhunāti ca yatināti ca [M 0.0053] imeheva sāmaññaguṇapadehi vutto na therenāti sagāravaguṇapadena, tathāpi so “visuddhācārasīlena nipuṇāmalabuddhinā”ti ca, “adandhagatinā subuddhinā”ti ca imehi adhikaguṇapadehi thomitattā “visuddhimaggādikārako ācariyabuddhaghosoyevā”ti sakkā gahetuṃ. So hi upasampannakālatoyeva paṭṭhāya ganthakovido pariyattivisāradaguṇasampanno, tasmiñca kāle ūnadasavasso bhaveyya, tasmā therenāti na vuttoti sakkā gahetunti.

Thus, it is stated without the agentive word “mayā.” By that, too, it is known that ‘the author of the Visuddhimagga is one person, and the author of the Abhidhamma commentaries is another.’ Although in the Abhidhamma commentaries the requester Buddhaghosa is spoken of with common terms of quality such as “bhikkhu” and “yati,” and not with the respectful term “thera,” still, because he is praised with superior attributes such as “of pure conduct and virtue, of subtle and stainless intellect” and “of agile intellect and good wisdom,” it can be accepted that Ācariya Buddhaghosa, the author of the Visuddhimagga and other works, is the same person. For from the time of his higher ordination, he was skilled in the scriptures and endowed with expertise in the textual tradition; at that time he might have been of less than ten years’ standing (vassa), and therefore he was not addressed as “thera.” This is the reasoning of those modern teachers.

Taṃ pana tesaṃ ativicāraṇamattameva. Na hi ācariyabuddhaghosatthero “tasmiṃ kāle ūnadasavasso”ti sakkā gahetuṃ, visuddhimagganigamanepi “buddhaghosoti garūhi gahitanāmadheyyena therenā”ti vacanato, na ca “visuddhācārasīlena, nipuṇāmalabuddhinā”ti vā, “adandhagatinā subuddhinā”ti vā ettakeheva dvīhi dvīhi guṇapadehi thomanena suthomito hoti, aññadatthu “nippabhīkatakhajjoto samudeti divākaro”ti thomanaṃ viya hoti. Nanu ācariyena attano ganthanigamanesu –

However, that is merely their excessive speculation. For the Elder Ācariya Buddhaghosa cannot be taken as being “of less than ten years’ standing at that time,” since even in the conclusion of the Visuddhimagga it says, “by the elder whose name, Buddhaghosa, was conferred by his teachers.” Moreover, he is not sufficiently praised merely by these pairs of attributes such as “of pure conduct and virtue, of subtle and stainless intellect” or “of agile intellect and good wisdom.” On the contrary, it is like praising the sun by saying, “the sun rises, having eclipsed the firefly.” Indeed, the Ācariya says in the conclusions of his own books:

“Paramavisuddhasaddhābaddhivīriyapaṭimaṇḍitena sīlācārajjavamaddavādiguṇasamudayasamuditena sakasamayasamayantaragahanajjhogāhaṇasamatthena paññāveyyattiyasamannāgatena tipiṭakapariyattibhede sāṭṭhakathe satthusāsane appaṭihatañāṇappabhāvena mahāveyyākaraṇenā”tiādinā –

‘…by the great grammarian, adorned with supremely pure faith, wisdom, and energy, endowed with a collection of qualities such as virtue, conduct, uprightness, and gentleness, capable of plunging into the thickets of his own doctrine and other doctrines, possessed of intelligence and discernment, having the radiance of unimpeded knowledge in the Teacher’s Dispensation with its commentaries, in its divisions of the Tipiṭaka,’ and so on.

Attano anucchavikāni guṇapadāni pakāsitāni, soyeva ca porāṇasīhaḷaṭṭhakathāyo saṅkhipitvā abhinavasaṅgahaṭṭhakathānaṃ ādikattā pubbaṅgamo, aññe pana abhinavaṭṭhakathākārā tasseva anuvattitvā avasesamekaṃ vā dve vā aṭṭhakathāyo akaṃsu. Abhidhammaṭṭhakathāsu ca yo yo attho visuddhimagge vutto, so so yathānuppattaṭṭhāne tato gahetvā tatheva vutto. Visesato pana paṭiccasamuppādavibhaṅgakhandhāyatanadhātusaccavibhaṅgavaṇṇanāsu jhānakathāvaṇṇanāsu ca ayamattho ativiya pākaṭo, yopi ca tattha appako katipayamatto visuddhimaggena visadiso saṃvaṇṇanābhedo dissati, sopi ābhidhammikānaṃ matānusārena yathā porāṇaṭṭhakathāyaṃ vutto, tatheva vuttoti veditabbo. Yathā ca aṭṭhasāliniyaṃ samantapāsādikāya vinayaṭṭhakathāya atideso [M 0.0054] dissati dha. sa. aṭṭha. 1 akusalakammapathakathā, tatheva samantapāsādikāyampi aṭṭhasāliniyā atideso dissateva pārā. aṭṭha. 1.11;. Yadi ca aṭṭhasālinī aññena katā bhaveyya, kathaṃ tāsu aññamaññātideso sakkā kātuṃ. Tasmā abhidhammaṭṭhakathāsu abhiyācako buddhaghoso ācariyena samānanāmo cūḷabuddhaghosoti yāvajjatanā ācariyaparamparāya gahito sotatthakīganthakārako aññoyeva, na ācariyamahābuddhaghosatthero. Teneva tattha vuttaṃ “bhikkhunā”ti ca “yatinā”ti ca.

He has proclaimed qualities befitting himself, has he not? Furthermore, is not the Elder Buddhaghosa himself the forerunner and first author of the new collected commentaries, having abridged the old Sinhala commentaries? Other authors of new commentaries, following only that Elder Buddhaghosa, composed the remaining one or two commentaries. In the Abhidhamma commentaries, whatever meaning was stated in the Visuddhimagga, that same meaning was taken from its corresponding place in the Visuddhimagga and stated in the same way. This is especially evident in the explanations of the Vibhaṅgas of Dependent Origination, Aggregates, Sense Bases, Elements, and Truths, and in the explanations of the Jhāna-kathā. Even the few slight differences in explanation seen there, which are dissimilar to the Visuddhimagga, should be understood as being stated according to the views of the Abhidhamma specialists, just as they were stated in the old commentaries. Just as a reference to the Vinaya commentary is seen in the Aṭṭhasālinī, so too a reference to the Aṭṭhasālinī is seen in the Vinaya commentary. If the Aṭṭhasālinī had been composed by another elder, how could they have made mutual cross-references to each other? Therefore, the requester in the Abhidhamma commentaries, Buddhaghosa, who had the same name as the Teacher, is none other than the author of the Sotatthakī, known as Cūḷabuddhaghosa by the succession of teachers to this day; he is not the Teacher, the Elder Mahābuddhaghosa. For that reason, it is said there simply 'by a monk' or 'by an ascetic'.

Yadi pana ettakena niṭṭhaṃ na gaccheyya, evampi vicāretabbaṃ – kinnu kho saṅghapālādayo therā visuddhimaggādīnaṃ karaṇatthāya ācariyabuddhaghosattheraṃ āyācamānā attanā samatthataroti saddahantā āyācanti udāhu asaddahantāti? Saddahantāyeva āyācantīti pākaṭoyevāyamattho. Tathā ca sati ācariyabuddhaghosatthero sayaṃ aññehi samatthatarova samāno kasmā aññaṃ āyāceyya. Na hi saddhāsampannassa thāmasampannassa yobbanasampannassa ācariyassa sundarataraṃ abhidhammaṭṭhakathaṃ kātuṃ bhāriyaṃ bhavissati. Abhidhammaṭṭhakathāsu ca vuttavacanāni visuddhimaggaāgamaṭṭhakathāsu vuttasaṃvaṇṇanāvacanehi ekākārāneva honti. Yadi ca abhidhammaṭṭhakathaṃ añño kareyya, kathamapi tāhi vacanākārassa visadisatā bhaveyya eva. Etāsaṃ nigamane ca dassitena “buddhaghosoti garūhi gahitanāmadheyyena therena katā”ti vacanena “ācariyabuddhaghosena katā”tveva pākaṭā honti, na aññenāti. Yepi “aññena katā”ti vadanti, tepi “iminā nāma therenā”ti ekaṃsato dassetuṃ na sakkonti, tathā dassetuñca lesamattampi sādhakavacanaṃ na dissati. Tasmā abhidhammaṭṭhakathāyopi idāni ācariyehi cūḷabuddhaghosoti voharitena buddhaghosena nāma bhikkhunāyācito visuddhimaggavinayāgamaṭṭhakathānaṃ kārako ācariyamahābuddhaghosattheroyeva akāsīti niṭṭhamettha gantabbanti.

If the matter is not settled by this much, it should be further considered in this way: when the elders like Saṅghapāla requested the Elder Ācariya Buddhaghosa to compose the Visuddhimagga and other works, did they request him believing he was more capable than themselves, or not? It is clear they requested him because they did believe so. That being the case, why would the Elder Ācariya Buddhaghosa, being himself more capable than others, request another? Indeed, it would not have been a heavy task for the Teacher, who was endowed with faith, strength, and youth, to compose a more excellent Abhidhamma commentary. Furthermore, the statements in the Abhidhamma commentaries are identical in form to the explanations in the Visuddhimagga and the commentaries on the four Nikāyas. If another had composed the Abhidhamma commentary, there would surely be some difference in the manner of expression. And by the statement shown in the conclusions of these Abhidhamma commentaries, 'composed by the Elder whose name, Buddhaghosa, was conferred by his teachers,' they are clearly known to be composed by Ācariya Buddhaghosa, not by another. Even those who say 'it was composed by another' cannot definitively show 'an elder by this name.' Not even a slight hint of supporting evidence can be found to show this. Therefore, it should be concluded here that the Elder Ācariya Mahābuddhaghosa himself, the author of the Visuddhimagga, the Vinaya commentary, and the Nikāya commentaries, composed the Abhidhamma commentaries as well, at the request of a monk named Buddhaghosa, whom teachers now refer to as Cūḷabuddhaghosa.

Yaṃ pana mahāvaṃse “ācariyabuddhaghoso sīhaḷadīpāgamanato pubbe jambudīpe vasanakāleyeva aṭṭhasāliniṃ akāsī”ti adhippāyena –

As for the statement in the Mahāvaṃsa, implying that ‘Ācariya Buddhaghosa composed the Aṭṭhasālinī while he was still living in Jambudīpa, before his arrival on the island of Sri Lanka’—

225. “Dhammasaṅgaṇiyākāsi, kacchaṃ so aṭṭhasālini”nti –

225. “He composed the Aṭṭhasālinī, the commentary on the Dhammasaṅgaṇī.”

Vuttaṃ, [M 0.0055] taṃ idāni dissamānāya aṭṭhasāliniyā na sameti. Tattha hi ganthārambheyeva visuddhimaggaṃ atidisitvā pacchāpi so ca, samantapāsādikā ca bahūsu ṭhānesu atidisīyanti. Tasmā tassā ācariyena sīhaḷadīpaṃ patvā visuddhimaggañceva samantapāsādikañca katvā pacchāyeva katabhāvo ativiya pākaṭoti.

This has been stated, but it does not agree with the Aṭṭhasālinī as it is seen now. For in the very prologue of that work, the Visuddhimagga is cross-referenced, and later on, both it and the Samantapāsādikā are cross-referenced in many places. Therefore, it is quite evident that the Teacher composed it only after arriving on the island of Sri Lanka and having already composed the Visuddhimagga and the Samantapāsādikā.

Kaṅkhāvitaraṇīaṭṭhakathākaraṇaṃ

The Composition of the Kaṅkhāvitaraṇī Commentary

Kaṅkhāvitaraṇiṃ nāma pātimokkhaṭṭhakathaṃ ācariyabuddhaghosatthero soṇattherena yācito mahāvihāravāsīnaṃ vācanāmagganissitaṃ sīhaḷapātimokkhaṭṭhakathānayaṃ nissāya ekampi padaṃ pāḷiyā vā mahāvihāravāsīnaṃ porāṇaṭṭhakathāhi vā avirodhetvā akāsi. Tena vuttaṃ tissaṃ aṭṭhakathāyaṃ –

The Elder Ācariya Buddhaghosa composed the commentary on the Pātimokkha named Kaṅkhāvitaraṇī at the request of the Elder Soṇa, relying on the method of the Sri Lankan Pātimokkha commentary which follows the tradition of the residents of the Mahāvihāra, without contradicting even a single word of the Pāli or the ancient commentaries of the Mahāvihāra residents. Thus it is said in that commentary:

“Sūratena nivātena, sucisallekhavuttinā;

Vinayācārayuttena, soṇattherena yācito.

“Requested by the Elder Soṇa, who is gentle and humble, of pure and austere conduct, and constantly devoted to the practice of the Vinaya,

Tattha sañjātakaṅkhānaṃ, bhikkhūnaṃ tassa vaṇṇanaṃ;

Kaṅkhāvitaraṇatthāya, paripuṇṇavinicchayaṃ.

For the sake of those monks in that Pātimokkha who have doubts, that they may overcome their doubts, an explanation with complete decisions,

Mahāvihāravāsīnaṃ, vācanāmagganissitaṃ;

Vattayissāmi nāmena, kaṅkhāvitaraṇiṃ subha”nti kaṅkhā aṭṭha. ganthārambhakathā ca.

Relying on the tradition of the residents of the Mahāvihāra, I shall set forth the beautiful commentary on the Pātimokkha named Kaṅkhāvitaraṇī.”

“Ārabhiṃ yamahaṃ sabbaṃ, sīhaḷaṭṭhakathānayaṃ;

Mahāvihāravāsīnaṃ, vācanāmagganissitaṃ.

“The entire Kaṅkhāvitaraṇī that I undertook to compose, based on the method of all the Sri Lankan commentaries and relying on the tradition of the residents of the Mahāvihāra,

Nissāya sā ayaṃ niṭṭhaṃ, gatā ādāya sabbaso;

Sabbaṃ aṭṭhakathāsāraṃ, pāḷiyatthañca kevalaṃ.

Has reached completion, having fully extracted the essence of all the commentaries and the entire meaning of the Pāli.

Na hettha taṃ padaṃ atthi, yaṃ virujjheyya pāḷiyā;

Mahāvihāravāsīnaṃ, porāṇaṭṭhakathāhi vā”ti kaṅkhā. aṭṭha. nigamanakathā ca.

In this work, there is no word that contradicts the Pāli or the ancient commentaries of the residents of the Mahāvihāra.”

Dhammapadaṭṭhakathākaraṇaṃ

The Composition of the Dhammapada Commentary

Aparāpi tisso aṭṭhakathāyo santi khuddakapāṭhaṭṭhakathā dhammapadaṭṭhakathā suttanipātaṭṭhakathā cāti, yā tāsu dissamānanigamanavasena ācariyabuddhaghoseneva katāti paññāyanti. Tattha pana vuttavacanāni kānici [M 0.0056] kānici āgamaṭṭhakathāsu vuttākārena na honti. Tasmā eke vadanti “netā ācariyabuddhaghosassā”ti. Ekacce pana “ācariyassa upathambhakattherehi paṭhamaṃ katā, pacchā ācariyena osānasodhanavasena pariyosāpitā vā bhaveyyuṃ, abhidhammaṭṭhakathaṃ āyācantena cūḷabuddhaghosena vā katā bhaveyyu”nti vadanti.

There are also three other commentaries: the commentary on the Khuddakapāṭha, the commentary on the Dhammapada, and the commentary on the Suttanipāta, which are understood to have been composed by Ācariya Buddhaghosa himself, based on the colophons seen in them. However, some of the statements made therein are not in the same manner as what is stated in the commentaries on the four Āgamas. Therefore, some say, “These are not works of Ācariya Buddhaghosa.” But others say, “They may have been first composed by elders who were assistants to the Teacher, and later finalized by the Teacher himself by way of a concluding revision; or they may have been composed by Cūḷabuddhaghosa, who requested the writing of the Abhidhamma commentary.”

Taṃ tathā vā hotu aññathā vā, idāni ekantato vinicchinituṃ na sukarameva. Tasmā tāsaṃ nigamanavacanavaseneva ettha pakāsayissāma. Tāsu hi dhammapadaṭṭhakathaṃ kumārakassapattherena āyācito sirikūṭassa (sirikuḍḍassa) rañño pāsāde viharanto paramparābhataṃ sīhaḷabhāsāya saṇṭhitaṃ porāṇaṭṭhakathaṃ pāḷibhāsāya āropetvā vitthāragatañca vacanakkamaṃ samāsetvā gāthāsu asaṃvaṇṇitapadabyañjanāni saṃvaṇṇetvā akāsi. Vuttañhi tattha ganthārambhe –

Whether it was so or otherwise, it is indeed not easy to determine conclusively now. Therefore, we will present it here only on the basis of the words of their colophons. Among these three, at the request of the Elder Kumārakassapa, he composed the Dhammapadaṭṭhakathā while dwelling in the palace of King Sirikūṭa (or Sirikuḍḍa). He rendered into the Pāli language the ancient commentary transmitted through tradition and established in the Sinhalese language, condensed the extensive sequence of words, and explained the words and phrases in the verses that had not been explained in the ancient commentary. For it is stated there at the beginning of the text:

“Paramparābhatā tassa, nipuṇā atthavaṇṇanā;

Yā tambapaṇṇidīpamhi, dīpabhāsāya saṇṭhitā…pe…

“That subtle explanation of the meaning of the Dhammapada, transmitted through tradition, is well-established in the island of Tambapaṇṇi in the Sinhalese language... pe...

Kumārakassapenāhaṃ, therena thiracetasā;

Saddhammaṭṭhitikāmena, sakkaccaṃ abhiyācito…pe…

By the Elder named Kumārakassapa, of steadfast mind, desiring the stability of the good Dhamma, I was respectfully requested... pe...

Taṃ bhāsaṃ ativitthāra, gatañca vacanakkamaṃ;

Pahāyāropayitvāna, tantibhāsaṃ manoramaṃ.

Having set aside that Sinhalese language and its very extensive word order, and having rendered it into the pleasing canonical Pāli language,

Gāthānaṃ byañjanapadaṃ, yaṃ tattha na vibhāvitaṃ;

Kevalaṃ taṃ vibhāvetvā, sesaṃ tameva atthato.

explaining only those words and phrases of the verses that were not made clear in that ancient commentary—as for the rest, the meaning remains the same as in that ancient commentary—

Bhāsantarena bhāsissa”nti dha. pa. aṭṭha. 1.ganthārambhakathā –

I will speak in another language [Pāli].”

Nigamane ca vuttaṃ –

And in the colophon it is said:

“Vihāre adhirājena, kāritamhi kataññunā;

Pāsāde sirikūṭassa, rañño viharatā mayā”ti dha. pa. aṭṭha. 2.nigamanakathā.

“While dwelling in the palace of King Sirikūṭa, in the monastery complex that was caused to be built by the grateful sovereign king, I [have acquired merit by composing this Dhammapada commentary].”

Ettha ca sirikūṭo nāma samantapāsādikānigamane siripāloti vutto mahānāmoyeva rājāti vadanti. Evaṃ sati mahesiyā ānayanaṃ samādāpanamārabbha tena raññā dinne dhūmarakkhapabbatavihāre vasantena sā katāti veditabbā. Vuttañhetaṃ mahāvaṃse –

Let it be further explained: here, they say that the one named Sirikūṭa is the very same King Mahānāma who is called Siripāla in the colophon of the Vinaya commentary (Samantapāsādikā). This being so, it should be understood that the commentary was composed while residing in the Dhūmarakkhapabbata monastery, which had been given by King Mahānāma at the persuasion and encouragement of his chief queen. For this is stated in the Mahāvaṃsa:

37-212.

37-212.

“Lohadvāra-ralaggāma-koṭipassāvanavhaye;

Tayo [M 0.0057] vihāre kāretvā, bhikkhūnaṃ abhayuttare.

“Having had three monastery complexes built, named Lohadvāra, Ralaggāma, and Koṭipassāvana, he gave them to the monks dwelling in the northern monastery named Abhayagiri.

213.

213.

Vihāraṃ kārayitvāna, dhūmarakkhamhi pabbate;

Mahesiyā’nayenā’dā, bhikkhūnaṃ theravādina”nti.

Due to the persuasion of the chief queen, he had a monastery built on the Dhūmarakkha mountain and gave it to the Theravādin monks.”

Tassa pana rañño kāle sā niṭṭhāpitāti na sakkā gahetuṃ. Tassa hi rañño ekavīsatimavasse samantapāsādikaṃ niṭṭhāpesi. So ca rājā dvāvīsatimavasse divaṅgato. Etthantare sādhikaekavassena “catasso ca āgamaṭṭhakathāyo tisso ca abhidhammaṭṭhakathāyo ayañca dhammapadaṭṭhakathā”ti sabbā etā na sakkā niṭṭhāpetunti.

However, it cannot be assumed that the Dhammapada commentary was completed during that King Mahānāma's reign. For he completed the Vinaya commentary in the twenty-first year of that king's reign, and that king passed away in his twenty-second year. In this interval of a little more than one year, it was impossible to complete all these works—the four Āgama commentaries, the three Abhidhamma commentaries, and this Dhammapada commentary.

Paramatthajotikāṭṭhakathākaraṇaṃ

The Composition of the Paramatthajotikā Commentary

Paramatthajotikaṃ nāma khuddakapāṭhassa ceva suttanipātassa ca aṭṭhakathaṃ kenacipi anāyācito attano icchāvaseneva akāsi. Vuttañhetaṃ khuddakapāṭhaṭṭhakathāya ganthārambhe –

He composed the Paramatthajotikā, the commentary on both the Khuddakapāṭha and the Suttanipāta, unsolicited by anyone and solely according to his own wish. For this is stated at the beginning of the Khuddakapāṭha commentary:

“Uttamaṃ vandaneyyānaṃ, vanditvā ratanattayaṃ;

Khuddakānaṃ karissāmi, kesañci atthavaṇṇanaṃ.

“Having venerated the Triple Gem, which is supreme among the objects of veneration and worthy of respect, I will compose an explanation of the meaning of some of the small Suttas called Khuddaka.

Khuddakānaṃ gambhīrattā, kiñcāpi atidukkarā;

Vaṇṇanā mādisenesā, abodhantena sāsanaṃ.

It is true that because these small Suttas called Khuddaka are very profound, it is exceedingly difficult for one like me, who does not yet understand the Dispensation, to compose this commentary as an explanation for them.

Ajjāpi tu abbhocchinno, pubbācariyanicchayo;

Tatheva ca ṭhitaṃ yasmā, navaṅgaṃ satthusāsanaṃ.

Nevertheless, the decisions of the former teachers remain uninterrupted even today, and likewise, the Master’s ninefold Dispensation stands firm.

Tasmāhaṃ kātumicchāmi, atthasaṃvaṇṇanaṃ imaṃ;

Sāsanañceva nissāya, porāṇañca vinicchayaṃ.

Therefore, relying on the Dispensation and the decisions of the former teachers, I wish to compose this commentary on the meaning.

Saddhammabahumānena, nāttukkaṃsanakamyatā;

Nāññesaṃ vambhanatthāya, taṃ suṇātha samāhitā”ti.

Neither from a desire for self-exaltation nor for the purpose of disparaging others, but solely out of a great desire for the good Dhamma, listen to that commentary of mine with collected minds.”

Bahū pana vicakkhaṇā imā ārambhagāthāyo vicinitvā “netaṃ ācariyabuddhaghosattherassa viya vacanaṃ hotī”ti vadanti. Ayañca nesaṃ vicinanākāro, ācariyabuddhaghoso hi yaṃ kañci ganthaṃ sīlādiguṇasampannena aññena āyācitova karoti, idha pana kocipi āyācako [M 0.0058] natthi. Punapi ācariyo “porāṇasīhaḷaṭṭhakathaṃ bhāsāparivattanavasena karissāmī”ti ca “mahāvihāravāsīnaṃ vācanāmaggaṃ nissāya karissāmī”ti ca evaṃ paṭiññaṃ katvāva karoti, idha pana tādisīpi paṭiññā natthi. Punapi ācariyo atigambhīratthānaṃ catunnañcāgamānaṃ abhidhammassa ca saṃvaṇṇanārambhepi dukkarabhāvaṃ na katheti, idha pana “sāsanaṃ abodhantena mādisenā”ti attanā sāsanassa abuddhabhāvaṃ pakāsetvā “atidukkarā”ti ca katheti. Tasmā “netaṃ ācariyabuddhaghosassa viya vacana”nti vadanti. Taṃ yuttaṃ viya dissati, ācariyo hi attano ganthanigamanesu “tipiṭakapariyattippabhede sāṭṭhakathe satthusāsane appaṭihatañāṇappabhāvenā”ti attano ñāṇappabhāvaṃ pakāsesi, so “sāsanaṃ abodhantena mādisena atidukkarā”ti īdisaṃ vacanaṃ na katheyyayevāti.

However, many discerning scholars, having examined these introductory verses, say: ‘This speech is not like the speech of the commentator Ācariya Buddhaghosa.’ This is the manner of those scholars’ analysis: Ācariya Buddhaghosa only composes a book when requested by another person who is endowed with virtues such as morality; but in this commentary, there is no one who has made a request. Furthermore, Ācariya Buddhaghosa usually composes a work only after making a declaration such as: ‘I will do this by way of translating the ancient Sinhalese commentary,’ or ‘I will do this relying on the tradition of the teachers of the Mahāvihāra’; but in this commentary, there is no such declaration. Furthermore, Ācariya Buddhaghosa does not speak of or complain about the difficulty of the task even at the beginning of the commentaries on the four great Āgamas or the Abhidhamma Piṭaka, which have extremely profound meanings. Here, however, having admitted his own lack of understanding of the Dispensation by saying, ‘by one like me, who does not understand the Dispensation,’ he also complains that it is ‘exceedingly difficult.’ Therefore, they say: ‘This speech is not like the speech of Ācariya Buddhaghosa.’ That analysis seems plausible; for Ācariya Buddhaghosa, in the conclusions of his commentaries, proclaimed his own intellectual power, saying: ‘[Buddhaghosa], who is endowed with the power of unimpeded knowledge in the Master’s Dispensation, which has the various divisions of the Tipiṭaka together with the commentaries.’ Such an Ācariya Buddhaghosa, who describes himself in this way, would not be expected to say such words as: ‘It is exceedingly difficult for one like me, who does not understand the Dispensation, to compose.’ Furthermore, if it were Ācariya Buddhaghosa, there would be no need for him to declare his lack of desire for self-exaltation or for disparaging others.

Jātakaṭṭhakathākaraṇaṃ

The Composition of the Jātaka Commentary

Jātakaṭṭhakathāpi ca ācariyabuddhaghosatthereneva katāti vadanti, kāraṇaṃ panettha na dissati. Sā pana atthadassittherena ca buddhamittattherena ca mahisāsakanikāyikena ca buddhadevattherenāti tīhi therehi abhiyācito mahāvihāravāsīnaṃ vācanāmaggaṃ nissāya katā. Imissāpi nāmaviseso natthi. Vuttaṃ himissā ārambhe –

They say that the Jātaka Commentary was also composed by the Teacher, the Elder Buddhaghosa, but no reason for this is apparent here. It was, however, composed at the request of three elders—the Elder Atthadassī, the Elder Buddhamitta, and the Elder Buddhadeva of the Mahisāsaka school—relying on the recitation tradition of the residents of the Mahāvihāra. This work, too, has no special name. For at its beginning it is said:

“Buddhavaṃsassa etassa, icchantena ciraṭṭhitiṃ;

Yācito abhigantvāna, therena atthadassinā.

“Wishing for the long endurance of this lineage of the Buddhas, I was approached and requested by the Elder Atthadassī,

Asaṃsaṭṭhavihārena, sadā suddhavihārinā;

Tatheva buddhamittena, santacittena viññunā.

who dwells aloof and is ever pure in his abiding; and likewise by the wise Buddhamitta of tranquil mind,

Mahisāsakavaṃsamhi, sambhūtena nayaññunā;

Buddhadevena ca tathā, bhikkhunā suddhabuddhinā.

and also by the bhikkhu Buddhadeva, of pure intelligence, who is skilled in method and arose in the Mahisāsaka lineage,

Mahāpurisacariyānaṃ, ānubhāvaṃ acintiyaṃ;

Tassa vijjotayantassa, jātakassatthavaṇṇanaṃ.

to compose an explanation of the meaning of the Jātaka, which illuminates the inconceivable power of the conduct of the Great Being.

Mahāvihāravāsīnaṃ, vācanāmagganissitaṃ;

Bhāsissaṃ bhāsato taṃ me, sādhu gaṇhantu sādhavo”ti.

I shall explain it, relying on the recitation tradition of the residents of the Mahāvihāra. As I explain it, may the virtuous listen to it well from me.”

Ettāvatā [M 0.0059] ca ācariyabuddhaghosattherassa ganthabhāvena pākaṭāhi sabbaṭṭhakathāhi saha visuddhimaggassa karaṇappakāro vitthārena vibhāvito hoti.

Thus far, the manner of composing the Visuddhimagga has been explained in detail, together with all the commentaries that are renowned as the works of the Teacher, the Elder Buddhaghosa.

Sakalalokapatthārakāraṇaṃ

The Reason for Its Spread Throughout the Entire World

Kissesa visuddhimaggo sakalaloke patthaṭoti? Parisuddhapiṭakapāḷinissayabhāvato, sikkhattayasaṅgahabhāvato, porāṇaṭṭhakathānaṃ bhāsāparivattanabhāvato, parasamayavivajjanato, sakasamayavisuddhito, sīladhutaṅgasamathaabhiññāpaññāpabhedādīnaṃ paripuṇṇavibhāgato, yāva arahattā paṭipattinayaparidīpanato, uttānānākulapadabyañjanasaṅkhatabhāvato, suviññeyyatthabhāvato, pasādanīyānaṃ diṭṭhānugatāpādanasamatthānaṃ vatthūnañca dīpanatoti evamādīhi anekasatehi guṇehi esa sakalaloke patthaṭo jāto.

Why has this Visuddhimagga spread throughout the entire world? It has become spread throughout the entire world because of these and hundreds of other such qualities: its reliance on the purified Pāḷi Tipiṭaka; its inclusion of the threefold training; its being a translation of the ancient commentaries; its avoidance of other doctrines; the purity of its own doctrine; its complete analysis of the divisions of virtue, the ascetic practices, serenity, the direct knowledges, wisdom, and so on; its clarification of the method of practice up to arahantship; its composition with clear and unconfused words and phrases; its easily intelligible meaning; and its exposition of inspiring stories capable of leading to the emulation of their example.

Ayañhi visuddhimaggo saṅgītittayārūḷhaparisuddhapāḷipiṭakameva nissāya pavatto, na mahāsaṅghikādīnaṃ sattarasannaṃ nikāyānaṃ piṭakaṃ, napi mahāyānikānaṃ piṭakaṃ. Saparivāraṃ sikkhattayañca ettha paripuṇṇameva saṅgahetvā dassitaṃ. Vuttañhetaṃ ācariyena āgamaṭṭhakathāsu ganthārambhe –

For this Visuddhimagga proceeds relying only on the pure Pāḷi Tipiṭaka established at the three Councils, not on the Tipiṭaka of the seventeen schools such as the Mahāsaṅghikas, nor on the Tipiṭaka of the Mahāyānists. Here, the threefold training with its accessory parts has been fully included and explained. For this was said by the Teacher at the beginning of the commentaries on the Nikāyas:

“Sīlakatā dhutadhammā, kammaṭṭhānāni ceva sabbāni;

Cariyāvidhānasahito, jhānasamāpattivitthāro.

“The description of virtue, the ascetic practices, and all the meditation subjects; the detailed exposition of the jhānas and attainments, together with the treatment of the temperaments;

Sabbā ca abhiññāyo, paññāsaṅkalananicchayo ceva;

Khandhādhātāyatani,ndriyāni ariyāni ceva cattāri.

all the direct knowledges, and the determination of the synthesis of wisdom; the aggregates, elements, sense bases, and faculties; and the four noble truths;

Saccāni paccayākāra,desanā suparisuddhanipuṇanayā;

Avimuttatantimaggā, vipassanābhāvanā ceva.

the truths, the teaching on the structure of conditionality, with its utterly pure and subtle method; the path of the continuous textual tradition, and the development of insight.

Iti pana sabbaṃ yasmā, visuddhimagge mayā suparisuddhaṃ;

Vuttaṃ tasmā bhiyyo, na taṃ idha vicārayissāmī”ti.

Since all this has been explained by me in a thoroughly purified manner in the Visuddhimagga, I will not examine it further here.”

Yasmā pana visuddhimaggo catunnaṃ āgamaṭṭhakathānaṃ avayavabhāvena kato, tasmā tā viya porāṇasīhaḷaṭṭhakathānaṃ bhāsāparivattanavasena ceva punappunāgatamatthānaṃ saṃkhipanavasena ca parasamayavivajjanavasena ca mahāvihāravāsīnaṃ parisuddhavinicchayasaṅkhātassa sakasamayassa dīpanavasena ca kato. Vuttañhetaṃ ācariyena –

Since the Visuddhimagga was composed as a part of the commentaries on the four Nikāyas, it was therefore made in the same way as them: by way of translating the language of the ancient Sinhala commentaries, by way of condensing repeatedly occurring material, by way of avoiding other doctrines, and by way of clarifying its own doctrine, known as the pure decision of the residents of the Mahāvihāra. For this was said by the Teacher:

“Apanetvāna [M 0.0060] tatohaṃ, sīhaḷabhāsaṃ manoramaṃ bhāsaṃ;

Tantinayānucchavikaṃ, āropento vigatadosaṃ.

“Having removed from it the Sinhala tongue, I am rendering it into the delightful language that accords with the method of the sacred texts, free from faults.

Samayaṃ avilomento, therānaṃ theravaṃsapadīpānaṃ;

Sunipuṇavinicchayānaṃ, mahāvihāre nivāsīnaṃ;

Hitvā punappunāgata-matthaṃ atthaṃ pakāsayissāmī”ti dī. ni. aṭṭha. 1.ganthārambhakathā ca.

Not opposing the doctrine of the elders who reside in the Mahāvihāra, the lamps of the lineage of the Elders, who are skilled in subtle decisions, and omitting repetitive material, I will clarify the meaning.”

“Majjhe visuddhimaggo, esa catunnampi āgamānañhi;

Ṭhatvā pakāsayissati, tattha yathābhāsitamatthaṃ.

“This Visuddhimagga, standing in the midst of all four Nikāyas, will clarify the meaning therein just as it was spoken.

Icceva kato tasmā, tampi gahetvāna saddhimetāya;

Aṭṭhakathāya vijānatha, dīghāgamanissitaṃ attha”nti dī. ni. aṭṭha. 1.ganthārambhakathā ca.

Thus was it composed. Therefore, taking this up along with this Commentary, understand the meaning that is based on the Dīgha Nikāya.”

“Sā hi mahāaṭṭhakathāya, sāramādāya niṭṭhitā esā;

Ekāsītipamāṇāya, pāḷiyā bhāṇavārehi.

“For this commentary is completed by taking the essence from the Great Commentary, in a Pāḷi text of eighty-one recitation sections.

Ekūnasaṭṭhimatto, visuddhimaggopi bhāṇavārehi;

Atthappakāsanatthāya, āgamānaṃ kato yasmā.

Since the Visuddhimagga, too, which is about fifty-nine recitation sections long, was composed to clarify the meaning of the Nikāyas,

Tasmā tena sahāyaṃ, aṭṭhakathā bhāṇavāragaṇanāya;

Suparimitaparicchinnaṃ, cattālīsaṃ sataṃ hotī”ti dī. ni. aṭṭha. 3.nigamanakathā ca.

therefore this commentary, together with that, by the count of recitation sections, when well measured and defined, becomes one hundred and forty.”

Yadi cāyaṃ visuddhimaggo ācariyena āgamaṭṭhakathāyo viya akatvā porāṇasīhaḷaṭṭhakathāyo ca anoloketvā kevalaṃ attano ñāṇappabhāveneva kato assa, nāyaṃ āgamaṭṭhakathānaṃ avayavoti gahetabbo assa, aññadatthu “āgamaṭṭhakathāyo mahāṭṭhakathāya sārabhūtā, visuddhimaggo pana na tassā sārabhūto, kevalaṃ ācariyassa matiyāva kato”ti evameva vattabbo assa. Yasmā pana tathā akatvā pubbe vuttappakāreneva kato, tasmā ayampi visuddhimaggo tāsaṃ āgamaṭṭhakathānaṃ karaṇākāreneva katoti ca, tatoyeva mahāṭṭhakathāya sārabhūtoti ca daṭṭhabbo.

If this Visuddhimagga had been composed by the Teacher solely through the power of his own knowledge—without making it like the Nikāya commentaries and without examining the ancient Sinhala commentaries—then it should not be regarded as a part of the Nikāya commentaries. Instead, it would have to be said: ‘The Nikāya commentaries are the essence of the Great Commentary, but the Visuddhimagga is not its essence; it was composed merely by the Teacher’s own opinion.’ But since it was not composed in that way, but rather in the manner described above, this Visuddhimagga, too, should be seen as having been composed according to the same method as those Nikāya commentaries, and for that very reason as being the essence of the Great Commentary.

Ekacce pana vicakkhaṇā ācariyabuddhaghosassa ganthesu uttarapakkhasāsanikānaṃ assaghosanāgajjunavasubandhuādīnaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ viya porāṇaganthe anissāya attano ñāṇeneva takketvā dassitaṃ dhammakathāvisesaṃ adisvā asantuṭṭhacittā evaṃ vadanti “buddhaghosassa aññaṃ anissāya attano ñāṇappabhāveneva abhinavaganthuppādanaṃ na passāmā”ti. [M 0.0061] Taṃ tesaṃ garahāvacanampi samānaṃ theravādīnaṃ pasaṃsāvacanameva sampajjati. Theravādino hi evaṃ jānanti “buddheneva bhagavatā sammāsambuddhena desetabbo ceva dhammo paññāpetabbo ca vinayo anavasesena desito ceva paññatto ca, soyeva dhammavinayo saddhāsampannehi bhikkhūhi ceva gahaṭṭhehi ca yathārahaṃ paṭipajjitabbo, na tato añño dhammavinayo takketvā gavesetabbo. Yadi pana añño dhammavinayo kenaci takketvā kathito assa, taṃ tasseva takkino sāsanaṃ hoti na satthu sāsanaṃ. Yaṃ yaṃ pana bhagavato dhammavinaye padabyañjanaṃ atthato apākaṭaṃ hoti, tattha tattha porāṇakehi paṭisambhidāchaḷabhiññādiguṇasampannehi bhagavato adhippāyaṃ jānantehi aṭṭhakathācariyehi saṃvaṇṇitanayena attho gahetabbo, na attanomativasenā”ti. Ācariyabuddhaghoso ca tesaṃ theravādīnaṃ aññataro, sopi tatheva jānāti. Vuttañcetaṃ ācariyena –

However, some discerning persons, not seeing in Ācariya Buddhaghosa’s works a special exposition of the Dhamma presented through reasoning based on his own knowledge without reliance on the ancient texts—like in the works of the monks of the northern school such as Aśvaghoṣa, Nāgārjuna, and Vasubandhu—became dissatisfied and said: “We do not see Buddhaghosa producing any new work solely by the power of his own knowledge without relying on another.” Although this is a statement of censure from them, it actually amounts to a statement of praise for the Theravādins. For the Theravādins know this: “The Dhamma to be taught and the Vinaya to be laid down were, without remainder, taught and laid down by the Blessed One, the Perfectly Enlightened One himself. That same Dhamma and Vinaya should be practiced as is fitting by faithful monastics and householders; no other Dhamma and Vinaya should be sought through speculation. If another Dhamma and Vinaya were to be declared by someone through speculation, that would be the teaching of that speculator, not the teaching of the Master. Moreover, wherever a word or phrase in the Blessed One’s Dhamma and Vinaya is not manifest in meaning, the meaning should be taken there in accordance with the way it has been explained by the ancient commentary teachers who knew the Blessed One’s intention, those endowed with the analytical knowledges, the six supernormal powers, and other virtues, and not according to one’s own opinion.” Ācariya Buddhaghosa was one of those Theravādins, and he too knew this. And this was stated by the Ācariya:

“Buddhena dhammo vinayo ca vutto,

Yo tassa puttehi tatheva ñāto;

So yehi tesaṃ matimaccajantā,

Yasmā pure aṭṭhakathā akaṃsu.

“The Dhamma and the Vinaya were taught by the Buddha, and were known in just that way by his sons; since they, not abandoning their opinion, composed the ancient commentaries of old.

Tasmā hi yaṃ aṭṭhakathāsu vuttaṃ,

Taṃ vajjayitvāna pamādalekhaṃ;

Sabbampi sikkhāsu sagāravānaṃ,

Yasmā pamāṇaṃ idha paṇḍitānaṃ.

Therefore, what is stated in the commentaries—setting aside any scribal error—is entirely the standard here for the wise who have reverence for the three trainings.

Tato ca bhāsantarameva hitvā,

Vitthāramaggañca samāsayitvā…pe…

Yasmā ayaṃ hessati vaṇṇanāpi,

Sakkacca tasmā anusikkhitabbā”ti pārā. aṭṭha. 1.ganthārambhakathā.

And then, having abandoned the other language, and having condensed the extensive sequence of words… …since this new explanation will also come to be, therefore it should be carefully studied.”

Teneva ācariyo bhagavato dhammavinayaṃ vā porāṇaṭṭhakathaṃ vā anissāya attano ñāṇena takketvā vā attanā paricitalokiyaganthehi gahetvā vā na kañci ganthaṃ akāsi. Yadi pana tādisaṃ kareyya, [M 0.0062] taṃ theravādino mahāpadesasutte dī. ni. 2.188; a. ni. 4.180 vuttanayena “addhā idaṃ na ceva tassa bhagavato vacanaṃ, buddhaghosassa ca therassa duggahita”nti chaḍḍeyyuṃyeva. Yato ca kho ayaṃ visuddhimaggo porāṇaṭṭhakathānaṃ bhāsāparivattanādivaseneva ācariyena kato, tatoyeva theravādino taṃ mahāpadesasutte vuttanayena “addhā idaṃ tassa bhagavato vacanaṃ, ācariyabuddhaghosassa ca therassa suggahita”nti sampaṭicchanti. Tenāpāyaṃ sakalaloke patthaṭo hoti.

For that very reason, the Ācariya did not compose any work by speculating with his own knowledge or by drawing from worldly texts familiar to him, independent of the Blessed One’s Dhamma and Vinaya or the ancient commentaries. If he were to do such a thing, the Theravādins, following the method stated in the Mahāpadesa Sutta, would surely reject it, saying: “Surely, this is not the word of the Blessed One, and it has been badly grasped by the elder Buddhaghosa.” But since this Visuddhimagga was made by the Ācariya simply by way of changing the language of the ancient commentaries and so on, the Theravādins accept it, following the method stated in the Mahāpadesa Sutta, saying: “Surely, this is the word of the Blessed One, and it has been well grasped by the elder, Ācariya Buddhaghosa.” For this reason, it has spread throughout the entire world.

Sīladhutaṅgādīnaṃ vibhāgo ca paṭipattinayaparidīpanañca pākaṭameva. Tathāyaṃ visuddhimaggo suviññeyyapadavākyehi ceva anākulapadavākyehi ca tantinayānurūpāya pāḷigatiyā suṭṭhu saṅkhato, tatoyeva cassa atthopi suviññeyyo hoti. Tasmā taṃ olokentā viññuno visuddhajjhāsayā khaṇe khaṇe atthapaṭisaṃvedino ceva dhammapaṭisaṃvedino ca hutvā anappakaṃ pītisomanassaṃ paṭisaṃvedenti.

The analysis of virtue, ascetic practices, and so on, and the complete elucidation of the method of practice are quite manifest. Likewise, this Visuddhimagga is well-composed with easily intelligible and unconfused words and sentences, with a Pāli style that accords with the textual method; for that very reason its meaning is also easily understood. Therefore, when intelligent people of pure intention examine it, becoming at every moment experiencers of the meaning and the Dhamma, they experience no little joy and mental gladness.

Anekāni cettha pasādāvahāni mahātissattheravatthuādīni visuddhi. 1.15 sīhaḷavatthūni ca dhammasenāpatisāriputtattheravatthuādīni visuddhi. 1.19 jambudīpavatthūni ca dīpitāni. Tāni passitvā anussarantānaṃ sappurisānaṃ balavapasādo ca uppajjati, “kadā nu kho mayampi īdisā bhavissāmā”ti diṭṭhānugatiṃ āpajjitukāmatā ca uppajjati.

Here, too, many inspiring stories are illuminated, such as the Sinhalese stories of the great elder Tissa and others, and the stories from Jambudīpa of the General of the Dhamma, the Elder Sāriputta, and others. When good persons see and recollect them, strong confidence arises in them, and the desire to follow their example arises, thinking: “When, indeed, will we too become like this?”

Evaṃ parisuddhapiṭakapāḷinissayatādīhi anekasatehi guṇehi ayaṃ visuddhimaggo sakalaloke patthaṭo jātoti veditabbo. Yathā cāyaṃ visuddhimaggo, evaṃ aññāpi ācariyena katā tipiṭakasaṅgahaṭṭhakathāyo porāṇaṭṭhakathānaṃ bhāsāparivattanabhāvādīhi guṇehi sakalaloke patthaṭāyeva honti.

Thus it should be understood that this Visuddhimagga has spread throughout the entire world because of its many hundreds of virtues, such as its reliance on the pure Pāli of the Piṭaka. And just as this Visuddhimagga has spread, so too the other commentaries on the Tipiṭaka collection composed by the Ācariya have spread throughout the entire world because of their virtues, such as being translations of the ancient commentaries.

Ettāvatā ca pana kimatthaṃ katotiādīnampi pañhānamattho vitthārena vibhāvitova hotīti.
