And by this much, the meaning of the questions beginning with “For what purpose was it made?” has been explained in detail.

Tatthetaṃ vuccati –

Herein, this is said:

1.

1.

Sambhāvanīyassa [M 0.0063] sudhīvarāna-

Mādattadhīriṭṭhapadassa yassa;

Paññādijātā lalitā guṇābhā,

Bhāteva lokamhi sataṃ mudāya.

He who is to be honored by the very wise, who has attained the position of an eminent and distinguished sage; the lovely splendor of his virtues, born of wisdom and so on, shines in the world for the joy of the good, like the sun.

2.

2.

Sa buddhaghosāvhathiraggadhīmā,

Vidūna’maccantasamādarā’dā;

Sabhāvajaṃ byattisasattiladdhaṃ,

Siriṃ dadhāteva subuddhaghoso.

That elder called Buddhaghosa, of supreme intelligence, receiving the utmost respect of the wise, maintains a glory that is natural and obtained through his manifest skill, that well-known Buddhaghosa.

3.

3.

“Sambuddhaseṭṭhe parinibbutasmiṃ,

Saṃvaccharānaṃ dasame satamhi;

Jāto”ti ñāto vibudhehi buddha-

Ghosaṅkuro pattasamattamānī.

“When the best of Buddhas had passed into Parinibbāna, in the tenth century of years, the sprout Buddhaghosa, who had attained the esteem of all, was known by the wise to be born.”

4.

4.

Viññū vidū’massa pumaggajāte,

Sañjātataṃ dakkhiṇadesabhāge;

Ramme’ndiyasmiṃ sujanākarasmiṃ,

Tattatthamesīna’mayaṃ patīti.

The wise know of his birth as an excellent man in the southern region, in delightful India, a mine of good people; this is the conviction of those who seek the true facts.

5.

5.

Moraṇḍagāmamhi sa tattha jāto,

Puññānito vippakulamhi sammā;

Sūrassa lokatthasamāvahatthaṃ,

Uppajjanāyā’dyaruṇova raṃsi.

He was born there in the village of Moraṇḍa, led rightly by his merit into a brahmin family; like the dawn light appearing for the sun to bring about the welfare of the world.

6.

6.

Saṃvaddhabuddhī sa pavuddhipatto,

Ārādhayaṃ ñātigaṇaṃ sadeva;

Vedesu vijjāsu tadaññasippa-

Ganthesvanāyāsapavīṇatā’gā.

With developed intelligence, having reached maturity, pleasing his circle of relatives at all times, he attained proficiency without difficulty in the Vedas, the sciences, and the texts of other arts.

7.

7.

Suddhādhimuttīna vivecanena,

Sārānu’sāroti viviñcamāno;

Vedesva’sāratta’mabujjhi yasmā,

Tuṭṭhiṃ sa nāpajji sutena sena.

Through the discernment of those with pure resolve, distinguishing essence from non-essence, because he understood the essencelessness of the Vedas, he found no satisfaction in his own learning.

8.

8.

Anvesato [M 0.0064] tassa pasatthasāraṃ,

Saddhammasāro savanena laddho;

Ninnova buddhassa sa sāsanamhi,

Ussāhajāto’pagamāya tattha.

While he was seeking the praised essence, he obtained the essence of the True Dhamma through hearing; inclining towards the Dispensation of the Buddha, he was filled with zeal to enter it.

9.

9.

Dhammābhilāsī sa viroci tattha,

Saṃladdhapabbajjupasampadova;

There’pasaṅkamma visuddhathera-

Vādīnikāyamhi patītapaññe.

Desirous of the Dhamma, he shone there, having obtained the going-forth and higher ordination; approaching the elders of renowned wisdom in the school of the pure Theravāda.

10.

10.

Tadā hi’suṃ dakkhiṇaindiyamhi,

Nivāsino theriyavaṃsajātā;

Tadaññavādī ca munī muninda-

Mataṃ yathāladdhi pakāsayantā.

For at that time in Southern India dwelt those born in the lineage of the Elders, and also other sages holding different views, proclaiming the doctrine of the Lord of Sages according to their own tenets.

11.

11.

Saddhammasārādhigamāya bhiyyo,

Pāḷiṃ samuggaṇhi jineritaṃ, sā;

Jivhaggalīlā manasā’sitā’ssa,

Lakkhīva puññe nivasaṃ babhāsa.

For the further attainment of the essence of the True Dhamma, he thoroughly learned the Pāli spoken by the Conqueror; residing in his mind, it played on the tip of his tongue and shone like Fortune dwelling in a man of merit.

12.

12.

Evaṃ tamuggaṇha’mabodhi sammā,

“Ekāyanoyaṃ suvisuddhiyāti;

Maggo vivaṭṭādhigamāya” tattho-

Yyogaṃ samāpajji paraṃ parattī.

Having thus learned it, he understood rightly: “This is the sole path for perfect purification, the way for the attainment of Nibbāna.” Seeking the supreme goal, he undertook the supreme application to it.

13.

13.

Sabhāvapaññā mahatī ca sattha-

Ntaropaladdhā vipulāva vijjā;

Tenassa buddhottisamuddatiṇṇe,

Akicchasādhittapabhāva’maññā.

His natural wisdom was great, and vast was the knowledge he had obtained from other treatises; thus they knew his ability to achieve without difficulty the crossing of the ocean of the Buddha's words.

14.

14.

Buddhassa kittīva sukittighoso,

Vattissate’ccassa garū viyattā;

Atthānvitaṃ nāmamakaṃsu buddha-

Ghosoti sambuddhamataṅgatassa.

His learned preceptors said of him: “His voice will be as renowned as the Buddha’s fame”; they gave the meaningful name “Buddhaghosa” to him who had accepted the Enlightened One’s view.

15.

15.

Mayūradūtavhayapaṭṭanasmiṃ,

Nivassa [M 0.0065] kañjīvhapurādike ca;

Sa andhakākhyātasadesiyaṭṭha-

Kathaṃ samuggaṇhi samāhitatto.

Residing in the port called Mayūradūta, and in Kañcīpura and other places, with a concentrated mind he thoroughly learned the commentary of his own country, known as the Andhaka.

16.

16.

Tāvattakenassa sumedhasassā-

Santuṭṭhacittassa tatuttarimpi;

Sambuddhavāṇīsu samattamatthaṃ,

Aññātumicchā mahatī ajāyi.

Although his wise mind was unsatisfied with just that much, a great desire arose in him to know the complete meaning even further in the words of the Fully Enlightened One.

17.

17.

Mahāmahindādivasīvarebhi,

Samābhatā yāṭṭhakathā sasārā;

Satheravādā suvinicchayā ca,

Tadā vibhātā vata laṅkayā’suṃ.

The essential commentaries that were brought by the great seers, Mahinda and others, together with the Theravāda and its correct decisions, indeed shone forth in Laṅkā at that time.

18.

18.

Pavattimetaṃ vidiya’ssa meta-

Dahosi “yaṃ nūna’bhirāmalaṅkaṃ;

Alaṅkarontiṃ ratanākaraṃva,

Upecca sikkhe’ṭṭhakathā mahantī.

Knowing this situation, this thought occurred to him: “Let me go to delightful Laṅkā and learn the Great Commentary, which adorns it like a mine of jewels.

19.

19.

Tā bhāsayā sīhaḷikāya raccā,

Tantiṃ samāropya navaṃ kareyyaṃ;

Evañhi desantariyāna buddha-

Mānīnamatthaṃ khalu sādhaye”ti.

Those commentaries composed in the Sinhalese language, I shall render into the canonical language and make them new; thus I will surely accomplish the purpose of those in other lands who honor the Buddha.”

20.

20.

Pure ca laṅkāgatasāsanaṃ yaṃ,

Sunimmalindūva himādimutto;

Pabhāsi, kismiñci tadāññavāda-

Manākulaṃ tā’kulataṃ jagāma.

In the past, the Dispensation that came to Laṅkā shone like the spotless moon freed from frost; though previously unagitated by any other doctrine, it later fell into confusion in some groups.

21.

21.

Jinamhi nibbānagate hi vassa-

Satantare sāsanikā samaggā;

Samānavādā jinasāsanamhi,

Na koci bhedopi tadā ahosi.

For within a century after the Conqueror went to Nibbāna, the followers of the Dispensation were in harmony; they held the same view in the Conqueror’s Dispensation, and at that time there was no division at all.

22.

22.

Pacchā [M 0.0066] ca saddhammadumāhatebhya-

Dhammehi vātehi paṭicca pāpe;

Jātehi saṃviggamanā samāya,

There’sa’muyyogamakaṃsu daḷhaṃ.

But afterwards, because of the evil winds of unrighteous doctrines that had arisen and struck the tree of the Good Dhamma, the elders, with anxious minds, made a firm effort for the sake of stilling them.

23.

23.

Saṅgītiyo kacca supesalehi,

Niggayhamānāpi thirehi daḷhaṃ;

Chinnāpi rukkhā’ssu punoruhāvā-

Kāsuṃva dhammaṃ vinayā’ññathā te.

Though firmly suppressed by the virtuous and steadfast elders at the councils, like trees that sprout up again even after being cut, those dissenters distorted the Dhamma and Vinaya.

24.

24.

Nānāgaṇā te ca anekavādā,

Saṃsaggakārā jinasāsane’suṃ;

Vādebhi aññehi jineritebhya-

Suddhāyamānā vinayañca dhammaṃ.

Various groups, holding diverse views, were associated with the Victor’s Dispensation; with doctrines other than those proclaimed by the Conqueror, they rendered the Vinaya and Dhamma impure.

25.

25.

Vādā ca vādī piṭakāni tesaṃ,

Laṅkaṃ malaṅkaṃva karaṃ’payātā;

Paṭiggahesuṃ pyabhayādivāsī,

Nāññe mahākhyātavihāravāsī.

The doctrines, the doctrinaires, and their Piṭakas arrived, making Laṅkā as if stained; the residents of Abhayagiri and others received them, but not the dwellers of the Great Monastery.

26.

26.

Yathā ca buddhābhihitāva pāḷi,

Tadatthasārā ca vasībhi ñātā;

Na “tedha vokkamma visuddhathera-

Vādī vivādī”ti pavatti kāci.

As the Pāḷi was spoken by the Buddha and its essential meaning was known by the masters, no report exists that the pure Theravādins, departing from that, had any disputes.

27.

27.

Jīvaṃva rakkhiṃsu satheravādaṃ,

Tantiṃ tadatthañca saniṇṇayaṃ te;

Tasmā na sakkāva tadaññavādi-

Vādebhi hantuṃ cu’pagantumaddhā.

They guarded the Theravāda as if it were life itself—its tradition, its meaning, and its decisions; therefore, it is assuredly impossible for those of other doctrines to harm or overcome it with their views.

28.

28.

Taṃvādasaṃbhedabhayañca maññayā,

“Duddhāravelāpi bhayehi tantinaṃ;

Sammohatādīhi bhave”ti potthakaṃ,

Āropya sammā paripālayiṃsu te.

And fearing the danger of the breaking of that doctrine, thinking, "Even in times of difficulty, dangers to the tradition may arise from delusion and the like," they inscribed it in books and carefully preserved it.

29.

29.

Tadā [M 0.0067] hi tesaṃ paṭibāhane raṇa-

Vidaṃva sikkhaṃ jinasāsanaddharo;

Sa buddhaghoso muni buddhipāṭavo,

Gato’si dīpaṃ varatambapaṇṇikaṃ.

Then, to ward them off, that sage Buddhaghosa, bearer of the Victor's teaching, skilled in battle and with keen intellect, went to the excellent island of Tambapaṇṇi.

30.

30.

Laṅkaṃ upecca sa mahāṭṭhakathāṇṇavassa,

Pāraṃ paraṃ vitaraṇe thiraniṇṇayova;

Saṃsuddhavaṃsajanivāsamahāvihāra-

Māgā’mbaraṃva udayindu’pasobhayanto.

Having reached Laṅkā, with firm resolve to cross to the other shore of that great ocean of commentaries, he approached the Mahāvihāra, the dwelling place of people of pure lineage, adorning it like the rising moon adorns the sky.

31.

31.

Tasmiñca dakkhiṇadisāya vasī sa tattha,

Sobhaṃ padhānagharasaññitapāriveṇaṃ;

Pāsāda’muttama’makā sujanebhi sebyaṃ,

Santo mahānigamasāmi suciṇṇadhammo.

He dwelt there in the southern part of the monastery in the splendid building known as Padhānaghara, a supreme edifice frequented by good people, which a peaceful master of the great town, of well-practiced Dhamma, had built.

32.

32.

Sammā ca yogamakarī budhabuddhamitta-

Therādi’manta’mupayāta’manūnatante;

Saṃsevito vividhañāyapabuddhiyā so,

Suttābhidhammavinayaṭṭhakathāsva’nūnaṃ.

He rightly applied himself, attending upon the wise Elder Buddhamitta and others who had reached the end of the complete scriptures; he was enlightened by various methods of reasoning in the Sutta, Abhidhamma, and Vinaya commentaries, without deficiency.

33.

33.

Veyyattiyaṃ’sa samaye samayantare ca,

Paññāya disva vivaṭaṃva nihītamatthaṃ;

Therā samaggajinamaggamatā’matāsī,

Maññiṃsu naggharatanaṃva sudullabhanti.

Seeing his proficiency in his own doctrine and in other doctrines, and how by his wisdom the hidden meaning was as if revealed, the elders—who knew the Conqueror's path and sought the Deathless—regarded him as a very rare, priceless jewel.

34.

34.

Viññāya dhammavinayatthayathicchadāne,

Cintāmaṇīti sunirūpitabuddhirūpaṃ;

Yasse’ttha nicchitamano kavisaṅghapāla-

Ttheruttamo janahitāya niyojayī taṃ.

Recognizing him—who gave the meaning of the Dhamma and Vinaya as desired, and whose well-established wisdom was like a wish-fulfilling jewel—the supreme Elder and poet Saṅghapāla, with a resolved mind, urged him for the welfare of the people.

35.

35.

“Kiñcāpi santi vividhā paṭipattiganthā,

Kesañci kiñci tu na buddhamatānusāraṃ;

Saṃsuddhatherasamayehi ca te viruddhā,

Tasmā karotu vimalaṃ paṭipattiganthaṃ”.

“Although diverse treatises on practice exist, some points in them are not in accordance with the Buddha's view and are even opposed to the traditions of the pure elders; therefore, please compose a flawless treatise on practice.”

36.

36.

Mettādayambudavanaṃ [M 0.0068] janabhūmiyaṃ’sa,

Saṃvassate ca’riyamaggagamagga’maggaṃ;

Saṃsodhanattha’miti “patthitatheraāsaṃ,

Pūressa’meta”miti kāsi visuddhimaggaṃ.

Being a raincloud of compassion over the human world, he rained down the foremost path for reaching the Noble Path; thinking, “I shall fulfill this aspiration of the elder,” he composed the Visuddhimagga.

37.

37.

Vīrānukampasatiyojitabuddhimā saṃ,

Oggayha, gayha ca’ khilaṭṭhakathā satantī;

Sāraṃ sakheda’manapekkhiya sādhukaṃ sa,

Yaṃ’kāsi, kaṃ nu’dha na rocayate budhaṃ so.

Endowed with wisdom joined with energy, compassion, and mindfulness, having delved into all the commentaries and texts and extracted their essence regardless of his own fatigue—what he composed, which wise man here would it not please?

38.

38.

Vutte’ttha bhāvaparamāva sabhāvadhammā,

Vatthū ca pītisukhavedaniyā’nitāva;

Puṇṇova sabbapaṭipattinayehi ceso,

Pupphābhiphullapavanaṃva virājate’yaṃ.

Herein are taught the ultimate realities in their true nature, and illustrative stories to be experienced with joy and bliss are included; being full of all modes of practice, this work shines like a grove in full bloom.

39.

39.

Yaṃ passiyāna parikappiya ratnasāra-

Gabbhaṃ visuddhi’mabhiyātu’mapekkhamānā;

Taṃ sāra’mādiyitu’māsu payuttayuttā,

Disvā hi naggharatanaṃ nanu vajjaye na.

Seeing it and conceiving it as a chamber filled with essential jewels, those hoping to attain purity quickly and rightly apply themselves to seize that essence. For having seen a priceless jewel, would one not take it?

40.

40.

Kantā padāvali’ha tantinayānusārā,

Sārātisāranayapanti pasiddhasiddhā;

Atthā ca santinugamāya tulāyamāno-

Yyogena mettha hi vinā paṭipatti kā’ññā.

Here the sequence of words is lovely, following the method of the scriptural tradition; a series of the most essential methods, perfectly established. And the meanings act like a balance for determining the path to peace—for indeed, without exertion here, what other practice is there?

41.

41.

Ābhāti satthu caturāgamamajjhago’yaṃ-

Atthe pakāsayiha bhāṇuva nekadabbe;

Medhāvipītijananaṃ’sa vidhāna’metaṃ-

Tītañhi yāva kavigocara’massa ñāṇaṃ.

This work shines, placed in the midst of the Teacher’s four Agamas, illuminating the meanings here like the sun among many objects. This composition of his generates joy for the wise; indeed, his knowledge has surpassed the range of poets.

42.

42.

Diṭṭhāva tikkhamati’massa visuddhimagga-

Sampādanena samupāttasudhīpadebhi;

Tenassa buddhavacanatthavibhāvanāya,

Pabyattasatti viditā viditāgamehi.

His sharp intellect is clearly seen through the completion of the Visuddhimagga by those who have attained the status of wise ones. Therefore, his manifest ability in explaining the meaning of the Buddha's word is known by those who have mastered the scriptures.

43.

43.

Khyātaṃ [M 0.0069] kavībhi’dhigataṃ yasa’māvahena,

Therassa suddhamatibuddhasirīvhayassa;

Lokattha’māvikatapatthana’mādiyāna,

Sāmañca ninnahadayena janāna’matthe.

Having accepted the clearly expressed request of the Elder named Buddhasiri of pure intellect—which brings widespread fame attained by poets—for the benefit of the world, he himself, with a heart inclined towards the welfare of beings...

44.

44.

Sambuddhabhāvaviditeni’minā samanta-

Pāsādikāvhavinayaṭṭhakathā paṇītā;

Sūro’dite viya tayā vinayatthamūḷhā-

Mūḷhī bhavanti jinanītipathā’dhigantvā.

...composed this Vinaya commentary named Samantapāsādikā. By it, those confused about the meaning of the Vinaya become unconfused, having understood the path of the Victor's guidance, just as when the sun has risen.

45.

45.

Laṅkā alaṅkatikatāva mahāmahinda-

Ttherena yā ca vinayaṭṭhakathā’bhatā, taṃ;

Kantāya sīhaḷagirāya girāyamānā,

Accantakantabahulā munayo purā’suṃ.

Lanka was indeed adorned by the Great Elder Mahinda; and that Vinaya commentary which was brought and recited in the delightful Sinhalese language—in the past, the sages were exceedingly delighted with it.

46.

46.

Aññā ca paccari-kurundisamaññitādī,

Dīpaṃ padīpakaraṇī vinayamhi yā’suṃ;

Saṅgayha tāsa’makhilatthanaye ca thera-

Vāde ca muttaratanāniva mekasutte.

And others known as Paccari, Kurundī, and so on, which were like lamps for the Island in the Vinaya; having gathered all their various methods of meaning and the Elder-traditions, like excellent jewels on a single thread...

47.

47.

Tāheva sīhaḷaniruttiyutañca tantiṃ,

Āropiyāna ruciraṃ atha vitthatañca;

Maggaṃ samāsanavasena yathā samatta-

Lokena yā garukatā katamānanā’kā.

...and having translated that tradition, which was in the Sinhalese language, into the pleasing scripture, and having condensed the extensive path so that it would be honored and revered by the entire world, he composed it.

48.

48.

Suddhanvayāgathavirā ca visuddhathera-

Vādī visuddhavinayāgamapujjadhammā;

Suddhaṃ kariṃsu na yathe’nti tadaññavādā,

Iccādi’māvikariyā’si nidānamettha.

Elders of pure lineage, holders of the pure Theravada, for whom the pure Vinaya and Agamas were revered—they kept the teaching pure so that other doctrines could not enter; thus was this manifestation of the introduction here.

49.

49.

Yasmiṃ manuññapadapanti subhā subodhā,

Atthā ca pītisama’vimhayatādibhāvī;

Citrā vicitramatijā kavicittahaṃsā,

Tasmā rasāyati tadatthanusārinaṃ yaṃ.

In which the lovely sequence of words is beautiful and easily understood, and the meanings bring about states like joy, tranquility, and wonder; varied, born of a diverse intellect, like swans for the minds of poets; therefore, it provides a taste of delight to those who follow its meaning.

50.

50.

Accantasāgaranibhā [M 0.0070] vividhā nayatthā,

Sante’ttha yā’su vinayaṭṭhakathā purāṇā;

Tāsaṃ yathābhimatapanti sutantikattaṃ,

Kiñhi’ssa kiñci balavīra’paṭicca kātuṃ.

The ancient Vinaya commentaries were like the boundless ocean, with various methods and meanings. To make their sequence conform to the desired clean and smooth scriptural style, what indeed could be done by relying on just a little strength and effort?

51.

51.

Uyyoga’massa karuṇāpahitaṃ paṭicca,

Paññāsahāyasahitaṃ balavañca daḷhaṃ;

Laddhāva yā nikhilalokamanuññabhūtā,

Medhāvinaṃ’nusabhagāva virājate sā.

Relying on his great and firm effort, prompted by compassion and accompanied by the help of wisdom, that commentary which is pleasing to the whole world was produced. It shines among the wise, like a constant companion at an assembly.

52.

52.

Viññūbhi yā “vinayasāgarapāratiṇṇe”,

Sambhāvitā “sutaraṇāyati sīghavāhā”;

Iccābhimānitaguṇā’jja rarāja yāva,

Kiṃ yaṃ thiraṃ lahu vinassati duppasayhaṃ.

That which is esteemed by the wise as 'the means to cross to the far shore of the Vinaya ocean' and as 'a swift-moving vessel for easy crossing'—with such honored qualities, it has shone until today. How can that which is firm and difficult to overcome perish quickly?

53.

53.

“Yā byāpinī’khilanayassa subodhinī ca,

Sotūbhi sevitasadātanadhammaraṅgaṃ;

Katvāna lokapahite saguṇe dadhantī,

Ṭhātū”ti naṭṭha’mupagā’ṭṭhakathā purāṇā.

Thinking, 'Let it remain—pervasive of all methods, easy to understand, a stage for the eternal Dhamma constantly resorted to by hearers, and bearing its own qualities for the benefit of the world,' the ancient commentaries went to destruction.

54.

54.

Janābhisattāya dayāya codito,

Vichejja khedaṃ vinayamhi sādhunaṃ;

Athāgamāna’ṭṭhakathāvidhānane,

Dhuraṃ dadhātuṃ’bhimukhā’si so sudhī.

Urged by compassion for the people, having removed the weariness of the virtuous in understanding the Vinaya, that wise one then set his face toward the task of arranging the Agamas and their commentaries.

55.

55.

Padmaṃva phullābhinataṃ subhāṇubhaṃ,

Laddhāna phullaṃ’tisayā’si cetanā;

Dāṭhādināgena thiraggadhīmatā,

Yā patthitā’rabbha tadatthasijjhane.

Just as a lotus about to bloom, having received the beautiful light of the sun, blooms fully; so his intention was exceedingly great regarding the fulfillment of the purpose requested by Dāṭhānāga of firm and lofty intellect.

56.

56.

Dīghāgamatthesu sabuddhivikkama-

Māgamma sārādhigamā sumaṅgala-

Nāmānugantāva vilāsinīti yā,

Saṃvaṇṇanā lokahitāya sambhavī.

Having attained the essence in the meanings of the Dīgha Agama through the power of his own intellect, this commentary named Vilāsinī, which follows the name Sumaṅgala, arose for the welfare of the world.

57.

57.

Gambhīramedhāvisayāgamamhipi,

[M 0.0071] Ārabbha buddhiṃ’sa sunimmalīkatā;

Viññātabuddhābhimatā bahū janā,

Aññatthasādhā mahatañhi buddhiyo.

Relying on his purified intellect, even in the Agama which is the domain of profound wisdom, many people understood the Buddha’s intent; for indeed, the intellects of the great accomplish the benefit of others.

58.

58.

Sā’nītavidvākkhimanā manāyitā,

Kantāgame dhammasabhāyate sadā;

Teneva maññe’ha tirokatā tayā,

Kiṃ sīghaga’ññatra pathaññagāmikā.

Attracting the eyes and minds of the wise, and cherished, it always acts as a Dhamma assembly in the delightful Agama. Therefore, I think, other commentaries were obscured by it. Why would those who can go swiftly on a good path go by another difficult one?

59.

59.

Patvā mahantā’mbara’mambudo yathā,

Lokatthasādhīpi mahāsayaṃ mati;

Tasmā’ssa siddhā’ṭṭhakathāparamparā,

Buddhippadānāya’ huvuṃ navā navā.

Just as a cloud, having reached the vast sky, accomplishes the welfare of the world, so too an intellect, having reached one of great aspiration, accomplishes the world's welfare. Therefore, his succession of commentaries, ever new, were completed to bestow understanding.

60.

60.

Buddhādimittaṃ thiraseṭṭha’muddisaṃ,

Saṃvaṇṇanā cāsi papañcasūdanī;

“Sabbatthasāre jinamajjhimāgame,

Laddhāna pītiṃ sujanā samentu”ti.

Referring to the excellent Elder named Buddhamitta, the commentary Papañcasūdanī arose, with the thought: 'Having obtained joy in the essence of all meanings in the Victor's Majjhima Agama, may good people be fully satisfied.'

61.

61.

Uppajji “sāratthapakāsinī”ti yā,

Sā jotipālassa yathābhilāsitaṃ;

Lokaṃ yathānāmikasāradīpanā,

Bhātā’si sammāpaṭipannapanthadā.

The commentary which arose as 'Sāratthapakāsinī' was in accordance with Jotipāla's desire. Illuminating the essence for the world as its name suggests, it shone, providing the path for those practicing rightly.

62.

62.

Sampūri kātuṃ’sa manoratho yayā,

Aṅguttarantāgamamatthavaṇṇanā;

Tannāmadheyyaṃ sujanañca jīvakaṃ,

So jotipālañca pasatthadhītimaṃ.

By which his desire to compose the commentaries on the meanings of the Agamas ending with the Aṅguttara was fulfilled—that which he composed referring to the good person Jīvaka and the wise Jotipāla, and which showed the excellent path to be followed by the Buddhas and others, is called by that name (Manorathapūraṇī). It was like a flower-grove of the True Dhamma, always a resort for the assembly of the wise.

63.

63.

Uddissa yaṃ’kāsi pavīṇataṃ karaṃ,

Buddhādisaṃsebyasumaggadassane;

Saddhammapupphāna’ vanāyitā’si sā,

Vidvālisaṅghassa sadāvagāhaṇā.

That commentary which he made—dedicated to Jīvaka and Jotipāla—producing proficiency in showing the excellent path to be resorted to by the Buddhas and others, was like a grove of flowers of the true Dhamma, a place of constant resort for the swarm of bees that are the wise.

64.

64.

Yena’ttaladdhiṃ [M 0.0072] pajahantu sādhavo,

Dubbodhadhamme ca sabhāvadīpane;

Bujjhantu, iccāsi’bhidhammasāgaro,

Tatthā’vatāraṃ sukarena sādhinī.

Thinking, 'By this may the virtuous abandon the view of self and understand the deep Dhamma that reveals the true nature,' thus arose the ocean of Abhidhamma. To make entry therein easy, there arose the Aṭṭhasālinī and the lovely Sammohavinodanī, born of the play of his wisdom; they were produced referring to a monk with a name equal to his own, 'Buddhaghosa', and they accomplish the welfare of good people.

65.

65.

Medhāvilāsā’ssa’huvu’ṭṭhasālinī,

Kantā ca sammohavinodanīti yā;

Tā buddhaghosoti satulyanāmika-

Māgamma jātā sujanatthasādhinī.

The Atthasālinī and the lovely Sammohavinodanī arose from the display of his wisdom; those two, accomplishing the benefit of the virtuous, were produced through the request of one with a name equal to his own, 'Buddhaghosa'.

66.

66.

Aññā ca pañcaṭṭhakathā’bhidhammaje,

Bhāve nidhāye’ttha yathā’ssu suttarā;

Gambhīramatthesu paviddhabuddhitaṃ,

Sampādanī satthu’tulattadīpanī.

And other five commentaries, which include the meanings produced in the Abhidhamma so that they might be easily crossed; they bring about an intellect that penetrates deep meanings and illuminate the Teacher's peerlessness.

67.

67.

Soṇāvhatherassa paṭicca yācanaṃ,

Tā yāya kaṅkhā vitaranti bhikkhavo;

Yā pātimokkhamhi, tadanvayāvhayaṃ,

Saṃvaṇṇanaṃ’kāsi sa dhīmataṃ varo.

At the request of the Elder named Soṇa, that best of the wise composed the commentary on the Pātimokkha, which is named in accordance with that purpose (Kaṅkhāvitaranī), and by which bhikkhus overcome their doubts.

68.

68.

Samattalokaṭṭhavibhāvirañjanā,

Kate’minā dhammapadassa vaṇṇanā;

Thiraṃ samuddissa kumārakassapaṃ,

Sataṃ manaṃ pītipaphullitaṃ yayā.

By him, the commentary on the Dhammapada was composed, which illuminates and delights in the welfare of the whole world. Referring to the firm Kumārakassapa, it makes the minds of the virtuous blossom with joy.

69.

69.

Aññā’ssa yā suttanipāta-khuddaka-

Pāṭhatthadātā paramatthajotikā;

Saṃvaṇṇanā jātakatanti maṇḍanā,

Tā honti lokassa hitappadīpinī.

His other commentaries—the Paramatthajotikā, which gives the meaning of the Suttanipāta and the Khuddakapāṭha, and the commentary that adorns the Jātaka scripture—these are lamps that illuminate the welfare of the world.

70.

70.

Nissesalokamhi pacāraṇicchā,

Laṅkāgatāna’ṭṭhakathāna’maddhā;

Yā theravādīna’mapūri buddha-

Ghosaggatherassa pabhāvaladdhā.

The desire of the Theravādins to circulate throughout the entire world the commentaries that had come to Laṅkā was certainly fulfilled, having been achieved through the power of the eminent Elder Buddhaghosa.

71.

71.

Bhaddaṃ’sa [M 0.0073] nāmañca, guṇā manuññā,

Samaggagāmī’nukaronti tesaṃ;

Sasaṅkasūrā hi sadātanā ye,

Lokaṃ pamodañca karaṃ caranti.

His auspicious name and pleasing virtues, spreading everywhere, emulate the sun and moon, which, existing perpetually, wander about bringing joy to the world.

72.

72.

Subuddhaghosassa vibhāvisatti-

Pabyatti’mārabbha thirāsabhassa;

Samaggaloko hi sutheravāde,

Mānaṃ pavaḍḍhesi anaññajātaṃ.

Indeed, on account of the manifestation of the brilliant wisdom and ability of the steadfast and noble Subuddhaghosa, the entire world has developed an unprecedented devotion to the excellent Theravāda.

73.

73.

Buddhoti nāmaṃ bhuvanamhi yāva,

Subuddhaghosassa siyā na kiñhi;

Laddhā hi sādhūbhi mahopakārā,

Mahagghavittāniva taṃsakāsā.

As long as the name 'Buddha' exists in the world, how could the fame of Subuddhaghosa not remain? For great benefits have been obtained from him by the virtuous, like treasures of great value.

74.

74.

Khīyetha vaṇṇo na samuddhaṭopi,

Nanva’ssa nekā hi guṇā anantā;

Ko nu’ddhareyyā’ khilasāgarode,

Tathāpi maññantu sudhī sadā teti.

His praise would not be exhausted even if fully extolled, for his many virtues are indeed endless. Who, indeed, could empty the waters of the entire ocean? Nevertheless, may the wise always think of him thus.

Chaṭṭhasaṅgītibhāranitthārakasaṅghasamitiyā pakāsitāyaṃ

This was published by the Sangha Committee that discharged the burden of the Sixth Council.

Visuddhimagganidānakathā niṭṭhitā.

The Introductory Discourse to the Visuddhimagga is finished.
